 Three-edge-coloring (Tait coloring) cubic graphs on the torus:
 A proof of Grünbaum’s conjecture

 August 24, 2026 

 Abstract 
    
 We prove that every cyclically 4-edge-connected cubic graph that can be embedded in the torus, with the exceptional graph class called “Petersen-like”, is 3-edge-colorable.
This means every (non-trivial) toroidal snark can be obtained from several copies of the Petersen graph using the dot product operation. The first two snarks in this family are the Petersen graph and one of Blanuša snarks; the rest are exposed by Vodopivec in 2008. This proves a strengthening of the well-known, long-standing conjecture of Grünbaum  [ 13 ]  from 1968. 
    
 This implies that a 2-connected cubic (multi)graph that can be embedded in the torus is not 3-edge-colorable if and only if it can be obtained from a dot product of copies of the Petersen graph by replacing its vertices with 2-edge-connected planar cubic (multi)graphs.
Here, replacing a vertex  v v  in a cubic graph  G G  is the operation that takes a 2-connected planar cubic multigraph  H H  and one of its vertices  u u  of degree 3, unifying  G − v G-v  and  H − u H-u  and connecting the neighbors of  v v  in  G − v G-v  with the neighbors of  u u  in  H − u H-u  with a matching. 
    
 This result is a highly nontrivial generalization of the Four Color Theorem, and its proof requires a combination of extensive computer verification and computer-free extension of existing proofs on colorability. 
    
 An important consequence of this result is a very strong version of the Tutte 4-Flow Conjecture for toroidal graphs. We show that a 2-edge connected graph embedded in the torus admits a nowhere-zero 4-flow unless it is Petersen-like (in which case it does not admit nowhere-zero 4-flows). Observe that this is a vast strengthening over the Tutte 4-Flow Conjecture on the torus, which assumes that the graph does not contain the Petersen graph as a minor because almost all toroidal graphs contain the Petersen graph minor, but almost none are Petersen-like. 
    
 As a consequence of our proofs, a quadratic-time algorithm for 3-edge-coloring cubic graphs of genus one is obtained. 
    
 Some of our proofs require extensive computer verification. The necessary source codes, together with the input and output files and the complete set of more than 14000 reducible configurations, are
available on Github 1 1 
           1 
          
          
          
         https://github.com/edge-coloring . Refer to the “README.md” file in each directory for instructions on how to run each program.  which can be considered as an addendum to this paper. Moreover, we provide pseudocodes for all our computer verifications. 
  
 
 
 1  Introduction 

 
 1.1  The Four Color Theorem, snarks, and our main results 

 
 The four-color theorem (4CT) states that every loopless planar graph is 4-colorable.
It was first shown by Appel and Haken  [ 2 ,  3 ]  in 1977, and Robertson et al.  [ 26 ]  gave a simplified version of the proof. 
 
 
 The 4CT has another equivalent formulation by taking planar duality (by Tait in 1880  [ 29 ] ): 
 
 
 Theorem 1.1 . 
 
 Every  2 2 -connected cubic planar graph is  3 3 -edge-colorable. 
 
 
 
 In this paper, we are interested in extending Theorem  1.1  to other surfaces, but before mentioning our result, our motivation is also from the famous Tutte’s  4 4 -Flow Conjecture . 
 
 
 Conjecture 1   (Tutte,  [ 31 ] ) . 
 
 Every  2 2 -connected graph without a Petersen graph minor admits a nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow 2 2 
                   2 
                  
                  
                  
                 For the precise definition of nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow, we refer the reader to the textbook by Diestel  [ 10 ] . . 
 
 
 
 While this conjecture is still open, its special case restricted to cubic graphs was proved in 1997 by Robertson, Seymour, and Thomas  [ 28 ] . The proof of this significant achievement is based on two nontrivial results, one about doublecross graphs  [ 11 ] , and the other one about apex cubic graphs. (The full proof of the latter case was not published.) 
 
 
 For cubic graphs, having a nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow is equivalent to having a 3-edge-coloring.
Motivated by questions about colorings and flows for graphs on surfaces, an ongoing effort is being made to formulate which cubic graphs that can be embedded in a fixed surface are 3-edge-colorable.
This question can be reduced to cyclically 4-edge-connected graphs. 3 3 
               3 
              
              
              
            A graph is  cyclically  k k -edge-connected  if after deleting fewer than  k k  edges from the graph, there is at most one component that contains a cycle. See also Section  2 . 
Cyclically 4-edge-connected cubic graphs with girth of at least 5 that are not 3-edge-colorable are called  snarks .
The Petersen graph was the first snark discovered  [ 25 ] . For a while, only sporadic examples were known, see, e.g.  [ 5 ,  17 ] , but today it is clear that snarks form a very rich family, although their complete description is totally out of reach (unless P = NP).
Snarks are an interesting and important class of graphs because they often appear as counterexamples to various intriguing conjectures. For example, the famous Cycle Double Cover Conjecture (CDCC) claims that every 2-edge-connected graph has a collection of cycles that cover each edge twice. For cubic graphs, this is equivalent to saying that the graph can be embedded in a surface so that all faces of the embedding are simple cycles. When a cubic graph has a 3-edge-coloring, it has a cycle double cover consisting of the cycles formed by each pair of colors. Therefore, snarks are the only possible counterexamples for CDCC among cubic graphs. In fact, if CDCC is true for snarks, it is true for all graphs  [ 18 ] . 
 
 
 The version of Theorem  1.1  has a chance to hold under some additional assumptions. In fact, recently, the following result was obtained in  [ 16 ] . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.2 . 
 
 The only snark embeddable in the projective plane is the Petersen graph.
In other words, a  2 2 -connected cubic graph  G G  that can be embedded in the projective plane is not  3 3 -edge-colorable if and only if  G G  can be obtained from the Petersen graph by replacing each vertex by a  2 2 -connected planar cubic graph. 
 
 
 
 This settles a conjecture by Mohar from 2004 and resolves a problem of Neil Robertson from the 1990s, see  [ 23 ] . 
 
 
 A well-known conjecture by Grünbaum from 1968  [ 13 ]  says the following. 
 
 
 Conjecture 2   (Grünbaum  [ 13 ] , 1968) . 
 
 If  G G  is a  2 2 -connected cubic graph embedded in the torus and  G G  is not  3 3 -edge-colorable, then  G G  contains two edges on the same face of the embedding, whose removal gives a planar graph in which the four endpoints of the removed edges lie on the boundaries of two faces. 
 
 
 
 This conjecture motivated a more general conjecture by Thompson, which says that if  G G  is a dual graph of a triangulation of the  ( d − 1 ) (d-1) -dimensional sphere (or even manifold) and has an even number of vertices, then  G G  is  d d -edge colorable (see Kalai  [ 19 ] ).
By Steinitz’ theorem, the case  d = 3 d=3  is exactly Theorem  1.1 . 
 
 
 Although the main interest of Grünbaum was about the torus, he originally asked a more general question of whether there are any snarks that are duals of triangulations of surfaces.
Today, it is known that the general conjecture does not hold for the surface of genus 5, as proved by Kochol  [ 20 ] .
Vodopivec  [ 32 ]  proved that there are infinitely many snarks that can be embedded in the torus.
They are certain dot products of copies of the Petersen graph; each of them contains two edges whose removal yields a planar graph.
In this paper, we outline another infinite family of toroidal snarks that are also dot products of copies of the Petersen graph. Our results then show that these two families exhaust all toroidal snarks. By classifying all their embeddings of genus 1, we obtain a proof of Grünbaum’s Conjecture  [ 13 ] . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.3 . 
 
 The only snarks embeddable in the torus are the Petersen graph and its dot products, the Blanuša-H k k  ( k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 ) family and the Blanuša-V k k  ( k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 ). 4 4 
                   4 
                  
                  
                  
                 See Section  2  for the definition of these graphs. 
 
 
 
 With this result, we give a complete characterization of non-3-edge-colorable cubic graphs that can be embedded in the torus, see Section  2 .
Grünbaum  [ 13 ]  conjectured that the dual graph of a triangulation of every two-dimensional manifold is always 3-edge-colorable. This is false as mentioned above, but our result implies that the dual graph of a triangulation in the torus is always 3-edge-colorable.
This is also interesting because the research community (see  [ 1 ,  21 ]  for example) is actively working on  Grünbaum colorings , by which we mean a partition of the edges of a triangulation of  G G  of some surface in three parts such that for each face  f f  of  G G , the three boundary edges of  f f  are in distinct parts of the edge-partition. Clearly,  G G  has a Grünbaum coloring if and only if its dual cubic graph has a proper 3-edge-coloring. Hence, our result implies that every triangulation of the torus has a Grünbaum coloring. 
 
 
 Our Theorem  1.3  yields another result about nowhere-zero 4-flows in more general (not necessarily cubic) graphs. It can be considered as a very strong version of the Tutte 4-Flow Conjecture. For that effect, we extend the definition of  Petersen-like  graphs to allow vertices of higher degrees. We start with a cubic graph that is the dot product of copies of the Petersen graph, and then we replace some of its vertices with planar graphs  H H  that need not be cubic; we only request that the vertex  u u  of  H H  that is split to connect with 3 edges to the Petersen graph be of degree 3. 
 
 
 Theorem 1.4 . 
 
 Every cyclically- 4 4 -edge-connected toroidal graph different from the Petersen graph and the Blanuša-Vodopivec snark family admits a nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow. 
 
 
 
 The proof of Theorem  1.4  is given in the appendix (section  G ).
Theorem  1.4  implies the following conjecture of
Neil Robertson from the mid-1990s; see Problem 5.5.19 in  [ 24 ] . 
 
 
 Conjecture 3   (Robertson, 1994) . 
 
 Every  2 2 -edge-connected graph in the torus whose representativity is at least  3 3 , has a nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow. 
 
 
 
 Robertson made a more general conjecture resembling Grünbaum’s Conjecture  2 . 
 
 
 Conjecture 4   (Robertson, 1994) . 
 
 For every surface  S S , there is a constant  r r  such that every  2 2 -edge-connected graph embedded in  S S  with representativity at least  r r  has a nowhere-zero  4 4 -flow. 
 
 
 
 By the result in  [ 16 ] , the value  r = 4 r=4  works for the projective plane, and by Theorem  1.4 ,  r = 3 r=3  works for the torus. 
 
 
 We obtain the following algorithmic result using Theorem  1.3 . 
 
 
 Theorem 1.5 . 
 
 The following algorithmic question can be solved in quadratic time,  O ⁡ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) , where  n = | V ⁡ ( G ) | n=|V(G)| .
 Input : A cubic graph  G G .
 Output : Either a 3-edge-coloring of  G G  or an obstruction showing that  G G  is not 3-edge colorable, or the conclusion that  G G  cannot be embedded in the torus (certified by obtaining a forbidden minor for embedding in the torus that is contained in  G G ). 
 
 
 
 Note that it is NP-complete to decide the 3-edge-colorability of cubic graphs, see  [ 15 ] . 
 
 
 
 1.2  Sketch of the proof: Technical difficulty over the proof for the projective planar case 

 
 The basic outline of our proof of Theorem  1.3  is similar to that for the projective planar case. A description of the proof and its main differences over that of the 4CT is given in  [ 16 ] , and we omit it here. 
 
 
 However, there are essential differences between the toroidal and projective planar case.
We list below some of the main issues in the torus case. 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 It is harder to obtain a set of valid discharging rules and reducible configurations. 
 
 
 The difficulty of finding reducible configurations has a great impact on the overall size of the unavoidable set of reducible configurations, as evidenced by the projective planar case.
(The planar case needs 633 configurations, whereas the projective planar case uses 5706 configurations.)
Although we have no mathematical explanation why the reducibility condition is strictly harder to satisfy for the torus than for the projective plane, we found that many configurations that are reducible for the projective plane are not reducible for the torus, whereas we have no examples for the converse direction. As a result, compared to the projective plane, we have to search further for reducible configurations and discharging rules, thus obtaining an even bigger unavoidable set of 14000+ configurations. 
 
 2.  
 
 The Petersen graph is not the only snark embeddable in the torus. 
 
 
 For the projective plane, the Petersen graph is the only embeddable snark.
The Petersen graph in the projective plane has many nice properties (most notably, the dual graph is the complete graph  K 6 K_{6} ), and these structural properties were used in  [ 16 ]  to prove that some given cubic subgraph can not be part of a snark.
However, for the torus, since there are infinitely many snarks of genus 1, there is much more flexibility in the structure of the snarks (examples are shown in Figures
 3 – 7 ).
This makes it harder to confirm whether a given graph must not be a subgraph of a snark. 
 
 3.  
 
 The properties of non-contractible cycles in the torus are more diverse. 
 
 
 As with the projective plane, the torus also has non-contractible cycles.
For the projective plane, if there is a non-contractible cycle in a graph, one can obtain a planar subgraph by replacing the cycle with some vertices, and some nice properties for the plane could be applied to the remaining subgraph.
However, we cannot do this for the torus because the remaining subgraph would be embedded in an annulus.
The subgraphs embedded in the annulus must be dealt with differently than those in the plane. This distinction is resolved by introducing “annular configurations”). 
 
 4.  
 
 The Euler characteristic of the torus is equal to zero. 
 
 
 The Euler characteristic plays a central role in the discharging method.
Essentially, the sum of the charge of every vertex is proportional to the Euler characteristic and stays the same throughout the discharging process.
For the projective plane, the Euler characteristic is positive, and the proof indicating that the resulting charge of every vertex must be nonpositive is sufficient to derive a contradiction. 
 
 
 On the other hand, for the torus, we need to confirm that a reducible configuration appears even when every vertex has a final charge of exactly zero rather than being strictly positive. This is because it is possible that every vertex ends up with a final charge of zero, no matter how we set up the discharging rules. 
 
 
 
 
 In Section  4 , we discuss the reducibility of configurations.
Roughly speaking, configurations are subgraphs in cubic graphs, and a configuration in  G G  is  reducible  if it can be replaced by a smaller one (by deleting edges and suppressing the resulting vertices of degree 2) without changing the non-3-edge-colorability of  G G .
Since the surface is more complicated, we need more reducible configurations.
Our complete list of reducible configurations consists of approximately 14000 configurations 5 5 
               5 
              
              
              
            They are available in the aforementioned Google drive , which is much more than that for the projective planar case (5706 configurations). 
 
 
 The discharging rules require a major revision as well.
Ultimately, we have constructed a list of 201 discharging rules (vs. the 169 discharging rules for the projective planar case). All the rules are described in Section  B  in the Appendix.
More details are provided in Sections  3  and  4 . 
 
 
 The second issue arises when trying to reduce a configuration whose reducibility is based on having two or more edges deleted 6 6 
               6 
              
              
              
            Following previous work, we adapt “contractions” in the dual graph rather than “deletions” in the primal. See Section  3 . .
The same issue (with the size of contraction size “two” replaced by “three”) appears in the projective planar case, and we basically follow the same strategy as in  [ 16 ] . The main difficulty over the projective planar case is that many more exceptional cases exist for the torus. This forces us to perform more involved computer testing.
We also utilize a lemma proved in Section  6 7 7 
               7 
              
              
              
            The precise statement is the following: if  C C  is a contractible cycle of length 6 or 7 in the triangulation  G ′ G^{\prime}  (the dual graph of the cubic graph  G G ) and the number of vertices strictly contained in the disk  D D  bounded by  C C  is at least 5, we can find another reducible configuration contained in  D ∖ C D\setminus C . , which is similar to the projective planar case, but since there are many more discharging rules, the proof is much more involved. 
 
 
 For some cases that do not pass computer verification, we provide proof for each configuration using additional criteria. There are, however, some differences over the projective planar case. We may have to enumerate contractions, not just a single possible way to reduce, but many ways to reduce, and in the end, at least one contraction should work. Moreover, we observe that  G G  is of order at least 38 by the result of  [ 6 ]  (who generated and examined all snarks of order at most 36). It turns out that only certain snarks (dot products of copies of the Petersen graph) can be embedded in the torus. This means that the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  is of order at least 19. This indeed helps a lot.
The full proof is given in Section  8 . 
 
 
 For the third issue, when dealing with reducibility, we must confirm that the outskirt of the configuration is induced, meaning that there should be no edges connecting two vertices of the configuration.
In the planar case, this can be achieved fairly easily. In our proof (and also in the projective planar case), there is a short-length cycle that may be  non-contractible . The minimum length of such a cycle is called the  representativity  (also called the  face-width ) of the embedded graph. For the projective planar case, the proof handles the cases of representativity up to 5. The proof in  [ 16 ]  uses a combination of discharging methods and reducibility checking, which also involve some ideas that are developed in  [ 11 ] . 
 
 
 On the other hand, for the torus case, the methods for the projective planar case would not work because we have to deal with the annulus (so we have to deal with two disks, instead of just one disk for the planar/projective planar case). Therefore we have to develop completely new ideas.
We first deal with the representativity-1 case, where we have an edge  e ∈ E ⁡ ( G ) e\in E(G)  such that  G − e G-e  is planar 8 8 
               8 
              
              
              
            In this case,  G G  is an apex graph. If we would be able to assume that every apex graph is 3-edge-colorable, this case would not be relevant. . Now, assume that the representativity of the embedding of  G G  is at least two. In this case,  G G  may contain multiple edges, but they would not form a face of size two. So we can still apply our discharging rules to the embedding because it is a 2-cell embedding. 
 
 
 Using our discharging rule, roughly, we show the following: 
 
 
 
 In the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  of a cubic graph  G G  embedded in the torus, if there is a vertex  v v  that ends up with a positive final charge, there is a subgraph  W W  that contains either  v v  or its neighbor, such that reducing  W ′ W^{\prime}  (which corresponds to  W W  in  G ′ G^{\prime} ) from  G G  results in a 3-edge-colorable cubic graph. 
 Here  W W  can be embedded either in a disk or in an annulus. 
 
 
 
 For the proof of 4CT, reducible configurations are required to be induced, however, in our case above, we allow the configuration  W W  to “wrap-around” the torus. Indeed what we do is as follows: as mentioned above, we first obtain approximately 14000 reducible configurations that are “induced” (i.e., none of the configurations is allowed to be “wrap-around”). Then, for each configuration, we enumerate ALL possible ways to “wrap-around” (there are approximately 50 ways for each reducible configuration) and test whether or not they are all reducible (so we test more than half a million configurations). Fortunately, they are all reducible. Therefore, if there is a vertex of positive final charge, we are done. 
 
 
 We now come to the fourth issue. It may happen that there is no vertex with positive final charge. Since the total charge is 0, this would mean that all vertices have final charge equal to 0.
This means that we cannot find a reducible configuration in the conventional way (i.e. a vertex of positive final charge implies the appearance of a reducible configuration).
We have to find a reducible configuration even if all the vertices of  G ′ G^{\prime}  end up with a final charge 0 (which we refer to as the  flat  case).
We need a lot of extra work here.
We show that if the degree of a vertex is at least ten, a vertex of final charge zero implies the appearance of a reducible configuration. Also, we can characterize all cases where all vertices in the third neighborhood 9 9 
               9 
              
              
              
            This neighborhood is called a  cartwheel  in  [ 26 ] .  of a vertex of degree at most nine have final charge zero. Using this, we show that a reducible configuration appears if vertices of degree nine whose final charge is zero are concentrated (see Lemma  9.1 ). We show an example in Figure  1 . In these cases, we may not have reducible configurations, but if two such vertices  v 0 , v 1 v_{0},v_{1}  are adjacent, we can find a reducible configuration in the union of their third neighborhoods. In doing so, we need many new reducible configurations. Using these results, we find reducible configurations even in the flat case. 
 
 
 Figure 1 :  Two configurations around  v 0 , v 1 v_{0},v_{1}  where the final charge is zero. If  v 0 , v 1 v_{0},v_{1}  are adjacent, then their union contains a reducible configuration, shown as the blue-colored subgraph surrounded by a dotted circle. 
 
 
 
 1.3  Some technical results and organization of the paper 

 
 In addition to the discharging method and the reducibility testing, which are detailed in Sections  3  and  4 , respectively, we utilize the following technical results. 
 
 
 
 (a)  
 
 Let  C C  be a contractible cycle of length  l l  in the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime} , where  l ∈ { 6 , 7 } l\in\{6,7\}  and the disk  D D  bounded by  C C  contains at least  l − 2 l-2  vertices. Then there is a reducible configuration that is strictly contained in the interior of  D D . 
 
 
 This is shown in Sections  5  and  6 . 
 
 (b)  
 
 We deal with the “flat” case in Section  9 . 
 
 
 
 
 The conclusion in (a) is the same as that in  [ 16 ] , but the details are different because we have more discharging rules, which makes the proof harder. The importance of (a) is that
if the assumption of (a) is satisfied, we can find another reducible configuration  J ′ J^{\prime}  in the disk  D D  bounded by  C C  in  G G , and we manage to show that by making a reduction using  J ′ J^{\prime} , the resulting graph obtained from  G G  is still 3-edge-colorable. More details will be given in Sections  5  and  6 . This means that if  C C  is a separating cycle of length exactly  l l  for  l = 6 , 7 l=6,7  in  G ′ G^{\prime} , we may assume that the component of  G ′ − C G^{\prime}-C  strictly inside the disk bounded by  C C  has at most  l − 3 l-3  vertices. 
 
 
 We have around 2000 reducible configurations that need contraction of two edges or more.
For each of these configurations, we provide a case-by-case analysis using results in Section  8  to prove that after reduction, the resulting cubic graph of  G G  still becomes 3-edge-colorable. We use computer programs to cover most of the cases.
The algorithm used for the analysis is provided in Appendix  C . 
 
 
 This paper is organized as follows.
In Section  2  we shall introduce the main notation.
The discharging method and the reducibility check are detailed in Sections  3  and  4 , respectively.
As pointed out above, we have to deal with the flat case. This is given in Section  9 .
Finally, in Sections  10  and  11 , we give the proof of Theorem  1.3  and our quadratic-time algorithm for Theorem  1.5 , respectively. 
 
 
 Some of the main conclusions of this work are based on extensive computation. This involves reducibility of configurations and the “unavoidability” (the discharging part). All computations have been programmed and run independently at least twice.
Pseudocodes of programs used in our computer verification are included in Section  C . 
 
 
 
 
 2  A minimal counterexample 

 
 Definition 2.1 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a cubic graph. An edge-cut  F ⊂ E ⁡ ( G ) F\subset E(G)  is a  cyclic cut  if at least two components of  G − F G-F  contain cycles. The graph is  cyclically  k k -edge-connected  if no cyclic cut of size less than  k k  exists. 
 
 
 
 Let  𝒯 1 \mathcal{T}_{1}  be the set of all simple cubic bridgeless graphs embeddable in the torus.
For  G ∈ 𝒯 1 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1} , the replacement of any cyclic 3-edge-cut by a vertex is called a  3-edge-cut reduction .
Similarly, the replacement of any cyclic 2-edge-cut by an edge is called a  2-edge-cut reduction .
These two reductions were introduced in the projective planar theorem  [ 16 ] . In this paper, we introduce a third reduction called the 4-edge-cut reduction. 
 
 
 Definition 2.2 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a cubic graph and let  F F  be an edge-cut of size exactly four.
Let  I 4 I_{4}  be the subcubic graph obtained from the Petersen graph by removing two adjacent vertices, see Figure  2 . 10 10 
                 10 
                
                
                
               This is an  annular island  of ring size 4, which we will define later in Section  3 . 
If one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  is isomorphic to  I 4 I_{4} , let us remove  I 4 I_{4}  and add two edges,  e ^ 1 \hat{e}_{1}  connecting the endpoints of  e 1 , e 1 ′ e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime} , and  e ^ 2 \hat{e}_{2}  connecting the endpoints of  e 2 , e 2 ′ e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime} .
(Note that the underlying surface that  I 4 I_{4}  is embedded is annular, so the two newly added edges can be drawn in the same surface as  G G  without  e ^ 1 \hat{e}_{1}  and  e ^ 2 \hat{e}_{2}  crossing each other.)
This operation is called a  4-edge-cut reduction . 
 
 
 
 Figure 2 :  The graph  I 4 I_{4}  as a subgraph of a toroidal graph, on which we can make a 4-edge-cut reduction. After deleting it,  e 1 , e 1 ′ e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime}  and  e 2 , e 2 ′ e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime}  can be connected in the skeleton of the removed  I 4 I_{4}  without crossing each other. 
 
 
 We refer to these reductions as  low edge-cut reductions .
Since there are exactly two connected components separated by a 2-edge or 3-edge or 4-edge-cut, there are two possible graphs that are obtained by making a low edge-cut reduction. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.3 . 
 
 A cubic graph  G ∈ 𝒯 1 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}  is  3 3 -edge-colorable if and only if any cubic graph obtained from  G G  by zero or more low edge-cut reductions is 3-edge-colorable. 
 
 
 
 The cases for 2-edge-cut and 3-edge-cut reductions are obvious. The 4-edge-cut reduction is just the inverse of making the dot product with the Petersen graph, and it is well known that a dot product of a cubic graph  G 1 G_{1}  with  P 10 P_{10}  is 3-edge-colorable if and only if  G 1 G_{1}  is 3-edge-colorable. 
 
 
 Let  𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0}  be a set of those graphs  G ∈ 𝒯 1 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}  that can be reduced to a graph isomorphic to  P 10 P_{10}  after a sequence of low edge-cut reductions. Graphs in  𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0}  are said to be  Petersen-like graphs .
We show several examples of Petersen-like graphs.
All of the examples are obtained by the inverse operation of a 4-edge-cut reduction, which we call  4-edge-cut expansions .
Note that a graph obtained from a toroidal graph by 4-edge-cut expansions is not necessarily toroidal, but here we only think about graphs in which the resulting graph is also toroidal. 
 
 
 The toroidal graph obtained from the Petersen graph by applying 4-edge-cut expansion only once is unique up to isomorphism.
This unique graph is known as the  Blanuša snark   [ 5 ] , being the second smallest snark. 
 
 
 Applying 4-edge-cut expansions to the Blanuša snark gives us two types of snark families: the Blanuša-H k k  ( k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 ) family and the Blanuša-V k k  ( k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 ) family.
The Blanuša-H k k  snark is obtained by applying 4-edge-cut expansions  k k  times horizontally, and the Blanuša-V k k  snark is obtained by applying 4-edge-cut expansions  k k  times vertically 11 11 
             11 
            
            
            
          The embedding of Blanǔsa snark
in the first figure in Figure  4  is the basis for vertical and horizontal alignment. .
In this paper, we call all snarks in the Blanuša-H k k  and Blanuša-V k k  family as well as the Blanuša snark itself the  Blanuša snark family , and we call H or V the  type  and  k k  the  level  of the Blanuša snark.
The original Blanuša snark has no type and has level  0 0 .
It is known that any two members of the Blanuša snark family with different type or level are nonisomorphic.
The embeddings of the Petersen graph, the Blanuša, Blanuša-H 1 1 , Blanuša-V 1 1 , and Blanuša-V2 snark, as well as their duals on the torus, are shown in Figures  3 – 7 .
We show all embeddings of each of these snarks (up to its automorphisms) since we need to investigate the properties of any embedding of these snarks later in this paper. 12 12 
             12 
            
            
            
          For Blanuša-V k k  ( k ≥ 3 k\geq 3 ), Blanuša-H l l  ( l ≥ 2 l\geq 2 ), the flexibility of embeddings only depends on the difference of how four edges of (2,2)-annulus-cut are embedded. This can be easily shown by induction on  k , l k,l . For safety, we enumerate all the embeddings of the snarks up to  k ≤ 3 , k ≤ 3 k\leq 3,k\leq 3  by computer. See Subsection  8.2  and Table  2 . 
 
 
 Figure 3 :  Embeddings of the Petersen graph and its dual in the torus. 
 
 
 Figure 4 :  Embeddings of the Blanuša snark and its dual in the torus. 
 
 
 Figure 5 :  Embeddings of the Blanuša-H1 snark and its dual in the torus. 
 
 
 Figure 6 :  Embeddings of the Blanuša-V1 snark and its dual in the torus. 
 
 
 Figure 7 :  Embeddings of Blanuša-V2 snark and its dual in the torus. 
 
 
 Because  P 10 P_{10}  is not 3-edge-colorable, Lemma  2.3  implies that graphs in  𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0}  are not 3-edge-colorable.
These are the obvious non-3-edge-colorable toroidal cubic graphs, and no other examples are known.
In this paper, we prove that these are the only non-3edge-colorable cubic graphs that can be embedded in the torus. In other words, any graph in  𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}  can be 3-edge-colored. While trying to prove this, we consider a hypothetical counterexample and discuss how it should look like until finally reaching a contradiction. 
 
 
 Definition 2.4 . 
 
 A 2-connected cubic graph  G ∈ 𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}  is called a  counterexample  if the graph does not admit a 3-edge-coloring. A counterexample is  minimal  if there are no counterexamples of smaller order in  𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0} . 
 
 
 
 Our main result, Theorem  1.3 , is equivalent to saying that there are no counterexamples in  𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0} . 
 
 
 The following lemmas, whose proof will be given in Appendix (section  D ), hold. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.5 . 
 
 A minimal counterexample  G ∈ 𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}  is cyclically 5-edge-connected. Furthermore, for any cyclic cut  F F  of size  5 5 , one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  must be a 5-cycle. 
 
 
 
 Definition 2.6 . 
 
 A  ( k , l ) (k,l) -annulus-cut in an embedded graph  G G  in the torus is an edge cut  { e 1 , … , e k , e k + 1 , … , e k + l } \{e_{1},...,e_{k},e_{k+1},...,e_{k+l}\}  such that there exists a non-contractible simple closed curve that intersects only  e 1 , … , e k e_{1},...,e_{k}  in  G G , and the same for  e k + 1 , … , e k + l e_{k+1},...,e_{k+l} . 
 
 
 
 We also prove the following two lemmas whose proof will be given in Appendix (section  D ). 
 
 
 Lemma 2.7 . 
 
 A minimal counterexample  G ∈ 𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}  has no cyclic (2,  ≤ \leq  2)-annulus-cut.
If a cyclic (2, 3)-annulus-cut exists in a minimal counterexample  G ∈ 𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0} , one of the connected components of the remaining graph obtained by removing the annulus-cut is a 5-cycle. 
 
 
 
 We also need the following result. 
 
 
 Lemma 2.8 . 
 
 A minimal counterexample  G ∈ 𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}  has no cyclic (1,  ≤ \leq  3)-annulus-cut. 
 
 
 
 Note that we cannot get rid of  ( 1 , 4 ) (1,4) -annulus-cut, but we can actually handle the representativity one case separately, see Lemma  5.7 . 
 
 
 These lemmas imply the following: 
 
 
 Lemma 2.9 . 
 
 A minimal counterexample  G ∈ 𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}  has no two noncontractible curves  C 1 , C 2 C_{1},C_{2}  hitting exactly two vertices, such that  C 1 , C 2 C_{1},C_{2}  bound the two cylinders that both contain at least one vertex (that is, there is one vertex neither on  C 1 C_{1}  nor on  C 2 C_{2}  but in the cylinder) 
 
 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample.
Throughout the paper, we will interchangeably consider the cubic graph  G ∈ 𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}  and its dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  in our statements and proofs.
Since  G G  is cubic and nonplanar,  G ′ G^{\prime}  is a triangulation in the torus. The graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  is loopless since otherwise,  G G  would have a cutedge. 
 
 
 For other definitions and notations used in the paper, we refer to Diestel’s book  [ 10 ]  for basic graph theory and to the monograph  [ 24 ]  for notions in topological graph theory. 
 
 
 
 3  Reducible configurations 

 
 In this section, we start by considering the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime} , which is a triangulation in the torus.
Let us first recall basic definitions of configurations and reducibility used in  [ 26 ] .
A  near-triangulation  is a plane graph whose faces are triangles with the exception of the infinite face. 
 
 
 Definition 3.1 . 
 
 A  configuration   K K  consists of a near-triangulation  G ⁡ ( K ) = ( V ⁡ ( K ) , E ⁡ ( K ) ) G(K)=(V(K),E(K)) , and a map  γ K : V ⁡ ( K ) → ℤ + \gamma_{K}\colon V(K)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{+}  satisfying the following conditions. 
 
 (i)  
 
 For every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K) ,  G ⁡ ( K ) − v G(K)-v  has at most two components, and if there are two, then  γ K ​ ( v ) = deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) + 2 \gamma_{K}(v)=\deg_{G(K)}(v)+2 . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 For every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K) , if  v v  is not incident with the infinite face, then  γ K ​ ( v ) = deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)=\deg_{G(K)}(v) , and otherwise  γ K ​ ( v ) > deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)>\deg_{G(K)}(v) . In either case,  γ K ​ ( v ) ≥ 5 \gamma_{K}(v)\geq 5 . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 K K  has ring-size  ≥ 2 \geq 2  where  ring-size  is  ∑ v ( γ K ​ ( v ) − deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) − 1 ) \sum_{v}(\gamma_{K}(v)-\deg_{G(K)}(v)-1) , and the summation runs over all vertices  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K)  that are on the infinite face and for which  G ⁡ ( K ) − v G(K)-v  is connected. 
 
 
 
 
 
 In what follows, we will have many figures showing configurations. In all of them, we will use the Heesch notation  [ 14 ]  designating vertex degrees. To help the reader, we collect the vertex-shapes in Figure  8 . We want to represent the degree of at least 11 in a few cases. Especially, in Figure  11 , the degree of 12 is represented by describing the number near the vertex. 
 
 
 Figure 8 :  Shapes used to designate vertices of specific degrees. 
 
 
 Definition 3.2 . 
 
 Let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be a triangulation in some surface  Σ \Sigma . A configuration  K K   appears  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  if 
 
 (i)  
 
 G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is an induced subgraph of  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 Every finite face of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is also a face of  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 Every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K)  satisfies  γ K ​ ( v ) = deg G ′ ⁡ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)=\deg_{G^{\prime}}(v) . 
 
 (iv)  
 
 There is a disk  D ⊂ Σ D\subset\Sigma  containing  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  in its interior, and the orientation of each oriented finite face of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is the same in  D D  (either all are clockwise or all are counterclockwise). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.3 . 
 
 Let  K K  be a configuration, and  R = ( V ⁡ ( R ) , E ⁡ ( R ) ) R=(V(R),E(R))  be a cycle whose length is equal to the ring-size of  K K  and is disjoint from  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K) . A near-triangulation  S S  is a  free completion  of K with  ring   R R  if 
 
 (i)  
 
 R R  is a cycle in  S S  that bounds the infinite face of  S S . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is a subgraph of  S S , induced on  V ⁡ ( S ) ∖ V ⁡ ( R ) V(S)\setminus V(R) , i.e.  G ⁡ ( K ) = S − V ⁡ ( R ) G(K)=S-V(R) , every finite region of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is a finite region of  S S , and the infinite region of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  includes  R R  and the infinite region of  S S . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 Every vertex  v v  of  S S  not in  V ⁡ ( R ) V(R)  has degree  γ K ​ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)  in S. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.4 . 
 
 An  island   I I  is a plane graph with the following properties. 
 
 (i)  
 
 I I  is 2-connected. 
 
 (ii)  
 
 Every vertex of  I I  has degree two or three. 
 
 (iii)  
 
 Every vertex of degree two is incident with the infinite region. 
 
 
 An island  appears  in a cubic graph  G ∈ 𝒯 1 G\in\mathcal{T}_{1}  if  I I  is a subgraph of  G G , and every finite face of  I I  is also a face of  G G . 
 
 
 
 For any configuration  K K , the inner dual 13 13 
             13 
            
            
            
          The  inner dual  is obtained from the dual graph by deleting the vertex that corresponds to the infinite face.  of the free completion of  K K  is an island. This island is called the  island of  K K  and will be denoted by  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K) . 
 
 
 Definition 3.5 . 
 
 Let  k k  be a positive integer.
A  match  is an unordered pair  { a , b } \{a,b\}  of distinct integers in  { 1 , … , k } \{1,\dots,k\} .
Two matches  { a , b } \{a,b\}  and  { c , d } \{c,d\}  with  1 ≤ a < c < b < d ≤ k 1\leq a<c<b<d\leq k  are said to  overlap .
A  signed match  is a pair  ( m , μ ) (m,\mu)  where  m m  is a match and  μ = ± 1 \mu=\pm 1 . 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.6 . 
 
 A  matching  is a set  M M  of matches such that any two distinct matches  { a 1 , b 1 } , { a 2 , b 2 } ∈ M \{a_{1},b_{1}\},\{a_{2},b_{2}\}\in M  is disjoint, i.e.  { a 1 , b 1 } ∩ { a 2 , b 2 } = ∅ \{a_{1},b_{1}\}\cap\{a_{2},b_{2}\}=\varnothing .
Let  M M  be a matching and  χ M \chi_{M}  be a mapping  M → { − 1 , + 1 } M\to\{-1,+1\} .
The set  M ′ = { ( m , χ ⁡ ( m ) ) ∣ m ∈ M } M^{\prime}=\{(m,\chi(m))\mid m\in M\}  is called the  signed matching  corresponding to  M M , and  χ M \chi_{M}  is called the  sign function .
We denote  E ⁡ ( M ) E(M)  as the union of all matches in  M M  where  M M  is a matching, and we denote  E ⁡ ( M ′ ) E(M^{\prime})  as the union of all matches in  M M  where  M ′ M^{\prime}  is a signed matching corresponding to  M M . 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.7 . 
 
 A  planar matching  is a matching  M M  such that no two different matches in  M M  overlap.
A  planar signed matching  is a signed matching corresponding to some planar matching  M M . 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.8 . 
 
 A  toroidal matching  is a matching  M M  with one of the following properties. 
 
 
 
 (i)  
 
 M M  is a planar matching. 
 
 (ii)  
 
 There exist two overlapping matches  { a 1 , b 1 } , { a 2 , b 2 } ∈ M \{a_{1},b_{1}\},\{a_{2},b_{2}\}\in M  ( a 1 < a 2 < b 1 < b 2 a_{1}<a_{2}<b_{1}<b_{2} ) such that, the following matching  M ′ M^{\prime}  is a planar matching. 
 

 
 
 M ′ \displaystyle M^{\prime} 
 = { { f ⁡ ( a ) , f ⁡ ( b ) } ∣ { a , b } ∈ M ∖ { { a 1 , b 1 } , { a 2 , b 2 } } } \displaystyle=\{\{f(a),f(b)\}\mid\{a,b\}\in M\setminus\{\{a_{1},b_{1}\},\{a_{2},b_{2}\}\}\} 
 
 
 
 where  ​ f ​ ( x ) \displaystyle\textrm{where }f(x) 
 = { x − a 2 + b 2 − a 1 − 3 + 2 ​ ( | M | − 2 ) ( x < a 1 ) x − a 2 + b 2 − b 1 − 1 ( a 2 < x < b 1 ) x − b 1 ( b 1 < x < b 2 ) x − a 1 + b 2 − a 2 − 2 ( a 1 < x < a 2 ) x − a 2 + b 2 − a 1 − 3 ( b 2 < x ) \displaystyle=\begin{cases}x-a_{2}+b_{2}-a_{1}-3+2(|M|-2)&(x<a_{1})\\
x-a_{2}+b_{2}-b_{1}-1&(a_{2}<x<b_{1})\\
x-b_{1}&(b_{1}<x<b_{2})\\
x-a_{1}+b_{2}-a_{2}-2&(a_{1}<x<a_{2})\\
x-a_{2}+b_{2}-a_{1}-3&(b_{2}<x)\\
\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 A  toroidal signed matching  is a signed matching corresponding to some toroidal matching  M M . 
 
 
 
 The mapping  f f  is a way to convert matchings embedded in the torus to the plane. (See Figure  9 .) 
 
 
 Figure 9 :  The mapping  f f  converts the left toroidal matching to the right planar matching. 
 
 
 Let a coloring  κ \kappa  be a mapping from  { 1 , … , k } \{1,\dots,k\}  to  { 0 , 1 , 2 } \{0,1,2\} .
The coloring  κ \kappa  satisfies  the parity condition  if  | κ − 1 ​ ( 0 ) | ≡ | κ − 1 ​ ( 1 ) | ≡ | κ − 1 ​ ( 2 ) | ( mod 2 ) |\kappa^{-1}(0)|\equiv|\kappa^{-1}(1)|\equiv|\kappa^{-1}(2)|\pmod{2} .
We will only consider colorings that satisfy the parity condition since every edge-coloring of edges in a cut satisfies the parity condition. 
 
 
 Definition 3.9 . 
 
 Let  κ : { 1 , … , k } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \kappa:\{1,\dots,k\}\rightarrow\{0,1,2\}  be a coloring that satisfies parity. For  θ ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } \theta\in\{0,1,2\} ,  κ \kappa  is said to  θ \theta -fit  a signed matching  M M  if 
 
 (i)  
 
 E ( M ) = { e ∣ 1 ≤ e ≤ k , κ ( e ) ≠ θ } E(M)=\{e\mid 1\leq e\leq k,\kappa(e)\neq\theta\} 
 
 (ii)  
 
 For any  ( { e , f } , μ ) ∈ M (\{e,f\},\mu)\in M ,  κ ⁡ ( e ) = κ ⁡ ( f ) \kappa(e)=\kappa(f)  if and only if  μ = 1 \mu=1 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.10 . 
 
 Let  ℳ \mathcal{M}  be some set of matchings.
A set  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  of colorings  { 1 , … , k } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \{1,\dots,k\}\rightarrow\{0,1,2\}  that satisfy parity is  consistent over   ℳ \mathcal{M}  if for every  κ ∈ 𝒞 \kappa\in\mathscr{C}  and every  θ ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 } \theta\in\{0,1,2\}  there is a signed matching  M ′ M^{\prime}  corresponding to a matching  M ∈ ℳ M\in\mathcal{M}  and a sign function  χ M \chi_{M}  such that  κ \kappa   θ \theta -fits  M ′ M^{\prime} , and  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  contains every coloring that  θ \theta -fits  M ′ M^{\prime} .
Especially, when  ℳ \mathcal{M}  is the set of all planar matchings,  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  is said to be consistent on the plane, and when  ℳ \mathcal{M}  is the set of all toroidal matchings,  𝒞 \mathscr{C}  is said to be consistent in the torus. 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.11 . 
 
 Let  I I  be an island, and let  ℳ \mathcal{M}  be a set of matchings.
We add a new incident edge to each vertex of degree 2 in  I I  and denote the set of all added edges by  R R .
Let  𝒞 ∗ \mathscr{C}^{\ast}  be the set of all colorings  { 1 , … , | R | } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \{1,\dots,|R|\}\rightarrow\{0,1,2\}  that satisfies parity.
Let  𝒞 0 \mathscr{C_{0}}  be the set of all  3 3 -edge-colorings of  R R  that are restrictions of  3 3 -edge-colorings of  I ∪ R I\cup R .
Let  𝒞 ′ \mathscr{C}^{\prime}  be the maximal consistent subset of  𝒞 ∗ − 𝒞 0 \mathscr{C}^{\ast}-\mathscr{C_{0}}  over  ℳ \mathcal{M} .
The island  I I  is said to be  D-reducible  over  ℳ \mathcal{M}  if  𝒞 ′ = ∅ \mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\emptyset .
We also call the configuration  K K   D-reducible  over  ℳ \mathcal{M}  if the corresponding island  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  is D-reducible. 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.12 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a subcubic graph and  F F  be a set of edges in  G G  that satisfy the following conditions. 
 
 •  
 
 The endpoints of  f ∈ F f\in F  have degree 3. 
 
 •  
 
 For all  v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) v\in V(G) , the number of edges adjacent to  v v  in  F F  is not  2 2 . 
 
 
 Let  G ∸ F G\dotdiv F  be the subcubic graph obtained from  G G  by deleting the edges in  F F  and suppressing its endpoints of degree 2. 
 
 
 
 Note that if  G G  is a cubic graph,  G ∸ F G\dotdiv F  is also a cubic graph. 
 
 
 Definition 3.13 . 
 
 Let  I I  be an island and let  X ⊆ E ⁡ ( I ) X\subseteq E(I)  be such that none of the vertices in  I I  is incident with exactly two edges of  X X .
Let  I ′ = I ∸ X I^{\prime}=I\dotdiv X  .
Let  𝒟 \mathscr{D}  be all restrictions to  R R  of  3 3 -edge-colorings of  I ′ ∪ R I^{\prime}\cup R .
 I I  is  C-reducible  if
 𝒟 ∩ 𝒞 ′ = ∅ \mathscr{D}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\emptyset . 
 
 
 The edges in  X X  are called  contraction edges 14 14 
                 14 
                
                
                
               Although the edges in  X X  are not contracted, but rather deleted in  I I , we use the term “contraction” following previous work.
This is because the term originated from definitions using the near-triangulation  K K  rather than the cubic dual  I I .  of  I I , and  X X  is denoted by  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I) .
The size of  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  is called the  contraction size  of  I I . 
 
 
 A configuration  K K  is also  C-reducible  if the corresponding island  I ⁡ ( K ) I(K)  is C-reducible.
In this case, we call the edges of  K K  corresponding to the edges in  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  the  contraction edges  and denote them by  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) . 
 
 
 
 Let  I I  be a reducible island in a minimal counterexample  G G .
By the definition of reducibility, if we can 3-edge color  G ∸ c ⁡ ( I ) G\dotdiv c(I) , we can also 3-edge color  G G .
This can be constructively proved by a method known as  Kempe changes   [ 4 ] [ 26 ] . Hence, we omit the proof here since it is identical to previous works for planar graphs (note that reducibility implies that Kempe chains are enough to extend a 3-edge coloring of  G G  from that of  G ∸ c ⁡ ( I ) G\dotdiv c(I) ). 
 
 
 This means that contracting the edges in  c ⁡ ( I ) c(I)  will not affect the non-3-edge colorability of a graph  G G  in which  I I  is a subgraph.
This implies the following lemma. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.14 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample embedded in the torus  Σ \Sigma .
Suppose that  C C  is a circuit of  G G  bounding a closed disk  Δ \Delta  such that the subgraph of  G G  formed by the vertices and edges drawn in  Δ \Delta  is a reducible island  I I .
Then  G ∸ c ⁡ ( I ) G\dotdiv c(I)  either has a bridge or belongs to  𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0} . 
 
 
 
 Now, we introduce a set of reducible configurations  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , which we have constructed by hand and verified each of its reducibility by computer programs.
There are more than 14000 reducible configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , and the reader can find all the reducible configurations (including contraction edges for  C C -reducible configurations) in the aforementioned Google Drive.
The set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}   15 15 
             15 
            
            
            
          Note that  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  consists of normal configurations and does not contain any annular configuration or any wrapping of a normal configuration (”normal configurations and wrapping” are defined later).  also happens to be an unavoidable set of reducible configurations in the torus.
We prove unavoidability by the discharging method later in this paper; see lemma  4.5 .
In Table  1 , we show the contraction size of each configuration of  𝒦 \mathcal{K} . The contraction size being zero means D-reducible.
Let us remark the following. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 There are 170 configurations that need to contract at least five edges.
Indeed, there are  90 , 59 , 16 , 3 , 1 , 1 90,59,16,3,1,1  configurations where the contraction edge size is 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, and 10, respectively. See Table  1 . 
 
 •  
 
 There are around 1000 configurations that contain pairs of vertices at a distance of exactly five (although none of our configurations contain pairs of vertices at a distance of six or more).

 3.1  General configurations 

 
 The definitions of  configurations  and  islands  stated above are derived from previous work.
However, in later sections, we need to use configurations that are not planar and even configurations with two or more boundaries. To account for this, we introduce a more general type of configurations which we define below. 
 
 
 Definition 3.15 . 
 
 A  general configuration   K K  consists of a graph  G ⁡ ( K ) = ( V ⁡ ( K ) , E ⁡ ( K ) ) G(K)=(V(K),E(K))  that is 2-cell embedded in some closed surface  𝕊 \mathbb{S} , a set of faces 16 16 
                   16 
                  
                  
                  
                 Face boundaries here need not be cycles; just closed facial walks.  of  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  which we denote by  𝒬 ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{Q}(K) , and a map  γ K : V ⁡ ( K ) → ℤ + \gamma_{K}:V(K)\to\mathbb{Z}_{+}  satisfying the following conditions: 
 
 1.  
 
 For every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K) , there are at most two faces in  𝒬 ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{Q}(K)  that are incident with  v v . If  v v  is incident with  Q ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ ( K ) Q\in\mathcal{Q}(K) , we denote by  w Q ​ ( v ) w_{Q}(v)  the number of times the boundary of  Q Q  (considered as a closed facial walk) touches  v v . (For convenience, if  v v  is not incident with  Q Q , we set  w Q ​ ( v ) = 0 w_{Q}(v)=0 .) 
 
 2.  
 
 For every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K) , if  v v  is not incident with a face in  𝒬 ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{Q}(K) , then  γ K ​ ( v ) = deg G ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)=\text{deg}_{G(K)}(v) , and otherwise  γ K ​ ( v ) ≥ deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) + ∑ Q ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ ( K ) w Q ​ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)\geq\deg_{G(K)}(v)+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}(K)}w_{Q}(v) .
In either case,  γ K ​ ( v ) ≥ 5 \gamma_{K}(v)\geq 5 . 
 
 3.  
 
 For every face  Q ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ ( K ) Q\in\mathcal{Q}(K) , there is a value  r ⁡ ( Q ) r(Q)  called the  ring size , defined by the following formula: 
 

 
 
 r ⁡ ( Q ) = ∑ v ( γ K ​ ( v ) − deg G ⁡ ( K ) ⁡ ( v ) − w Q ​ ( v ) ) , r(Q)=\sum_{v}(\gamma_{K}(v)-\deg_{G(K)}(v)-w_{Q}(v)), 
 
 
 where the summation runs over all vertices  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K)  that are incident with  Q Q . 
 
 4.  
 
 All faces that are of size greater than 3 must be in  𝒬 ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{Q}(K) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 We can see that a (normal) configuration is the special case of general configurations, where  𝒬 ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{Q}(K)  consists of only one face (the outer face boundary). 
 
 
 We also define  free completions  and  rings  for general configurations. 
 
 
 Definition 3.16 . 
 
 Let  K K  be a general configuration and let  ℛ ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{R}(K)  be a set of cycles, in which for each cycle  R ∈ ℛ ⁡ ( K ) R\in\mathcal{R}(K)  there exists a corresponding face  Q R ∈ 𝒬 ⁡ ( K ) Q_{R}\in\mathcal{Q}(K)  such that  | V ⁡ ( R ) | = r ⁡ ( Q R ) |V(R)|=r(Q_{R}) .
A graph  S S  embedded in the torus is a  free completion  of  K K  with  ring set   ℛ ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{R}(K)  if the following conditions are satisfied. 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 R R  is a subgraph of  S − G ⁡ ( K ) S-G(K) , and for every vertex of  r r  of  R R , there is a vertex adjacent to  r r , which is in the boundary of the corresponding face  Q R Q_{R} . 
 
 2.  
 
 G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  is a subgraph of  S S , induced on  V ⁡ ( S ) ∖ V ⁡ ( R ) V(S)\setminus V(R) . 
 
 3.  
 
 Every  v ∈ V ⁡ ( S ) v\in V(S)  not in  V ⁡ ( R ) V(R)  has degree  γ K ​ ( v ) \gamma_{K}(v)  in  S S . 
 
 
 
 
 
 We can again see that this definition coincides with the original definition of free completions when  K K  is a normal configuration (with the ring set  ℛ ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{R}(K)  containing the ring  R R ). 
 
 
 When the surface  𝕊 \mathbb{S}  is a sphere and  ℛ ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{R}(K)  contains exactly two faces, we call  K K  an  annular configuration .
An  annular island  is obtained by taking the dual of a free completion of an annular configuration and removing vertices that correspond to  R ∈ ℛ ⁡ ( K ) R\in\mathcal{R}(K) .
It corresponds to the fact that a normal island is obtained by taking the inner dual of a free completion of a normal configuration. 
 
 
 For annular configurations, we define reducibility using the following  annular matchings . 
 
 
 Definition 3.17 . 
 
 Let  k k  be a positive integer and let  0 < a < b 0<a<b  be positive integers.
Let  M M  be a matching with  one of  the following properties. 
 
 
 
 (i)  
 
 There are no matches  { i , j } ∈ M , i < j \{i,j\}\in M,i<j  such that  i < a < j i<a<j , and the two disjoint matchings  M 1 = { { i , j } ∈ M ∣ i < j < a } M_{1}=\{\{i,j\}\in M\mid i<j<a\}  and  M 2 = { { i , j } ∈ M ∣ a < i < j } M_{2}=\{\{i,j\}\in M\mid a<i<j\}  are both planar matchings. 
 
 (ii)  
 
 There exists a match  { a ′ , b ′ } ∈ M , a ′ < a < b ′ \{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}\in M,a^{\prime}<a<b^{\prime}  such that, the matching  M ′ = { { f ( i ) , f ( j ) } ∣ { i , j } ∈ M , { i , j } ≠ { a ′ , b ′ } } M^{\prime}=\{\{f(i),f(j)\}\mid\{i,j\}\in M,\{i,j\}\neq\{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}\}  is a planar matching.
Here,  f f  is a mapping defined as below. 
 

 
 
 f ⁡ ( x ) \displaystyle f(x) 
 = { x ( x < a ′ ) x − a ′ + b − a + a ′ − 2 ( a ′ < x < a ) x − a + a ′ + b − b ′ − 1 ( a ≤ x < b ′ ) x − b ′ + a ′ − 1 ( b ′ < x ) \displaystyle=\begin{cases}x&(x<a^{\prime})\\
x-a^{\prime}+b-a+a^{\prime}-2&(a^{\prime}<x<a)\\
x-a+a^{\prime}+b-b^{\prime}-1&(a\leq x<b^{\prime})\\
x-b^{\prime}+a^{\prime}-1&(b^{\prime}<x)\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 We call such  M M  a  annular matching  of size  ( a , b − a ) (a,b-a) . 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.18 . 
 
 Let  K K  be a general configuration with  ℛ ⁡ ( K ) \mathcal{R}(K)  containing two rings  R 1 , R 2 R_{1},R_{2} .
We label the edges in  R 1 R_{1}  with numbers  { 1 , ⋯ , r 1 } \{1,\cdots,r_{1}\}  and edges in  R 2 R_{2}  with numbers  { r 1 + 1 , ⋯ , r 1 + r 2 } \{r_{1}+1,\cdots,r_{1}+r_{2}\} , where  r 1 = | R 1 | r_{1}=|R_{1}|  and  r 2 = | R 2 | r_{2}=|R_{2}| .
Labelings are done in a direction such that the two disks bounded by  R 1 R_{1}  and  R 2 R_{2}  preserve the surface’s orientability. 
 
 
 Then, with a coloring  κ : { 1 , ⋯ , r 1 + r 2 } → { 0 , 1 , 2 } \kappa:\{1,\cdots,r_{1}+r_{2}\}\to\{0,1,2\} , we define  θ \theta -fitting  with the above edge/label relationship.
The notions of  consistency ,  D-reducibility ,  C-reducibility  are defined in the same way as for normal configurations. 
 
 
 
 
 3.2  Weak appearance of configurations 

 
 The definition of the appearance of configurations can also be expanded to general configurations.
We will define another term, “weak appearance,” for use in later sections. 
 
 
 Definition 3.19 . 
 
 Let  K K  be a general configuration with free completion  S S , and let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be a triangulation.
 K K  is said to  appear  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  if there exists a mapping  f : V ⁡ ( S ) → V ⁡ ( G ′ ) f:V(S)\to V(G^{\prime})  such that 
 
 (i)  
 
 There is no  v , u ∈ V ⁡ ( S ) v,u\in V(S)  where at least one of  v , u v,u  is in  V ⁡ ( K ) V(K)  such that  v ≠ u v\neq u  and  f ⁡ ( v ) = f ⁡ ( u ) f(v)=f(u) . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 For any  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K) ,  γ K ​ ( v ) = deg G ′ ​ ( f ⁡ ( v ) ) \gamma_{K}(v)=\text{deg}_{G^{\prime}}(f(v)) . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 For any  v , u , w ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v,u,w\in V(K)  such that the three vertices are vertices of a triangle face in  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K) , the three vertices  f ⁡ ( v ) , f ⁡ ( u ) , f ⁡ ( w ) f(v),f(u),f(w)  are vertices of a triangle face in  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 Furthermore,  K K  is said to be  weakly appearing  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  when condition (i) is dropped. 
 
 
 
 We also define a term closely related to weak appearance as follows. 
 
 
 Definition 3.20 . 
 
 Let  K K  and  K ′ K^{\prime}  be general configurations.
 K ′ K^{\prime}  is said to be a  wrapping  of  K K  if there exists a mapping  f : V ⁡ ( K ) → V ⁡ ( K ′ ) f:V(K)\to V(K^{\prime})  such that 
 
 (i)  
 
 For any  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K) ,  γ K ​ ( v ) = γ K ′ ​ ( f ⁡ ( v ) ) \gamma_{K}(v)=\gamma_{K^{\prime}}(f(v)) . 
 
 (ii)  
 
 For any  v , u , w ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v,u,w\in V(K)  such that the three vertices are vertices of a triangle face in  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K) , the three vertices  f ⁡ ( v ) , f ⁡ ( u ) , f ⁡ ( w ) f(v),f(u),f(w)  are vertices of a triangle face in  G ⁡ ( K ′ ) G(K^{\prime}) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 By definition, if a general configuration  K ′ K^{\prime}  is a wrapping of another general configuration  K K , and  K ′ K^{\prime}  appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} ,  K K  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 Why do we need these terms?
As discussed in this section, the appearance of a reducible configuration  K K  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  results in a reduction of  G ′ G^{\prime} .
However, the  discharging  method used in Section  4  only asserts the weak appearance of some reducible configuration  K K , which is not enough to warrant a reduction of  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 This is when the wrappings of  K K  come into play.
Even if we can only assert the weak appearance of  K K , if we find out that for any wrapping of  K K  (say  K ′ K^{\prime} ) is also reducible, we can use  K ′ K^{\prime}  instead for finding a reduction of  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 More about this is discussed in Section  7 . 
 
 
 
 3.3  Distance five in reducible configurations 

 
 In the proof of the 4CT in  [ 26 ] , there are 633 reducible configurations, all of which are of diameter at most four.
On the other hand, in our set of reducible configurations  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , there are many configurations whose diameter is five (although we do not use any reducible configuration of diameter six or more). 
 
 
 If a reducible configuration  K K  in a planar graph is of diameter at most four,  K K  is an induced subgraph (or otherwise, there is a separating cycle of order at most five with both sides having at least two vertices, which would not exist in a minimal counterexample).
This is also the case for the doublecross.
However, in the torus case, we have to deal with the case when a reducible configuration is not induced. 
 
 
 As with the sections above,  G ′ G^{\prime}  is a triangulation that is embedded in the torus, and  K K  is a reducible configuration in  G ′ G^{\prime} .
Let us assume that there are two vertices  u , v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) u,v\in V(K)  that are at distance five in  K K , and there is an edge  u ​ v ∈ E ⁡ ( G ′ ) uv\in E(G^{\prime}) .
Then we obtain a cycle  C C  of length exactly six, and assume that  C C  is a contractible cycle in  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 In the cubic graph  G G ,  C C  corresponds to a 6-cut, which yields a contractible curve of order exactly six. 
 
 
 Using Lemma  2.5  and assuming Lemma  6.2 , which we will prove in Section  6 , we get the following fact. 
 
 
 Fact 3.21 . 
 
 If  G ′ G^{\prime}  is a minimal counterexample, then  G ′ G^{\prime}  contains no cycles with any of the following properties: 
 
 •  
 
 A contractible cycle of length at most four that divides  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two nonempty components. 
 
 •  
 
 A contractible cycle of length five that divides  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two components of order at least two. 
 
 •  
 
 A contractible cycle of length six that divides  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two components of order at least four. 
 
 
 
 
 
 To handle the above case, we show the following. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.22 . 
 
 Let  C C  be a cycle of length  6 6  in  G ′ G^{\prime} , as discussed above. Then, it satisfies Fact  3.21 , and thus cannot appear in a minimal counterexample. 
 
 
 
 The proof of Lemma  3.22  is the same as that of Lemma 3.16 in  [ 16 ]  except that we only care about a contractible cycle here (the noncontractible cycle case will be handled in Section  7 ). We use the same computer program. In almost all cases, we conclude that the cycle would contradict Fact  3.21 . However, there are 23 cases that we cannot immediately conclude. We show them in Figure  10 . For the remaining 22 cases, we confirm that there are separating 4-cycles, which would still contradict Fact  3.21 . For one case drawn in the second column from the left in the last row, another reducible configuration C(2) 17 17 
               17 
              
              
              
            C(2) is the second configuration drawn in Figure  11 , which is explained later.  appears, so we do not care about the case. Therefore, Lemma  3.22  holds. 
 
 
 Figure 10 :  The 23 cases that remain after computer-check in the proof of Lemma  3.22 . The dotted lines represent edges when we assume two vertices of distance five are adjacent. 
 
 
 
 
 4  Discharging process 

 
 Let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be an internally 6-connected triangulation in the torus 18 18 
             18 
            
            
            
          We assume that the representativity of  G ′ G^{\prime}  is at least two. Note that it could be exactly two, so there could be multiple edges. But the embedding is 2-cell, so we can still apply the rule below . We start by assigning to each vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) v\in V(G^{\prime})  the value 
 

 
 
 T 0 ​ ( v ) := 10 ​ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( v ) ) , T_{0}(v):=10(6-d(v)), 
 
 
 which we call the  initial charge at  v v . It follows by Euler’s formula (see Lemma  4.4  below) that 
 

 
 
 ∑ v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) T 0 ​ ( v ) = 0 . \sum_{v\in V(G^{\prime})}T_{0}(v)=0. 
 
 
 Now we redistribute the charge among vertices by applying the  discharging rules  that are shown in Section  B  in the appendix. There are 201 rules (compared to 33 rules for the proof of the 4CT and that for the doublecross case). 
 
 
 Discharging rules are formally defined as follows. 
 
 
 Definition 4.1 . 
 
 A  rule  is six-tuple  R = ( G ⁡ ( R ) , β R , δ R , r ⁡ ( R ) , s ⁡ ( R ) , t ⁡ ( R ) ) R=(G(R),\beta_{R},\delta_{R},r(R),s(R),t(R)) , so that 
 
 (i)  
 
 G ⁡ ( R ) = ( V ⁡ ( R ) , E ⁡ ( R ) ) G(R)=(V(R),E(R))  is a near-triangulation, and for each  v ∈ V ⁡ ( R ) v\in V(R) ,  G ⁡ ( R ) − v G(R)-v  is connected. 
 
 (ii)  
 
 β R : V ⁡ ( R ) → ℕ \beta_{R}\colon V(R)\rightarrow\mathbb{N} ,  δ R : V ⁡ ( R ) → ℕ ∪ { ∞ } \delta_{R}\colon V(R)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\} , such that  5 ≤ β R ​ ( v ) ≤ δ R ​ ( v ) 5\leq\beta_{R}(v)\leq\delta_{R}(v)  for each  v ∈ V ⁡ ( R ) v\in V(R) . 
 
 (iii)  
 
 r ⁡ ( R ) r(R)  is a positive integer. 
 
 (iv)  
 
 s ⁡ ( R ) , t ⁡ ( R ) ∈ V ⁡ ( R ) s(R),t(R)\in V(R)  are distinct adjacent vertices. 
 
 
 A configuration  K K   obeys  the rule  R R  if  G ⁡ ( K ) = G ⁡ ( R ) G(K)=G(R)  and every vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) v\in V(K)  satisfies  β R ​ ( v ) ≤ γ K ​ ( v ) ≤ δ R ​ ( v ) \beta_{R}(v)\leq\gamma_{K}(v)\leq\delta_{R}(v) . 
 
 
 
 A rule is a near-triangulation in which each vertex  v v  is given a range of possible degrees, the interval  [ β R ​ ( v ) , δ R ​ ( v ) ] [\beta_{R}(v),\delta_{R}(v)] . The value  r ⁡ ( R ) r(R)  represents the amount of charge that is sent from the vertex  s ⁡ ( R ) s(R)  to its neighbor  t ⁡ ( R ) t(R) . We have  r ⁡ ( R ) = 1 r(R)=1  or  r ⁡ ( R ) = 2 r(R)=2  for all our rules. We describe the degree range  [ β R , δ R ] [\beta_{R},\delta_{R}]  by the same convention as in configurations. There are three cases. When  β R ​ ( v ) = δ R ​ ( v ) \beta_{R}(v)=\delta_{R}(v) , we describe  β R ​ ( v ) \beta_{R}(v)  by one of the shapes shown in Figure  8 . When  5 = β R ​ ( v ) < δ R ​ ( v ) 5=\beta_{R}(v)<\delta_{R}(v) , we describe  δ R ​ ( v ) \delta_{R}(v)  as the vertex shape and set  − -  next to the vertex. When  β R ​ ( v ) < δ R ​ ( v ) = ∞ \beta_{R}(v)<\delta_{R}(v)=\infty , we describe  β R ​ ( v ) \beta_{R}(v)  as the vertex shape and set  + +  next to the vertex. All our rules have one of these three types of degree ranges. 
 
 
 The set of 201 rules exhibited in Figure  34  in the appendix is denoted by  ℛ \mathcal{R} .
An edge with an arrow represents the direction in which a charge moves. Thus,  s ⁡ ( R ) , t ⁡ ( R ) s(R),t(R)  are two vertices that are incident with an edge with an arrow.
The number of arrows in the figure represents  r ⁡ ( R ) r(R) . 
 
 
 We calculate the amount of charge sent along an edge  s ​ t st  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  by checking which rules can be used on this edge. For a rule  R ∈ ℛ R\in\mathcal{R} ,  r ⁡ ( R ) r(R)  gives the amount of charge to send,  s ⁡ ( R ) s(R)  is the vertex that sends the charge, and  t ⁡ ( R ) t(R)  is the vertex that receives charge. We  apply a rule  R R  when a configuration  K K , which obeys  R R  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Note that each 19 19 
             19 
            
            
            
          The very first rule (R1) is the only exception that is used only once.  rule  R ∈ ℛ R\in\mathcal{R}  can be applied at the edge  s ​ t st  in two ways, where the triangles in  G ⁡ ( R ) G(R)  have the same orientation as in  G ′ G^{\prime} , and when they have opposite orientation. 20 20 
             20 
            
            
            
          We just consider the orientation of triangles locally around the edge  s ​ t st . 
 
 
 Definition 4.2 . 
 
 For adjacent vertices  u , v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) u,v\in V(G^{\prime}) , we define  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) \phi(u,v)  as the sum of the values  r ⁡ ( R ) r(R)  over all rules  R ∈ ℛ R\in\mathcal{R}  that are applied with  s ⁡ ( R ) = u s(R)=u , and  t ⁡ ( R ) = v t(R)=v . This is  the amount of charge sent from  u u  to  v v . 
 
 
 
 Definition 4.3 . 
 
 For a vertex  u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) u\in V(G^{\prime}) , we set 
 

 
 
 T ⁡ ( u ) := T 0 ​ ( u ) + ∑ v ∼ u ( ϕ ⁡ ( v , u ) − ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) ) . T(u):=T_{0}(u)+\sum_{v\sim u}(\phi(v,u)-\phi(u,v)). 
 
 
 The obtained value is the  final charge  at  u u . 
 
 
 
 The discharging method uses the following easy observation. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.4 . 
 
 ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) T ⁡ ( u ) = 0 \sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}T(u)=0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By applying any discharging rule, the total sum of all charges remains the same. Thus,  ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) T ⁡ ( u ) = ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) 10 ​ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( u ) ) \sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}T(u)=\sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}10(6-d(u)) . If  n n  is the number of vertices of  G ′ G^{\prime} , then Euler’s formula for the triangulation  G ′ G^{\prime}  implies that  1 2 ​ ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) d ⁡ ( u ) = | E ⁡ ( G ′ ) | = 3 ​ n \tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}d(u)=|E(G^{\prime})|=3n , and thus we have: 
 

 
 
 ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) T ⁡ ( u ) = ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) 10 ​ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( u ) ) = 60 ​ n − 10 ​ ∑ u ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) d ⁡ ( u ) = 60 ​ n − 60 ​ n = 0 . \sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}T(u)=\sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}10(6-d(u))=60n-10\sum_{u\in V(G^{\prime})}d(u)=60n-60n=0. 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma  4.4  implies that either (i) there is a vertex  u u  with  T ⁡ ( u ) > 0 T(u)>0 , or (ii)  T ⁡ ( u ) = 0 T(u)=0  for each vertex  u u  in  V ⁡ ( G ′ ) V(G^{\prime}) . Known proofs of the 4CT show that for any vertex  u u  with  T ⁡ ( u ) > 0 T(u)>0 , there is a reducible configuration in the vicinity of  u u . This yields a contradiction to the assumption that we have a minimum counterexample. 
 
 
 We have a similar result on the torus, but there is some difference. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.5 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample with representativity at least two. Let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be the dual of  G G . If there is a vertex  v v  of  V ⁡ ( G ′ ) V(G^{\prime})  with  T ⁡ ( v ) > 0 T(v)>0 , a configuration that is isomorphic to one of the reducible configurations of  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears. 
 
 
 
 We show Lemma  4.5  by computer check, but we can show this lemma without the help of computers when  d G ′ ​ ( v ) = 5 d_{G^{\prime}}(v)=5  or  d G ′ ​ ( v ) = 6 d_{G^{\prime}}(v)=6  (Lemma  4.6 ) or  d G ′ ​ ( v ) ≥ 12 d_{G^{\prime}}(v)\geq 12  (Lemma  4.10 ). For other cases, computer-free proof would be too long. Note also that the representativity one case is handled separately, see Lemma  5.7 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 11 :  The reducible configurations used in the computer-free proof in this paper 
 
 
 Lemma 4.6 . 
 
 Let  G ′ = ( V , E ) G^{\prime}=(V,E)  be an internally 6-connected triangulation. Let  v ∈ V v\in V  be a vertex of degree 5 or 6.
Suppose that no configurations shown in Figure  11  weakly appear in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Then,  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0 . 
 
 
 
 The proof of Lemma  4.6  is the same as that given in  [ 16 ] .
Indeed the set of configurations in Figure  11  includes the configurations used in hand-written proofs of  [ 16 ] . The rules in  ℛ \mathcal{R}  such that the degree of a vertex that receives or sends charge is at most 6 (i.e.  δ R ​ ( s ⁡ ( R ) ) ≤ 6 \delta_{R}(s(R))\leq 6  or  δ R ​ ( t ⁡ ( R ) ) ≤ 6 \delta_{R}(t(R))\leq 6 ) is the same as that of rules used in  [ 16 ] . Hence, the proof of Lemma  4.6  is the same as that of Lemma about the final charge of degree  5 5  or  6 6  in  [ 16 ] .
We denote a configuration in Figure  11  by C(i)  ( 1 ≤ i ≤ 29 ) (1\leq i\leq 29)  in the natural order, from top to bottom in a row and from left to right in a column.
The bold lines in Figure  11  represents contraction edges used for C-reducibility. 
 
 
 4.1  Maximum charge that a vertex can send 

 
 Let  n n  be a positive integer. We want to figure out the case that a vertex sends charge  n n  to a vertex of degree at least  7 7 . We calculate it by combining a set of rules of  ℛ \mathcal{R}  such that the edge where charge moves coincide, and then by checking that none of our reducible configurations of  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears. We use computer-check to calculate it. The pseudocode of this algorithm is the same as the algorithm described in  [ 16 ] .
We now show the following. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.7 . 
 
 Assume that none of our reducible configurations in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Then, any vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) v\in V(G^{\prime})  can send charge at most  6 6  to any one of its neighbors.
If it sends charge  6 6  ( 5 5  resp.), then we have one of the cases shown in Figure  31  (Figure  32 , resp.) in the appendix.
If a vertex of degree  5 5  sends charge  4 4 , we have one of the cases in Figure  33  in the appendix. 
 
 
 
 We denote the case in row  i i  and column  j j  in Figure  31  by send6( i i ,  j j ). We use the same notation to denote the case in Figure  32  by send5( i i ,  j j ) and Figure  33  by send4( i i ,  j j ), respectively. 
 
 
 Using Lemma  4.7 , we prove the following. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.8 . 
 
 Assume that none of our reducible configurations in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Let  u u  be a vertex of degree at least  7 7  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  and  v 1 , v 2 , v 3 v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}  be three consecutive neighbors of  u u 
listed in the clockwise order in the embedding of  G ′ G^{\prime} .
If  ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u ) = 6 \phi(v_{1},u)=6 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) ≤ 5 \phi(v_{2},u)\leq 5  and  ϕ ⁡ ( v 3 , u ) ≤ 4 \phi(v_{3},u)\leq 4  hold. Moreover, if all the equalities hold ( ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) = 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( v 3 , u ) = 4 \phi(v_{2},u)=5,\phi(v_{3},u)=4 ), we have one of the cases shown in Figure  13 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 When the degree of  v 1 v_{1}  is 7 (that corresponds to send6(2,4), or send6(2,5)),  ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) ≤ 4 \phi(v_{2},u)\leq 4  holds by Lemma  4.7 . 
 
 
 We consider the case when degree of both  v 1 v_{1}  and  v 2 v_{2}  is 6 (that corresponds to send6(1,4), send6(2,1)).
First, we consider the case when  v 1 v_{1}  sends charge 6 in send6(1, 4).
The left figure in Figure  12  represents this situation.
The degree of  v 4 v_{4}  is at least 7, otherwise, C(2), or C(3) weakly appear.
The degree of  v 3 v_{3}  is not 5, otherwise, C(2) weakly appears.
When the degree of  v 3 v_{3}  is 6,  ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) \phi(v_{2},u)  is at most 2, otherwise one of C(17), C(16), C(24) weakly appears in the following three cases  d ⁡ ( v 5 ) = 5 d(v_{5})=5 , or  d ⁡ ( v 5 ) = 6 , d ⁡ ( v 6 ) = 5 d(v_{5})=6,d(v_{6})=5 , or  d ⁡ ( v 5 ) = 6 , d ⁡ ( v 6 ) = 6 , d ⁡ ( v 7 ) = 5 d(v_{5})=6,d(v_{6})=6,d(v_{7})=5  respectively.
When the degree of  v 3 v_{3}  is at least 7,  ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) \phi(v_{2},u)  is at most 1 by the rules we used. 
 
 
 Second, we consider the case when  v 1 v_{1}  sends charge 6 in send6(2, 1).
The right figure in Figure  12  represents this situation.
The degree of  v 4 v_{4}  is at least 7 since otherwise C(14) or C(15) weakly appears.
The value  ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) \phi(v_{2},u)  is at most 3, since otherwise one of C(4), C(25) weakly appears in the following two cases  d ⁡ ( v 5 ) = 5 d(v_{5})=5 , or  d ⁡ ( v 5 ) = 6 , d ⁡ ( v 6 ) = 5 d(v_{5})=6,d(v_{6})=5  respectively. 
 
 
 It remains to handle the case when the degree of  v 1 v_{1}  is 6 and the degree of  v 2 v_{2}  is 5 (that corresponds to send6(1,  i i ) ( 1 ≤ i ≤ 5 1\leq i\leq 5 ), send6(2,  j j ) ( 1 ≤ j ≤ 3 1\leq j\leq 3 ). When  ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) ≥ 5 \phi(v_{2},u)\geq 5 , the only possible case is that  v 2 v_{2}  sends charge 5 in send5(1,2) by Lemma  4.7 , so  ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) ≤ 5 \phi(v_{2},u)\leq 5  holds. The equality holds only when  v 1 v_{1}  sends charge 6 in send6(1,2), send6(1,3), or send6(2,2).
The degree of  v 3 v_{3}  is 5 in every case. Let  w , x , y w,x,y  be neighbors of  v 3 v_{3}  such that  u , v 2 , x , y , z u,v_{2},x,y,z  are listed in the clockwise order in the embedding of  G ′ G^{\prime} . If the degree of  y y  is 5, C(2) weakly appears. If the degree of  y y  is 6 and the degree of  z z  is 5, C(17) weakly appears. Hence  ϕ ⁡ ( v 3 , u ) ≤ 4 \phi(v_{3},u)\leq 4  by Lemma  4.7  (Figure  32 ). Moreover, the equality holds only when
 ϕ ⁡ ( v 3 , u ) \phi(v_{3},u)  is exactly  4 4  in send4(1,4), or send4(2,1). However C(19) weakly appears in send4(2,1), so send4(1,4) is the only case. Hence, if  ϕ ⁡ ( v 3 , u ) \phi(v_{3},u)  is exactly  4 4 , all cases are enumerated in Figure  13 .
∎
