 Lemma 4.9 . 
 
 Assume that none of our reducible configurations in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} .
Let  u u  be a vertex of degree at least 7 in  G ′ G^{\prime} , and  v 1 , v 2 , … , v n ​ ( 1 ≤ n ≤ d G ′ ​ ( u ) ) v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}(1\leq n\leq d_{G^{\prime}}(u))  be consecutive neighbors of  u u  listed in the clockwise order in the embedding of  G ′ G^{\prime} .
The sum of  ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u ) ​ ( 1 ≤ i ≤ n ) \phi(v_{i},u)(1\leq i\leq n)  is at most  5 ​ n + 1 5n+1 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This proof is by induction on  n n .
When  n ≤ 3 n\leq 3 , the claim holds by Lemma  4.7 ,  4.8 .
We assume  n ≥ 4 n\geq 4 . Suppose for a contradiction that a counterexample to the claim exists.
By Lemma  4.8 , either of  ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u ) ≤ 5 \phi(v_{1},u)\leq 5 , or  ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u ) = 6 , ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) ≤ 4 \phi(v_{1},u)=6,\phi(v_{2},u)\leq 4 , or  ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u ) = 6 , ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u ) = 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( v 3 , u ) ≤ 4 \phi(v_{1},u)=6,\phi(v_{2},u)=5,\phi(v_{3},u)\leq 4  holds. In every case, the remaining vertices contradict the induction hypothesis, so the claim holds.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Using these lemmas, we obtain the following result. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.10 . 
 
 Assume that none of our reducible configurations in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} .
Any vertex  v v  of degree at least 12 has final charge less than  0 0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We fix the clockwise order of neighbors of  v v  in the embedding of  G ′ G^{\prime} . When we say the “next” neighbor, it means the next neighbor in this clockwise order.
When a neighbor of  v v  sends charge 6 to  v v , the next neighbor sends charge at most 4, or the next neighbor sends charge 5 and the next after next neighbor sends charge at most 4 by Lemma  4.8 . Hence, there is one neighbor that sends charge at most 4 when a neighbor of  v v  that sends a charge of  6 6  so the average amount of charges the neighbors of  v v  send to  v v  is at most  5 5 .
This implies that the final charge of  v v  is less than 0 ( T ⁡ ( v ) < 0 T(v)<0 ) if the degree of  v v  is at least 13 ( 10 ​ ( 6 − d ) + 5 ​ d = 60 − 5 ​ d < 0 10(6-d)+5d=60-5d<0 ). If the degree of  v v  is 12, the final charge can become exactly 0. We show the case when the degree of  v v  is exactly 12 in the rest of the proof. We denote  u i u_{i} ( 0 ≤ i < 12 0\leq i<12 ) as neighbors of  v v  such that  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  is the next neighbor of  u i u_{i}  ( 0 ≤ i < 12 0\leq i<12 ,  u i ​ ( i ≥ 12 ) u_{i}(i\geq 12)  represents  u i mod 12 u_{i\bmod 12} ). 
 
 
 
 (1)  
 
 If  ϕ ⁡ ( u i , v ) = ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 1 , v ) = 5 \phi(u_{i},v)=\phi(u_{i+1},v)=5  and the average amount of charges the neighbors of  v v  send to  v v  is 5, then for each  0 ≤ i < 12 0\leq i<12 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) = 5 \phi(u,v)=5  holds. 
 
 
 Suppose for a contradiction that a counterexample of (1) exists.
Let  i , j i,j  be two different indexes such that one of the followings holds: 
 
 •  
 
 i < j i<j ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u i − 1 , v ) ≠ 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( u j + 1 , v ) ≠ 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( u k , v ) = 5 \phi(u_{i-1},v)\neq 5,\phi(u_{j+1},v)\neq 5,\phi(u_{k},v)=5  ( i ≤ k ≤ j i\leq k\leq j ), or 
 
 •  
 
 i > j i>j ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u i − 1 , v ) ≠ 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( u j + 1 , v ) ≠ 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( u k , v ) = 5 \phi(u_{i-1},v)\neq 5,\phi(u_{j+1},v)\neq 5,\phi(u_{k},v)=5  ( i ≤ k < 12 i\leq k<12  or  0 ≤ k ≤ j 0\leq k\leq j ). 
 
 
 A counterexample of (1) has such indexes. We show only the first case (the proof of the second case is the same.). By Lemma  4.8 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( u i − 1 , v ) < 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( u j + 1 , v ) < 5 \phi(u_{i-1},v)<5,\phi(u_{j+1},v)<5 . By Lemma  4.9 , the vertices  v k v_{k}  ( j + 2 ≤ k < 12 j+2\leq k<12  or  0 ≤ k ≤ i − 2 0\leq k\leq i-2 ) send at most  5 ​ ( 9 − j + i ) + 1 5(9-j+i)+1  (average charge  5 5  plus one). Hence, the claim (1) holds. 
 
 
 
 (2)  
 
 If  ϕ ⁡ ( u i , v ) = 6 , ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 1 , v ) = 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 2 , v ) = 4 \phi(u_{i},v)=6,\phi(u_{i+1},v)=5,\phi(u_{i+2},v)=4 , then the average amount of charges the neighbors of  v v  send to  v v  is less than 5. 
 
 
 If  ϕ ⁡ ( u i , v ) = 6 , ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 1 , v ) = 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 2 , v ) = 4 \phi(u_{i},v)=6,\phi(u_{i+1},v)=5,\phi(u_{i+2},v)=4 , degree of  u i + 3 u_{i+3}  is 5 by Lemma  4.8  (Figure  13 ). The amount of charges a vertex of degree 5 sends is at most 5 by Lemma  4.7 . If  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 3 , v ) < 5 \phi(u_{i+3},v)<5 , claim (2) holds by (1).
Moreover, if  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 3 , v ) = 5 \phi(u_{i+3},v)=5  and  ϕ ⁡ ( v i + 4 , v ) < 5 \phi(v_{i+4},v)<5 , claim (2) holds by the same reason. By claim (1), neither  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 3 , v ) = 5 \phi(u_{i+3},v)=5  nor  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 4 , v ) = 5 \phi(u_{i+4},v)=5 . Hence the only possible case that contradicts claim (2) is  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 3 , v ) = 5 \phi(u_{i+3},v)=5  and  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 4 , v ) = 6 \phi(u_{i+4},v)=6 .
However, C(17) weakly appears by considering  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 2 , v ) = 4 , ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 3 , v ) = 5 , ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 4 , v ) = 6 \phi(u_{i+2},v)=4,\phi(u_{i+3},v)=5,\phi(u_{i+4},v)=6  and Lemma  4.8  (Figure  13 ). Hence claim (2) holds. 
 
 
 If the average amount of charges the neighbors of  v v  send to  v v  is exactly 5, by combining claims (1) and (2), one of the followings happens: (i)  ϕ ⁡ ( u i , v ) = 5 \phi(u_{i},v)=5  for each  0 ≤ i < 12 0\leq i<12 , or (ii)  ϕ ⁡ ( u i , v ) = 6 \phi(u_{i},v)=6  ( i mod 2 = 0 i\bmod 2=0 ),  ϕ ⁡ ( u j , v ) = 4 \phi(u_{j},v)=4  ( i mod 2 = 1 i\bmod 2=1 ). 
 
 
 First, we handle case (i).
We apply Lemma  4.7  (especially Figure  32 ).
The degree of  u i u_{i}  is 5 or 6 or 7. If the degree of  u i u_{i}  is 7 and the degree of  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  is 5 or 6, then  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 1 , v ) \phi(u_{i+1},v)  is not 5. None of degrees of  u i , u i + 1 u_{i},u_{i+1}  is 7. Hence, all degrees of  u i u_{i}  ( 0 ≤ i < 12 0\leq i<12 ) are 5 or 6. 
 
 
 Suppose all degrees of  u i u_{i}  are 6. It is possible when all  u i u_{i}  ( 0 ≤ i < 12 0\leq i<12 ) sends charges of 5 in send5(6, 1) since for any other cases described in Figure  32 , there exists a vertex of degree 5 that is adjacent to  v v . However, C(17) weakly appears in this case. Hence, the degree of some  u i u_{i}  is 5. 
 
 
 Suppose some  u i u_{i}  is in send5(1, 2). The degree of either  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  or  u i − 1 u_{i-1}  is 5. Assume without loss of generality that the degree of  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  is 5. Let  w , x , y w,x,y  be neighbors of  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  such that  v , u i , w , x , y v,u_{i},w,x,y  are listed in clockwise order of the embedding of  G ′ G^{\prime} . The degree of  w w  is 6. If the degree of  x x  is 5, C(2) weakly appears. If the degree of  x x  is 6 and the degree of  y y  is 5, C(17) weakly appears. Hence  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 1 , v ) \phi(u_{i+1},v)  cannot be 5. 
 
 
 Suppose some  u i u_{i}  is in send5(1, 3). Assume without loss of generality that both degrees of  u i + 1 , u i + 2 u_{i+1},u_{i+2}  are 5 and the degrees of neighbors of  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  except  v , u i , u i + 2 v,u_{i},u_{i+2}  are 6. By the condition of degrees,  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  must be in send5(1, 3). However, C(20) weakly appears in this case. 
 
 
 When some  u i u_{i}  is in send5(1, 1), degree of both  u i − 1 u_{i-1}  and  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  is 5. Hence, both  u i − 1 u_{i-1}  and  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  are in send5(1,1). Hence, all vertices  u i u_{i}  ( 0 ≤ i < 12 0\leq i<12 ) are in send5(1,1). However, C(28) weakly appears in this case. 
 
 
 Second, we handle case (ii).
The amount of charges a vertex of degree 5 sends is at most 5 by  4.7 , so the degree of  u i u_{i}  ( i mod 2 = 0 i\bmod 2=0 ) is not 5. We only consider the case that  u i u_{i}  ( i mod 2 = 0 i\bmod 2=0 ) is in one of the following cases: send6(1, 1), send6(1, 4), send6(1, 9) since for other cases, the degree of  u i − 2 u_{i-2}  or  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  is 5. 
 
 
 Suppose some  u i u_{i}  ( i mod 2 = 0 i\bmod 2=0 ) is in send6(1, 4). The degree of  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  or  u i − 1 u_{i-1}  is 6. Assume without loss of generality that the degree of  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  is 6, so  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  is also in send6(1, 4). In this case,  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 1 , v ) \phi(u_{i+1},v)  is at most 2. 
 
 
 Suppose some  u i u_{i}  ( i mod 2 = 0 i\bmod 2=0 ) is in send6(1, 9). The degree of  u i + 1 u_{i+1}  is 5. If  ϕ ⁡ ( u i + 1 , v ) \phi(u_{i+1},v)  is 4, the degree of  u i + 2 u_{i+2}  is 5 by Lemma  4.7  (Figure  33 ). In this case, C(1) weakly appears. 
 
 
 Hence all vertices  u i u_{i}  ( 0 ≤ i < 12 0\leq i<12 ,  i mod 2 = 0 i\bmod 2=0 ) are in send6(1, 1). However, C(29) weakly appears in this case.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 4.11 . 
 
 Let  H H  be an internally 6-connected triangulation in the plane (or the torus). Assume that none of our reducible configurations in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears  H H .

 Lemma 4.13 .

Let  u u  be a vertex in  H H  and  v , w , x v,w,x  be the neighbors of  u u  so that  v , w v,w  are adjacent and  w , x w,x  are adjacent. Assume that the degree of  v , w , x v,w,x  is at least  9 9 . 
 
 •  
 
 If the degree of  u u  is  5 5 , then  ϕ ⁡ ( u , v ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , w ) + ϕ ⁡ ( u , x ) ≤ 10 \phi(u,v)+\phi(u,w)+\phi(u,x)\leq 10 . If the equality holds, they are in Figure  17 .

 Lemma 4.14 . 
 
 Let  H H  be an internally 6-connected triangulation in the plane (or the torus). Assume that none of our reducible configurations in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  H H . 
 
 (a)  
 
 Let  u 1 , u 2 u_{1},u_{2}  be two adjacent vertices of degree at least 9 in  H H , and  v 1 , v 2 , … , v n v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}  be neighbors of  u 1 u_{1}  listed in the clockwise order of  H H  such that  v 1 ​ u 1 ​ u 2 v_{1}u_{1}u_{2}  constitutes a facial triangle. See Figure  19 . Then, 
 
 •  
 
 If the degree of  v 1 v_{1}  is 5,  ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 2 ) + ∑ i = 1 n ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u 1 ) ≤ 5 ​ n + 3 \phi(v_{1},u_{2})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(v_{i},u_{1})\leq 5n+3 . 
 
 •  
 
 If the degree of  v 1 v_{1}  is at least  6 6 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 2 ) + ∑ i = 1 n ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u 1 ) ≤ 5 ​ n \phi(v_{1},u_{2})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(v_{i},u_{1})\leq 5n . 
 
 
 
 (b)  
 
 Let  u 1 , u 2 , u 3 u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}  be vertices of degree at least 9 in  H H , and  v 1 , v 2 , … , v n v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}  be neighbors of  u 1 u_{1}  listed in the clockwise order of  H H  such that both  v 1 ​ u 1 ​ u 2 , v 1 ​ u 2 ​ u 3 v_{1}u_{1}u_{2},v_{1}u_{2}u_{3}  are facial triangles. See Figure  19 . Then,  ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 2 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 3 ) + ∑ i = 1 n ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u 1 ) ≤ 5 ​ n + 6 \phi(v_{1},u_{2})+\phi(v_{1},u_{3})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(v_{i},u_{1})\leq 5n+6 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First, we prove (a). 
 
 
 By Lemma  4.12 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 2 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 1 ) = 8 \phi(v_{1},u_{2})+\phi(v_{1},u_{1})=8  in the case described in Figure  17 . If  ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u 1 ) \phi(v_{2},u_{1})  is at most 4, the claim holds since  ∑ i = 3 n ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u 1 ) ≤ 5 ​ n − 9 \sum_{i=3}^{n}\phi(v_{i},u_{1})\leq 5n-9  by Lemma  4.9 . Hence, we assume
 ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 2 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 1 ) = 8 \phi(v_{1},u_{2})+\phi(v_{1},u_{1})=8  and  ϕ ⁡ ( v 2 , u 1 ) ≥ 5 \phi(v_{2},u_{1})\geq 5 . Then  v 2 v_{2}  sends charge 5 in either of send5(1,1) or send5(1,2) or send5(1,3). In every case, C(2), or C(17) weakly appears. 
 
 
 Second, we prove (b). By Lemma  4.13 ,  ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 1 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 2 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( v 1 , u 3 ) ≤ 10 \phi(v_{1},u_{1})+\phi(v_{1},u_{2})+\phi(v_{1},u_{3})\leq 10 . By Lemma  4.9   ∑ i = 2 n ϕ ⁡ ( v i , u 1 ) ≤ 5 ​ n − 4 \sum_{i=2}^{n}\phi(v_{i},u_{1})\leq 5n-4 . The statement holds by combining these two formulas.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 5  Looking at a disk bounded by a short cycle 

 
 In this section, we show Lemma  5.1 , which states that a reducible configuration appears strictly inside the disk bounded by a cycle with some specified conditions.
This lemma is necessary to handle 6,7-edge-cuts in Section  6 . The same statement of this lemma is proved in  [ 16 ] . The configuration set and rule set used in  [ 16 ]  are different from our sets  𝒦 , ℛ \mathcal{K},\mathcal{R} , but we show that the same statement holds for our setting too. The key to proving this lemma is Lemma  5.2 . In  [ 16 ] , Lemma  5.2  implied Lemma  5.1  by combining discharging method to ensure the appearance of configuration if a positively charged vertex exists.
Hence, to prove Lemma  5.1  in our setting  𝒦 , ℛ \mathcal{K},\mathcal{R} , we only have to check the hypothesis of Lemma  5.2 , especially 3.1 and 3.2 below.
In fact, we already checked these two statements in Lemma  4.14 , so Lemma  5.1  holds for our sets  𝒦 , ℛ \mathcal{K},\mathcal{R}  too. 
 
 
 It turns out that Lemma  5.1  is quite useful for other cases, including the planar, the doublecross, the apex case, and the projective planar cases. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.1 . 
 
 Let  T T  be an internally 6-connected near-triangulation in the plane, which is a subgraph of  G ′ G^{\prime} . Let  C C  be an induced cycle in  T T , which bounds the infinite region of  T T . Assume that there is no vertex in  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  which is adjacent to at least four consecutive vertices of  C C  and there are more than  18 5 ⋅ | C | − 12 \frac{18}{5}\cdot|C|-12  edges in  T T  between  C C  and  T − C T-C . Then a reducible configuration in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  appears in  T T . 
 
 
 
 Lemma 5.2 . 
 
 Let  T T  be an internally 6-connected near-triangulation in the plane and let  C C  be an induced cycle in  T T , which bounds the infinite region of  T T . Let  ℛ , 𝒦 \mathcal{R},\mathcal{K}  be a set of rules and a set of reducible configurations respectively. Suppose that no configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  appears in  T T . 
 
 
 Assume that 
 
 1.  
 
 there is no vertex in  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  which is adjacent to at least four consecutive vertices of  C C ,

 Then, there is a vertex in  V ⁡ ( T − C ) V(T-C)  that ends up with a final charge positive. 
 
 
 
 In this section, we extend Lemma  5.1  to the special triangulation. 
 
 
 Let  T T  be a triangulation in the plane, and let  e = a ​ b , e ′ = a ′ ​ b ′ e=ab,e^{\prime}=a^{\prime}b^{\prime}  to be two edges in  T T , and assume that we add two multiple edges  e 1 = a ​ b , e 1 ′ = a ′ ​ b ′ e_{1}=ab,e^{\prime}_{1}=a^{\prime}b^{\prime}  (thus there are now exactly  3 ​ n − 4 3n-4  edges). We say that  T T  is  nearly internally 6-connected , if 
 
 •  
 
 for any contractible cycle  C C  of order at most five such that one open disk  Δ \Delta  bounded by  C C  contains none of  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime} ,  Δ \Delta  contains no vertex or  Δ \Delta  consists of one vertex and  | V ⁡ ( C ) | = 5 |V(C)|=5 , and 
 
 •  
 
 there is no contractible cycle  C C  of order at most three such that both sides divided by  C C  contain at least one of  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 
 The vertices  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}  in  T T  are incident to multiple edges. The degree of a vertex  v v  of  T T  is calculated by the number of edges that are incident to  v v , not by the number of adjacent vertices. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.3 . 
 
 Let  T T  be a nearly internally 6-connected triangulation.
Let  c , d c,d ( c ′ , d ′ c^{\prime},d^{\prime} , resp.) be the vertices of  T T  such that the facial triangles  a ​ b ​ c abc ,  a ​ b ​ d abd ( a ′ ​ b ′ ​ c ′ a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime} ,  a ′ ​ b ′ ​ d ′ a^{\prime}b^{\prime}d^{\prime} , resp.) exist respectively.(since  T T  is nearly internally 6-connected, the number of such vertices for each edge  a ​ b ab ,  a ′ ​ b ′ a^{\prime}b^{\prime}  is exactly two).
Let  k k  be the number of vertices in  v ∈ { c , d , c ′ , d ′ } v\in\{c,d,c^{\prime},d^{\prime}\}  such that the degree of  v v  is more than 5.
If one of the following holds, there is a reducible configuration in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  that weakly appears in  T T  and that contains neither  e , e 1 e,e_{1}  nor  e ′ , e 1 ′ e^{\prime},e^{\prime}_{1} . 
 
 •  
 
 k = 4 k=4 , or 
 
 •  
 
 k ≤ 3 k\leq 3  and  d ⁡ ( a ) + d ⁡ ( b ) + d ⁡ ( a ′ ) + d ⁡ ( b ′ ) > ⌊ ( 112 − 3 ​ k ) / 5 ⌋ d(a)+d(b)+d(a^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime})>\lfloor(112-3k)/5\rfloor . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Note that the nearly internally 6-connectivity of  T T  implies that all degrees of  T T  including  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}  are at least 5. We prove Lemma  5.3  by using the following claims. 
 
 
 Claim 5.4 . 
 
 

 
 
 ∑ v ∈ V ⁡ ( T ) ∖ { a , b , a ′ , b ′ } 10 ⋅ ( 6 − d ⁡ ( v ) ) = 10 ⋅ ( d ⁡ ( a ) + d ⁡ ( b ) + d ⁡ ( a ′ ) + d ⁡ ( b ′ ) ) − 160 . \sum_{v\in V(T)\setminus\{a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}}10\cdot(6-d(v))=10\cdot(d(a)+d(b)+d(a^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime}))-160.

 | V ⁡ ( T ) | − | E ⁡ ( T ) | + | F ⁡ ( T ) | = 2 |V(T)|-|E(T)|+|F(T)|=2  holds by Euler formula. Hence, this claim holds.

 Claim 5.5 . 
 
 The total amount of charges sent from  T − { a , b , a ′ , b ′ } T-\{a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}  to  { a , b , a ′ , b ′ } \{a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}  by rules in  ℛ \mathcal{R}  is at most  5 ⋅ ( d ⁡ ( a ) + d ⁡ ( b ) + d ⁡ ( a ′ ) + d ⁡ ( b ′ ) ) − 48 − 3 ​ k 5\cdot(d(a)+d(b)+d(a^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime}))-48-3k .

 Claim 5.6 . 
 
 The total amount of final charges accumulated over all vertices  v ∈ V ⁡ ( T ) v\in V(T) , except  { a , b , a ′ , b ′ } \{a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\} , is at least  5 ⋅ ( d ⁡ ( a ) + d ⁡ ( b ) + d ⁡ ( a ′ ) + d ⁡ ( b ′ ) ) − 112 + 3 ​ k 5\cdot(d(a)+d(b)+d(a^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime}))-112+3k . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By Claims  5.4  and  5.5 ,  { 10 ⋅ ( d ⁡ ( a ) + d ⁡ ( b ) + d ⁡ ( a ′ ) + d ⁡ ( b ′ ) ) − 160 } − { 5 ⋅ ( d ⁡ ( a ) + d ⁡ ( b ) + d ⁡ ( a ′ ) + d ⁡ ( b ′ ) ) − 48 − 3 ​ k } = 5 ⋅ ( d ⁡ ( a ) + d ⁡ ( b ) + d ⁡ ( a ′ ) + d ⁡ ( b ′ ) ) − 112 + 3 ​ k \{10\cdot(d(a)+d(b)+d(a^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime}))-160\}-\{5\cdot(d(a)+d(b)+d(a^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime}))-48-3k\}=5\cdot(d(a)+d(b)+d(a^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime}))-112+3k .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of Lemma  5.3 . 
 
 By Claim  5.6 , if  k ≤ 3 k\leq 3  and  d ⁡ ( a ) + d ⁡ ( b ) + d ⁡ ( a ′ ) + d ⁡ ( b ′ ) > ⌊ ( 112 − 3 ​ k ) / 5 ⌋ d(a)+d(b)+d(a^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime})>\lfloor(112-3k)/5\rfloor , the total amount of final charges accumulated over all vertices  V ⁡ ( T ) V(T) , except  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime} , is positive. 
 
 
 If  k = 4 k=4 , the total amount of final charges accumulated over all vertices  V ⁡ ( T ) V(T) , except  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime} , is at least zero but can be exactly zero. This happens only when  d ⁡ ( a ) + d ⁡ ( b ) + d ⁡ ( a ′ ) + d ⁡ ( b ′ ) = 20 d(a)+d(b)+d(a^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime})=20 , so degree of  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}  is all 5. Let  e e  denote the neighbor of  a a  other than  b , c , d b,c,d . The amount  ϕ ⁡ ( c , a ) + ϕ ⁡ ( c , b ) + ϕ ⁡ ( e , a ) \phi(c,a)+\phi(c,b)+\phi(e,a)  must be 10 by claim  5.5 . Since the degree of  c c  is at least  6 6 , this is possible only when  ϕ ⁡ ( c , a ) + ϕ ⁡ ( c , b ) = 4 \phi(c,a)+\phi(c,b)=4  and  ϕ ⁡ ( e , a ) = 6 \phi(e,a)=6  by Lemma  4.12 . However, either of the degree of  c , d c,d  is 5 when  ϕ ⁡ ( e , a ) = 6 \phi(e,a)=6  by Lemma  4.7  (Figure  31 ). This contradicts the formula  k = 4 k=4 . Hence, the total amount of final charges accumulated over all vertices  V ⁡ ( T ) V(T) , except  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime} , does not become exactly zero. 
 
 
 Therefore, in both cases, at least one vertex of  V ⁡ ( T ) ∖ { a , b , a ′ , b ′ } V(T)\setminus\{a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}  has the final positive charge. Before moving charges, the degrees of  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}  are increased to at least 13, so no configuration of  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  contains a vertex in  a , b , a ′ , b ′ a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime} . Hence, a reducible configuration in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  T T  and it contains neither  e e  nor  e ′ e^{\prime} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 5.1  When representativity  = 1 =1 

 
 In this subsection, we assume that the representativity of the embedding of  G G  in the torus is exactly one. The purpose of this subsection is to apply Lemma  5.3  to avoid a loop in the discharging and find a reducible configuration that does not “wrap around” a non-contractible loop.
Let  e = u ​ v e=uv  be an edge of  G G  such that  G − e G-e  is planar. Such an edge  e e  is unique since two different such edges constitute a 2-edge cut that contradicts Lemma  2.5 . We embed  G − e G-e  in the plane so that the local rotation of incident edges of each vertex is the same as that of the original embedding of  G G  in the torus. Since  G − e G-e  is planar, such an embedding exists. Let  T T  be the dual graph of  G − e G-e  in the plane. The degree of vertices  u , v u,v  of  G − e G-e  is 2, and the degrees of other vertices are 3, so  T T  has two multiple edges, and all faces except two faces bounded by two multiple edges are triangles. Also,  T T  is nearly internally 6-connected by Lemmas  2.5  and  2.7 ,  2.8 .
By deleting  e e  from  G G , the toroidal embedding of  G − e G-e  has one face that is homomorphic to the annulus. The face is bounded by two paths. Each of the two paths becomes a facial cycle in the embedding of  G − e G-e  in the plane. Let  a , a ′ a,a^{\prime}  be the two vertices of  T T  that correspond to these two facial cycles. The vertices  a , a ′ a,a^{\prime}  incident with multiple edges. Let  b , b ′ b,b^{\prime}  be the two vertices of  T T  such that each of  a ​ b ab ,  a ′ ​ b ′ a^{\prime}b^{\prime}  is a multiple edge. We use the symbols  c , d , c ′ , d ′ c,d,c^{\prime},d^{\prime}  to denote the same vertices of Lemma  5.3 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 21 :  The reducible annular islands used in the proof of Lemma  5.7 . 
 
 
 To prove Lemma  5.7 , we use the fact that all the annular islands described in Figure  21  are reducible. The bold edges depicted in the figure represent the edges to be deleted to become C-reducible. The size of edges deleted for all islands is exactly one, so we do not have to worry about the resulting graph (after deletion) having a bridge or becoming Petersen-like. We denote the annular islands in Figure  21  by A( i i ) ( 1 ≤ i ≤ 5 1\leq i\leq 5 ) in the natural order. 
 
 
 Lemma 5.7 . 
 
 If the representativity of the embedding of  G G  in the torus is exactly one, either one of the annular islands described in Figure  21  appears, or a reducible configuration  K K  in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime}  (which is the dual of  G G ) in such a way that  K K  does not contain a non-contractible loop in  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We divide the case analysis depending on  d T ​ ( a ) , d T ​ ( a ′ ) d_{T}(a),d_{T}(a^{\prime}) . By Lemma  2.8 ,  d T ​ ( a ) , d T ​ ( a ′ ) ≥ 5 d_{T}(a),d_{T}(a^{\prime})\geq 5 . By Lemma  2.5 ,  d T ​ ( b ) , d T ​ ( b ′ ) ≥ 5 d_{T}(b),d_{T}(b^{\prime})\geq 5 , so if  d T ​ ( a ) + d T ​ ( a ′ ) > 12 d_{T}(a)+d_{T}(a^{\prime})>12 ,  d T ​ ( a ) + d T ​ ( a ′ ) + d T ​ ( b ) + d T ​ ( b ′ ) > 22 d_{T}(a)+d_{T}(a^{\prime})+d_{T}(b)+d_{T}(b^{\prime})>22 , so a reducible configuration appears by Lemma  5.3 . We handle the remaining cases. 
 
 
 
 (1)  
 
 d T ​ ( a ) = 5 , d T ​ ( a ′ ) = 5 d_{T}(a)=5,d_{T}(a^{\prime})=5 
 
 
 If  d T ​ ( b ) + d T ​ ( b ′ ) > 12 d_{T}(b)+d_{T}(b^{\prime})>12 ,  d T ​ ( a ) + d T ​ ( a ′ ) + d T ​ ( b ) + d T ​ ( b ′ ) > 22 d_{T}(a)+d_{T}(a^{\prime})+d_{T}(b)+d_{T}(b^{\prime})>22 , so we can apply Lemma  5.3 .
If  d T ​ ( b ) = 5 d_{T}(b)=5  and  d T ​ ( b ′ ) = 7 d_{T}(b^{\prime})=7 , the degrees of  c , d c,d  are more than 5 since A(1) is reducible. Hence, we can apply Lemma  5.3  for  k = 2 k=2 .
If  d T ​ ( b ) ≤ 6 d_{T}(b)\leq 6  and  d T ​ ( b ′ ) ≤ 6 d_{T}(b^{\prime})\leq 6 , the degrees of  c , d , c ′ , d ′ c,d,c^{\prime},d^{\prime}  are more than 5 since A(1), A(2) are reducible. Hence, we can apply Lemma  5.3  for  k = 4 k=4 . 
 
 
 
 (2)  
 
 d T ​ ( a ) = 5 , d T ​ ( a ′ ) = 6 d_{T}(a)=5,d_{T}(a^{\prime})=6  (or  d T ​ ( a ) = 6 , d T ​ ( a ′ ) = 5 d_{T}(a)=6,d_{T}(a^{\prime})=5 ) 
 
 
 If  d T ​ ( b ) + d T ​ ( b ′ ) > 11 d_{T}(b)+d_{T}(b^{\prime})>11 ,  d T ​ ( a ) + d T ​ ( a ′ ) + d T ​ ( b ) + d T ​ ( b ′ ) > 22 d_{T}(a)+d_{T}(a^{\prime})+d_{T}(b)+d_{T}(b^{\prime})>22 , so we can apply Lemma  5.3 .
If  d T ​ ( b ) = 5 , d T ​ ( b ′ ) = 6 d_{T}(b)=5,d_{T}(b^{\prime})=6 , the degrees of  c , d c,d  are more than 5 since A(3), A(4) are reducible. Hence we can apply Lemma  5.3  for  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 . If  d T ​ ( b ) = 6 , d T ​ ( b ′ ) = 5 d_{T}(b)=6,d_{T}(b^{\prime})=5 , we can prove it in the same way.
If  d T ​ ( b ) = 5 , d T ​ ( b ′ ) = 5 d_{T}(b)=5,d_{T}(b^{\prime})=5 , the degrees of  c , d , c ′ , d ′ c,d,c^{\prime},d^{\prime}  are more than 5 since A(3), A(4) are reducible. Hence we can apply Lemma  5.3  for  k = 4 k=4 .

 6  Cuts of size 6 or 7 

 
 Let us remind the reader that  G G  is a minimal counterexample and  G ′ G^{\prime}  is the dual of the embedding of  G G  in torus. In this section, we will show that one side of  G G  divided by a 6-edge-cut or a 7-edge-cut is relatively small. By Lemma  2.5 , we know that one side of  G G  divided by a cut of order at most five is small.
Let us observe that edges in a cut of  G G  correspond to a circuit 21 21 
             21 
            
            
            
          A circuit is a closed walk, allowing some vertex to be used twice or more. A cycle is a closed walk but does not allow each vertex to be used twice or more. We use ”circuit” here to handle the case that a subwalk of  C C  may be a noncontractible cycle.   C C  in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Throughout this section, we shall consider  G ′ G^{\prime} . The goal of this section is to prove Lemma  6.2  that roughly says that the disk bounded by a circuit of length six or seven contains at most three or four vertices strictly inside. 
 
 
 The proof of this lemma needs a lot of case analysis, unfortunately.
The problem is that when we find a C-reducible configuration  K K  inside the circuit  C C  in  G ′ G^{\prime} , after contractions in  K K , it is still possible that the resulting graph would end up with the dual of a Petersen-like graph after 2,3-cycle reductions 22 22 
             22 
            
            
            
          2,3-cycle reductions corresponds to 2,3-cut reductions in dual . There are a lot of cases we have to handle, so we do need some computer checks, see Section  6.2 . 
 
 
 To this end, we first handle C(1), which is a special case of Lemma  6.2 , because the ring of C(1) is of length exactly six, and C(1) consists of four vertices (which would violate Lemma  6.2  below). The configuration C(1) has two different contractions of size exactly six for C-reducibility as shown in Figure  22 . We prove that  G ′ G^{\prime}  would not result in the dual graph of any Petersen-like graph after one of the contractions in Lemma  6.1 .
The proof of the lemma is deferred to Appendix  E.1 .
We also handle C(27) separately. C(27) is used only when all degrees are 6 in Section  9 . Hence, we do not need to care about it here. 
 
 
 Let  C C  be a separating circuit of length  l = 6 , 7 l=6,7 . If  C C  bounds no disk (i.e.  C C  is not a cycle but  C C  consists of several contractible cycles), it is of no interest since one component of  G ′ − C G^{\prime}-C  has at most one vertex by Lemma  2.5 .
A separating circuit  C C  of length  l = 6 , 7 l=6,7  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  is  balanced  if there is a closed disk  D D  that is bounded by  C C  and both of  D − C D-C  and  G ′ − D G^{\prime}-D  contains at least  l − 2 l-2  vertices. 
 
 
 Figure 22 :  A free completion of  K K  with the ring, where  K K  is C(1). The bold lines represent the contraction edges used for C-reducibility. 
 
 
 Lemma 6.1 . 
 
 If C(1) appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} , then after some edge contraction and low-cycle reductions,  G ′ G^{\prime}  results in a graph in  𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0} . 
 
 
 
 Lemma 6.2 . 
 
 
 •  
 
 If there is a balanced separating circuit  C ′ C^{\prime}  in  G ′ G^{\prime} , there is a balanced separating circuit  C C  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  such that a configuration  K K  of  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  appears in a disk bounded by  C C  and the dual graph obtained from  G ′ G^{\prime}  by contracting edges of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  is in  𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0} . 
 
 •  
 
 If there is a separating circuit  C C  of length  l ∈ { 6 , 7 } l\in\{6,7\}  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  that bounds a disk  D ′ D^{\prime}  where  D ′ − C D^{\prime}-C  contains at most  l − 3 l-3  vertices. Then a disk  D D  bounded by  C C  is one of the graphs in Figure  23   ( l = 6 ) (l=6)  or Figure  24   ( l = 7 ) (l=7) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 23 :  The disks bounded by a separating circuit of length six. 
 
 
 Figure 24 :  The disks bounded by a separating circuit of length seven. 
 
 
 The first claim states that  G ′ G^{\prime}  has no balanced circuit.
The second claim states that we have the precise characterization of a closed disk  D D  bounded by  C C  if  D − C D-C  has at most  l − 3 l-3  vertices.
The second claim is easy to verify by enumerating the structure of graphs of at most  l − 3 l-3  vertices inside the disk.
Our proof of the first claim of Lemma  6.2  depends on the computer-check described in Section  6.1 ,  6.2 . 
 
 
 To prove the first claim of Lemma  6.2 , we choose  C C  as a separating circuit of length  l ∈ { 6 , 7 } l\in\{6,7\}  with the following condition. Let  D D  denote the closed disk bounded by  C C . 
 
 (1)  
 
 C C  is a balanced separating circuit. 
 
 (2)  
 
 D − C D-C  contains the minimum number of vertices under the condition (1) (If there are two or more minimizers, we preferentially choose one with  l = 6 l=6 .). 
 
 (3)  
 
 If a chosen circuit  c 0 ​ c 1 ​ c 2 ​ c 3 ​ c 4 ​ c 5 ​ c 6 c_{0}c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}c_{4}c_{5}c_{6}  by the condition (1), (2) has a chord  c 0 ​ c 2 c_{0}c_{2}  outside the disk bounded by the circuit and a facial triangle  c 0 ​ c 1 ​ c 2 c_{0}c_{1}c_{2}  exists, then we choose a circuit  c 0 ​ c 2 ​ c 3 ​ c 4 ​ c 5 ​ c 6 c_{0}c_{2}c_{3}c_{4}c_{5}c_{6}  as  C C . 
 
 (4)  
 
 If a chosen circuit  c 0 ​ c 1 ​ c 2 ​ c 3 ​ c 4 ​ c 5 ​ c 6 c_{0}c_{1}c_{2}c_{3}c_{4}c_{5}c_{6}  by the condition (1), (2), (3) has a path  c 0 ​ v ​ c 3 c_{0}vc_{3}  such that  v v  is outside the disk bounded by the circuit and facial triangles  c 0 ​ c 1 ​ v , c 1 ​ c 2 ​ v , c 2 ​ c 3 ​ v c_{0}c_{1}v,c_{1}c_{2}v,c_{2}c_{3}v  exist, then we choose a circuit  c 0 ​ v ​ c 3 ​ c 4 ​ c 5 ​ c 6 c_{0}vc_{3}c_{4}c_{5}c_{6}  as  C C . 
 
 
 Obviously, a chosen circuit by conditions (1), (2) is balanced.
Also, if a circuit chosen by conditions (1), (2) is balanced, the same applies for a circuit chosen by adding conditions (3), (4).
In the following, we show  C C  is the desired balanced separating circuit of the first claim of Lemma  6.2 . 
 
 
 By the condition (2) of  C C , a circuit  C ′ ( ≠ C ) C^{\prime}(\neq C)  in  D D  of length  k ∈ { 6 , 7 } k\in\{6,7\}  that bounds the disk that contains at least  k − 2 k-2  vertices strictly inside contradicts the definition of  C C  in most cases. An exceptional case is a circuit of length  k = 7 k=7  that shares five or four consecutive edges with  C C  and  l = 6 l=6 . It does not contradict the definition of  C C  due to the conditions (3), (4) and because this circuit may not be balanced. We explain why this circuit may not be balanced.
If  l = 6 l=6 , then exactly four vertices may be in  G ′ − D G^{\prime}-D , but there must be five vertices outside a disk bounded by a balanced circuit of length seven, so a circuit of length  k = 7 k=7  that shares five edges with  C C  may not be balanced.
If  l = 6 l=6  and the number of vertices in  C C  is at most five (a vertex appears twice in  C C ), then  C C  is not a cycle, and there exists a subwalk of  C C  that is a noncontractible cycle.
If the number of vertices in  C C  is five,  C C  passes through one vertex twice, so a circuit of length  k = 7 k=7  that shares four edges with  C C  may not be balanced.
If the number of vertices in  C C  is at most four, the region obtained by deleting  D D  consists of (i) one disk, or (ii) two or three regions (each region is a disk or an annulus). In the first case, if  G ′ − D G^{\prime}-D  has at most four vertices,  D − C D-C  has also at most four vertices by the condition (2), so  G ′ G^{\prime}  has at most twelve vertices. It is not a minimal counterexample by the result of  [ 6 ] . In the second case, it contradicts  G ′ − D G^{\prime}-D  has at least four vertices by Lemma  2.5 ,  2.7 ,  2.8 .  23 23 
             23 
            
            
            
          A (1,4)-annulus-cut is possible but edges of  C C  do not constitute it when  l = 6 l=6 . . Hence, a circuit of length  k = 7 k=7  that shares at most three edges with  C C  can contradict the minimality of  C C . 
 
 
 We assume that a path  P P  that connects two vertices  u i , u j ​ ( i ≠ j ) u_{i},u_{j}(i\neq j)  of  C C  outside  D D  exists. The symbol  E E  represents  P + u i ​ u i + 1 + u i + 1 ​ u i + 2 + … + u j − 1 ​ u j P+u_{i}u_{i+1}+u_{i+1}u_{i+2}+...+u_{j-1}u_{j}  when  i < j i<j , otherwise  P + u i ​ u i + 1 + … + u l − 1 ​ u 0 + … + u j − 1 ​ u j P+u_{i}u_{i+1}+...+u_{l-1}u_{0}+...+u_{j-1}u_{j} . If  E E  bounds a disk that is disjoint from  D D ,  P P  is called  a contractibly connected  u i ​ u j u_{i}u_{j} -path of  C C . Note that a contractible connected  u 0 ​ u 2 u_{0}u_{2} -path and  u 2 ​ u 0 u_{2}u_{0} -path are different since  E E  is made from  u 0 ​ u 1 + u 1 ​ u 2 u_{0}u_{1}+u_{1}u_{2}  in the former case, while  E E  is made from  u 2 ​ u 3 + u 3 ​ u 4 + … + u l − 1 ​ u 0 u_{2}u_{3}+u_{3}u_{4}+...+u_{l-1}u_{0}  in the latter case.
We call a contractibly connected  u i ​ u j u_{i}u_{j} -path of  C C  whose length is 1  a contractibly connected  u i ​ u j u_{i}u_{j} -chord of  C C . 
 
 
 The condition (3) seems to be tricky, but the condition (3)
is important for the following claim. 
 
 
 Claim 6.3 . 
 
 A contractibly connected chord of  C C  does not exist. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 When  l = 7 l=7 ,  u i ​ u i + 1 ​ u i + 2 u_{i}u_{i+1}u_{i+2}  is not a facial triangle by the condition (3) of  C C , so a contractibly connected  u i ​ u i + 2 u_{i}u_{i+2} -chord does not exist. If other kinds of contractibly connected chords exist, it would constitute a cycle of length  k ∈ { 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 } k\in\{2,3,4,5\}  with  C C  that contradicts Lemma  2.5 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 The condition (4) is also useful for the following claim. 
 
 
 Claim 6.4 . 
 
 If a contractibly connected  u i ​ u j u_{i}u_{j} -path of length 2 of  C C  exists,  j − i ≤ 2 j-i\leq 2  or  ( j + l ) − i ≤ 2 (j+l)-i\leq 2  holds. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Suppose  l = 7 l=7 , and a contractibly connected path  u i ​ v ​ u i + 3 u_{i}vu_{i+3}  exists. Then, a contractibly connected chord of  C C  outside  D D  exists or there exists a vertex  v ′ v^{\prime}  outside  D D  ( v ′ v^{\prime}  can be the same as  v v ) such that a path  u i ​ v ′ ​ u i + 3 u_{i}v^{\prime}u_{i+3}  exists and facial triangles  c 0 ​ c 1 ​ v ′ , c 1 ​ c 2 ​ v ′ , c 2 ​ c 3 ​ v ′ c_{0}c_{1}v^{\prime},c_{1}c_{2}v^{\prime},c_{2}c_{3}v^{\prime}  exist by Lemma  2.5 . That contradicts Claim  6.3  or the condition (4) of  C C . If other kinds of contractibly connected path of length  2 2  that contradict this claim exist, it contradicts Lemma  2.5 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Claims  6.3 ,  6.4  are important in enumerating all possibilities of 2,3-cycles that contain vertices outside  D D  after contraction. 
 
 
 To use Lemma  5.1 , we have to handle a few cases when  C C  is in a particular situation: when  C C  has a chord in  D D , or a vertex strictly inside  D D  is adjacent to at least four consecutive vertices of  C C , or at most  18 5 ​ l − 12 \frac{18}{5}l-12  edges exist between  C C  and vertices in  D − C D-C . In these cases, we find a circuit of length at most six in  D D , which enables us to get a characterization of  D D  by Lemma  2.5  or the almost-minimality of  C C  or find a reducible configuration strictly inside  D D . The characterization found here is enumerated in Figure  23  ( l = 6 l=6 ),  24  ( l = 7 l=7 ), which viloates  D − C D-C  has at least  l − 2 l-2  vertices ( C C  is balanced).
Therefore, we find a reducible configuration strictly inside  D D  by Lemma  5.1 . In the rest of the section, we show that the dual of the resulting graph belongs to  𝒯 1 ∖ 𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}  after contracting  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  of a C-reducible configuration  K K  and 2,3-cycle reductions. More precisely, we have to show that the dual of the resulting graph has no bridge and is not a Petersen-like graph. 
 
 
 6.1  Bridge 

 
 A bridge corresponds to a loop in the dual graph, so we care about a loop in  G ′ G^{\prime}  after contraction. There are two types of loops in the toroidal graphs: contractible, noncontractible.
We handle a noncontractible loop and one particular type of contractible loop in Section
 8.1.1 ,  8.1.2 
(The checks in those sections are independent of the result of this section). In this section, we handle other kinds of contractible loops.
By computer check, we show the following claim. 
 
 
 Claim 6.5 . 
 
 For a C-reducible configuration  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K} , assume that  K K  appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} , and a contractibly connected chord of the ring of  K K  becomes a contractible loop after contracting edges of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) . Then, a contractible cycle of length  k ∈ { 6 , 7 } k\in\{6,7\}  with the following properties exists. 
 
 •  
 
 The cycle contains an edge in  G ′ G^{\prime}  that becomes a loop after contraction, and 
 
 •  
 
 the cycle separates  G ′ G^{\prime}  into two components, each of which has at least  k − 2 k-2  vertices. 
 
 •  
 
 If  k = 7 k=7 , at least four edges of the cycle are incident with a vertex of  K K . 
 
 
 
 
 
 The third condition is necessary to handle the almost-minimality of  C C  as explained above.
When a cycle described in Claim  6.5  is inside  D D , that contradicts the definition of  C C . Otherwise, a chord of  C C  outside  D D  exists since the edges contracted are strictly inside  D D . That contradicts Claim  6.3 .
In this discussion, we show that a contractible loop does not exist after contraction, so the following claim holds. 
 
 
 Claim 6.6 . 
 
 For every C-reducible configuration  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  that appears strictly inside  D D , after contracting c( K K ) in  G ′ G^{\prime} , no contractible loops occur. 
 
 
 
 By the same argument of Claim  6.6 , we can prove Lemma  3.22  in Section  3 , by using Lemma  6.2 . 
 
 
 
 6.2  Petersen-like graph 

 
 The graph obtained by a 2,3-edge-cut reduction that is caused by (2,1),(1,1)-annulus-cut never becomes a Petersen-like graph since such a cut ensures that the representativity of this graph is at most one. Hence, we only consider 2,3-edge-cut reductions that correspond to a contractible cycle of length 2,3 in the dual (2,3-cycle reductions).
Also, the representativity of  G , G ′ G,G^{\prime}  is at least two since a graph of the representativity one does not become Petersen-like.
We denote  H H  as the graph obtained from  G ′ G^{\prime}  by deleting vertices outside  D D  and splitting vertices of  C C  if identified (we need to split vertices if  C C  is not a cycle as explained at the beginning of this proof). The graph  H H  is a near triangulation in the plane such that  C C  bounds the outer face boundary.
Remind that  K K  is a configuration inside  D D  (also in  H H ), and  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  is a set of edges to be contracted for C-reducibility. 
 
 
 6.2.1  No 2,3-cycle reductions outside  D D 

 
 First, we consider the case that no two different vertices of  C C  are identified and no chord of  C C  exists in  H H  after contraction. In this case, no 2,3-cycle that contains a vertex outside  D D  appears after contractions since two non-consecutive vertices in  C C  have a distance at least 2 in  H / c ⁡ ( K ) H/c(K) , and no contractibly connected chord of  C C  outside  D D  exists by Claim  6.3 . Hence, our approach is to search a circuit  C ′ C^{\prime}  in the dual of the Petersen graph or graphs in the Blanuša snark family (e.g. Petersen graph, Blanuša, Blanuša-V1, Blanuša-H1 snark) that was originally  C C  before contractions. To do that, we search a circuit  C ′ C^{\prime}  of length  l ∈ { 6 , 7 } l\in\{6,7\}  that bounds a disk  D ′ D^{\prime}  with the following conditions in the dual of the Petersen graph or graphs in the Blanuša snark family. 
 
 (a)  
 
 No two vertices of  C ′ C^{\prime}  are identified and no chord of  C ′ C^{\prime}  exists in  D ′ D^{\prime} . 
 
 (b)  
 
 The number of vertices not in  D ′ D^{\prime}  is at least  l − 2 l-2 . 
 
 (c)  
 
 For every two noncontractible cycles of length two  v 0 ​ v 1 , v 2 ​ v 3 v_{0}v_{1},v_{2}v_{3}  ( i ≠ j → v i ≠ v j i\neq j\rightarrow v_{i}\neq v_{j} ) of the Petersen graph or graphs in the Blanuša snark family, both two annulus regions separated by these cycles share a common area with the region strictly inside  D ′ D^{\prime} . 
 
 
 The condition (a) is obvious in this case. The condition (b) comes from the definition of  C C . The condition (c) comes from Lemma  2.7 . The restriction  i ≠ j → v i ≠ v j i\neq j\rightarrow v_{i}\neq v_{j}  represents the cyclicity of a cut in Lemma  2.7 . The condition (c) is important in restricting infinite Petersen-like graphs to finite ones. More precisely, we do not need to care about Blanuša-H k k  ( k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 ), Blanuša-V l l  ( l ≥ 2 l\geq 2 ) since every circuit that satisfies the condition (c) in every embedding of these graphs is of length at least 8, so our targets are only the Petersen graph, Blanuša snark, and Blanuša-V1 snark. Luckily, almost all circuits in the dual of every embedding of these graphs do not satisfy all conditions (a), (b), (c). Only one circuit in these graphs satisfies all the conditions; it is the circuit of length 7 that surrounds the vertex of degree 7 in Blanuša-V1 snark (see Figure  6 ).
We handle the circuit by checking that all the configurations of  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  have at least two vertices or a vertex of degree not 7 after contraction and 2,3-cycle reductions by computer. Hence, we do not need to worry about getting such a circuit after contraction. Thus we have the following. 
 
 
 Claim 6.7 . 
 
 For every C-reducible configuration  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  except C(1), C(27), which appears strictly inside  D D , after contracting c( K K ) in  G ′ G^{\prime} , assume that every two non-consecutive vertices of  C C  are of distance at least  2 2  in  H / c ⁡ ( K ) H/c(K) . Then,  G ′ G^{\prime}  does not become a Petersen-like graph after contraction and 2,3-cycle reductions. 
 
 
 
 
 6.2.2  2,3-cycle reductions outside  D D 

 
 Figure 25 :  The 10 cases checked by computer.
In the figure,  u 0 u_{0}  is the vertex in the upper left. The remaining vertices  u 1 , u 2 , … u_{1},u_{2},...  start after  u 0 u_{0}  in the counter-clockwise order.
The bold line represents contraction edges of  K K .
The red line represents a set of edges that consist of a contractible cycle of length  4 4 ,  5 5 , or  6 6  that will not be erased after reductions. 
 
 
 Figure 26 :  The 15 cases checked by computer.
In the figure,  u 0 u_{0}  is the vertex in the upper left. The remaining vertices  u 1 , u 2 , … u_{1},u_{2},...  start after  u 0 u_{0}  in the counter-clockwise order.
The bold line represents contraction edges of  K K .
The red line represents a set of edges that consist of a contractible cycle of length  4 4 ,  5 5 , or  6 6  that will not be erased after reductions. 
 
 
 Second, we consider the other cases: the distance between two non-consecutive vertices of  C C  may be at most 1 in  H H  after contraction.
In this case, we check the number of vertices inside a circuit of specified length in  H H  after contraction and 2,3-cycle reductions, which is explained later. We have to enumerate contractible 2,3-cycles that get rid of vertices. Especially, cycles that can contain edges outside  D D  are dangerous. This is because such a cycle does not contradict the almost-minimality of  C C . There are two possibilities of appearing such a cycle after contractions. One consists of a path inside  D D  and a contractibly connected path of  C C  whose length is at most 3. The other consists of two paths inside  D D  and two homotopic noncontractibly connected paths of  C C , where a  noncontractibly connected path  of  C C  is a path  P P  outside  D D  that connects two vertices of  C C  such that  C + P C+P  contains some noncontractible cycle. Two noncontractibly connected paths  P 1 , P 2 P_{1},P_{2}  of  C C  are  homotopic  if two noncontractible cycles in  P 1 + C , P 2 + C P_{1}+C,P_{2}+C  are homotopic. The former kind of cycle appears in restricted situations by Claims  6.3  and  6.4  (See two cases (A), (B) below.). However, in these restricted situations, such a cycle still appears and we have no idea how to count vertices inside the cycle. Hence, we quit assessing the number of vertices in the region that can be bounded by such a cycle, but in other regions, we calculate it. To do so,
we enumerate all possible combinations of distances in  H / c ⁡ ( K ) H/c(K)  obtained by combining the following two cases (A), (B). 
 
 (A)  
 
 Two different nonconsecutive vertices in  C C  are identified (of distance 0 in  H / c ⁡ ( K ) H/c(K) ) after contraction, 
 
 (B)  
 
 Two vertices  u i , u i + 2 u_{i},u_{i+2}  in  C C  are of distance 1 in  H / c ⁡ ( K ) H/c(K) . 
 
 
 These two cases are important in tracking 2,3-cycles that contain vertices both inside and outside  D D . By Claims  6.3  and  6.4 , vertices described in (A), (B) combined with a contractibly connected path of length 2, or 3 are the only cases that can be a 2,3-cycle that contain vertices both inside and outside  D D  after contraction of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  by one contractibly connected path.
We enumerate all combinations of distances and check that these situations do not happen by the contraction of  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  needed for C-reducibility. This check is done by computer. All the cases are enumerated in Claim  6.9 : (6cut-1),…,(6cut-10),(7cut-1),….,(7cut-15). There are 10 cases for  l = 6 l=6 , and 15 cases for  l = 7 l=7 . The distance function  d ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) d(\cdot,\cdot)  is measured in  H / c ⁡ ( K ) H/c(K) . The corresponding figures are shown in Figures  25  and  26 .
Note that the red-colored edges are related to the evidence used to prove the resulting graph after contraction and 2.3-cycle reductions is not a Petersen-like graph. Indeed, a set of red-colored edges becomes a cycle of length 4, 5, or 6 after contraction. Also, the disk bounded by the cycle after contraction is not surrounded by 2,3-cycle after contraction by Lemma  2.5 , and Claims  6.3  and  6.4 , so the disk remains after 2,3-cycle reductions. Note that it is not a mistake that the cycle of length  4 4  in (7cut-9) after contraction is not red-colored since the disk bounded by the cycle can be removed by a contractibly connected  u 3 ​ u 0 u_{3}u_{0} -path of length 3.
No cycle of length 4, 5, or 6 of the dual of any embedding of the Petersen graph or graphs in the Blanuša snark family has the following properties (i), (ii), (iii).
The reader can check these properties by considering Figures  3 – 7 . 
 
 
 
 (i)  
 
 A circuit of length  4 4  that bounds a disk that contains at least 1 vertex strictly inside. 
 
 (ii)  
 
 A circuit of length  5 5  that bounds a disk that contains at least 2 vertices strictly inside. 
 
 (iii)  
 
 A circuit of length  6 6  that bounds a disk that contains at least 4 vertices strictly inside. 
 
 
 Hence, the number of vertices that remain after contraction inside the cycle that consists of red edges and several contracted edges can be a certificate of being non-Petersen-like graphs. 
 
 
 To do so, we calculate the number of vertices after contraction. If a cycle inside  D D  becomes a 2,3-cycle after contractions, it is possible that this cycle may originally contradict the minimality of  C C . We only consider cycles inside  D D  that do not contradict the minimality of  C C  and assume that vertices inside these cycles are erased.
We have to take care also about cycles that are not completely inside  D D .
As explained at the beginning of the section, a path inside  D D  and a contractibly connected path of  C C  can constitute a 2,3-cycle that is outside  D D , but we do not have to care about it since such a cycle does appear in the region where we count the number of vertices  24 24 
                 24 
                
                
                
              For example, when checking (6cut-1), you may wonder if the distance between  u 2 u_{2}  and  u 4 u_{4}  can be 1 after contractions and a contractibly connected  u 2 ​ u 4 u_{2}u_{4}  path of length 2 can become a 2,3-cycle that get rid of vertices inside the cycle that consists of red-colored edges and contracted edges. However, we checked this case in (6cut-5), so we can assume that no such cycle appears in (6cut-1).  (i.e. the region bounded by red edges and several contracted edges). 
 
 
 Also, we care about a cycle that consists of two paths inside  D D  and two homotopic noncontractibly connected paths of  C C . The length of two homotopic noncontractibly connected paths of  C C  must be short, namely at most 3, to become a 2,3-cycle after reductions. Using this, we check whether the existence of such paths contradicts the length of cycle  C C  (which can be 6, 7) or not.
We assume that vertices are deleted only by cycles that pass the check (i.e. that are compatible with the length of  C C .).
More detailed implementations of this check are explained in Appendix  C.1 . 
 
 
 We treat (7cut-12) as a special case. If we get a Petersen-like graph in (7cut-12), and a 6-cycle in (7cut-12) bounds a disk that consists of exactly 3 vertices strictly inside, all these 3 vertices have degree 5 by observing a 6-cycle in the dual of any embedding of the Petersen graph or graphs in Blanuša snark family.
When such a case happens, the cycle does not satisfy both conditions (b) and (c) in Section  6.2.1  (Here, we have to restrict that the number in the condition (b) is  l − 3 l-3  by considering the possibility of being removed vertices by a 3-cycle that contains vertices outside  D D ). Hence, our condition (iii) in (7cut-12) can become weaker than described above: the number  4 4  becomes  3 3 . We actually check under this condition. 
 
 
 The other criterion of this check is checking the almost-minimality of  C C . The criterion is the same as the one used in Section 7.1 in  [ 16 ] . We explain it in short to make this paper self-contained.
When some two vertices of  C C  become identified with each other after contractions of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) , these two vertices are in the ring of  K K .
When some vertices on  C C  are of distance 1 after contractions of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) , these two vertices are in the ring of  K K , or one is in the ring, and the other is adjacent to a vertex of the ring.
Hence, assuming one of (6cut-1),…,(7cut-15) happens, we can find a short contractibly connected path of the ring of  K K , which is part of  C C . This path would constitute a cycle with a path in  K K  that could contradict the almost-minimality of  C C .
Let us explain how to check the argument above, using a specific example. In fact, this example corresponds to (6cut-2).
Let us assume  l = 6 l=6  and  d ⁡ ( u 0 , u 2 ) = d ⁡ ( u 3 , u 5 ) = 0 d(u_{0},u_{2})=d(u_{3},u_{5})=0  after contraction of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) .
Note that these four vertices belong to a ring of  K K .
For each configuration  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K} , we enumerate four vertices  a , b , c , d a,b,c,d  in the ring such that  a , b , c , d a,b,c,d  are in the clockwise order listed in the ring of  K K , and  d ⁡ ( a , b ) = d ⁡ ( c , d ) = 0 d(a,b)=d(c,d)=0  after contracting edges of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) .
In this case, we have several constraints that two vertices of the ring of  K K  are connected by a path of specified length outside  K K  (e.g. a contractibly connected  a ​ b ab (,  b ​ a ba )-path of length 2(, 4) of the ring exists respectively). We check by computer whether or not all such paths constitute a cycle that contradicts the almost-minimality of  C C . 
 
 
 By computer-check described above, we ensure that almost all configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  except C(1), C(27) does not become a Petersen-like graph after contraction and reductions. However, there is one configuration in (7cut-9) that we cannot conclude immediately. We can handle it in Claim  6.8 . 
 
 
 Figure 27 :  
 
 
 Claim 6.8 . 
 
 The configuration shown in Figure  27 , (7cut-9) does not occur. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By computer check, (7cut-9) can occur when  C C  is the cycle described in Figure  27 . The bold lines represent contraction edges. We denote  C ′ C^{\prime}  as the cycle that consists of contraction edges and  u 0 ​ u 1 u_{0}u_{1} ,  u 1 ​ u 2 u_{1}u_{2} ,  u 2 ​ u 3 u_{2}u_{3} .
The vertex  u 1 u_{1}  is not identical to any vertex of  b , c , d b,c,d , since this leads to a cycle that contradicts Lemma  2.5 . The vertex  u 2 u_{2}  is not identical to any vertices of  a , b , c a,b,c  by the same reason. Hence,  C ′ C^{\prime}  bounds a disk where at least 4 vertices ( b , c b,c  and two vertices of degree  5 5  in this configuration) exist. That contradicts the minimality of  C C .
∎ 
 
 
 
 The above discussion and the result of computer-check imply the following claim. 
 
 
 Claim 6.9 .

 •  
 
 (6cut-1)  d ⁡ ( u 0 , u 2 ) = 0 d(u_{0},u_{2})=0 , 
 
 •

 (6cut-10)  d ⁡ ( u 0 , u 2 ) = 1 d(u_{0},u_{2})=1  and  d ⁡ ( u 2 , u 4 ) = 1 d(u_{2},u_{4})=1  and  d ⁡ ( u 4 , u 0 ) = 1 d(u_{4},u_{0})=1 , 
 
 •  
 
 (7cut-1)  d ⁡ ( u 0 , u 2 ) = 0 d(u_{0},u_{2})=0 , 
 
 •

 •

 •

 •

 The proof of Lemma  6.2  is obtained by applying Claims  6.6 ,  6.7  and  6.9 , and the result in Section

 7  Wrappings of Configurations 

 
 With the discharging algorithm described in Section  4 , we have confirmed that either a vertex  v ∈ G ′ v\in G^{\prime}  with the resulting charge  T ⁡ ( v ) > 0 T(v)>0  or one of its neighbors is contained in some weakly appearing (defined in Section  3 ) reducible configuration  K K .
If  K K  appears in  G ′ G^{\prime}  as well (as an induced subgraph), we can simply reduce  G ′ G^{\prime}  by contracting some arbitrary edge in  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K)  when  K K  is D-reducible, or the edges in  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  when  K K  is C-reducible.
However, if this is not the case, there is a wrapping of  K K  (say,  K ′ K^{\prime} ) such that  K ′ K^{\prime}  appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} , instead of  K K .
We confirm that for almost all the cases of  K ′ K^{\prime} ,  G ⁡ ( K ′ ) G(K^{\prime})  admits a representativity one curve (in the resulting graph), or  K ′ K^{\prime}  is reducible as well. 
 
 
 To be more precise, we perform a brute-force check on every configuration  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  to confirm that the following property holds. 
 
 
 Lemma 7.1 . 
 
 For every general configuration  K ′ K^{\prime}  such that  K ′ K^{\prime}  is a strict wrapping of  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  (i.e.  G ⁡ ( K ′ ) G(K^{\prime})  is not isomorphic to  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K) ),  K ′ K^{\prime}  is reducible with  c ⁡ ( K ′ ) ≤ 4 c(K^{\prime})\leq 4 .
Moreover, for all cases such that  3 ≤ c ⁡ ( K ′ ) ≤ 4 3\leq c(K^{\prime})\leq 4 , there is always a small cyclic cut in  G ∸ c ⁡ ( K ′ ) G\dotdiv c(K^{\prime})  which violates Lemma  2.7 . 
 
 
 
 We have implemented an algorithm that takes an input configuration  K K , and outputs a set of general configurations that are all possible wrappings of  K K .
This is done by taking two distinct vertices  u , v ∈ V ⁡ ( S ) u,v\in V(S)  and an edge  e u , e v ∈ E ⁡ ( S ) e_{u},e_{v}\in E(S)  such that  u ∈ e u , v ∈ e v u\in e_{u},v\in e_{v} , and trying to “identify” the two vertex/edge pairs  ( u , e u ) (u,e_{u})  and  ( v , e v ) (v,e_{v}) 
(Here,  S S  is the  free completion  of the configuration  K K ). Then we try to identify vertices as many as possible.
Formally, the identification process succeeds if the following condition is satisfied. 
 
 
 Definition 7.2 . 
 
 A  rooted path  on  S S  is a sequence of vertices  v 1 , v 2 , ⋯ , v k ∈ V ⁡ ( S ) v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{k}\in V(S)  (for  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 ) such that the path  P = v 1 v 2 ⋯ v k P=v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{k}  is on  S S .
We call  v 1 v_{1}  the  starting vertex ,  v 1 ​ v 2 v_{1}v_{2}  the  starting edge  (if it exists), and  v k v_{k}  the  ending vertex  of the rooted path.
The  positive/negative angle  on  v i v_{i}  ( 1 < i < k 1<i<k ) in a rooted path  P P  is the smallest  n n  such that  π v i ± n ​ ( v i − 1 ) = v i + 1 \pi_{v_{i}}^{\pm n}(v_{i-1})=v_{i+1} , when  π v i \pi_{v_{i}}  is the local rotation 25 25 
                 25 
                
                
                
               Here, we borrow the term  local rotation  from  [ 24 ]  but we slightly alter the definition so that it returns vertices instead of edges: A local rotation  π v \pi_{v}  is a cyclic permutation of the neighbors of  v v , such that if we fix a neighbor  u u  of  v v , the vertices  u , π v ​ ( u ) , π v ​ ( π v ​ ( u ) ) , ⋯ u,\pi_{v}(u),\pi_{v}(\pi_{v}(u)),\cdots  form a cyclic walk around  v v . Since the underlying graph is a triangulation, the three vertices  v , u , π v ​ ( u ) v,u,\pi_{v}(u)  must always construct a triangle.  on  v i v_{i}  and  π v i ± n \pi_{v_{i}}^{\pm n}  is the function  π v i \pi_{v_{i}}  or  π v i − 1 \pi_{v_{i}}^{-1}  applied  n n  times. 
 
 
 Two rooted paths  P P  and  Q Q  are  parallel  if the following conditions are satisfied. 
 
 •  
 
 P = ( v 1 , ⋯ , v k ) P=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{k})  and  Q = ( u 1 , ⋯ , u k ) Q=(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})  both have the same length. 
 
 •  
 
 For all  1 ≤ i < k 1\leq i<k , if  v i v_{i}  and  u i u_{i}  are both in  G ⁡ ( K ) G(K) ,  γ K ​ ( v i ) = γ K ​ ( u i ) \gamma_{K}(v_{i})=\gamma_{K}(u_{i}) 
 
 •  
 
 For all  1 < i < k 1<i<k , either the positive angle or negative angle on  v i v_{i}  on  P P  and  u i u_{i}  on  Q Q  is the same. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Claim 7.3 . 
 
 Let  K K  be a configuration and let  u ∈ V ⁡ ( K ) , v ∈ V ⁡ ( S ⁡ ( K ) ) u\in V(K),v\in V(S(K))  be two different vertices,  e u , e v e_{u},e_{v}  be two different edges incident to  u , v u,v , respectively, in  S ⁡ ( K ) S(K)  (The free completion of  K K ).
If for any parallel rooted paths  P P  and  Q Q  in  S ⁡ ( K ) S(K)  where the starting vertex is  u , v u,v  and the starting edge is  e u , e v e_{u},e_{v} , respectively, one of the following conditions holds, there exists a general configuration  K ′ K^{\prime}  such that  K ′ K^{\prime}  is a wrapping of  K K . 
 
 •  
 
 One of the ending vertices in  P , Q P,Q  is not in  V ⁡ ( K ) V(K) . 
 
 •  
 
 The degree (value of  γ K \gamma_{K} ) of the ending vertices of  P , Q P,Q  are equal. 
 
 
 This  K ′ K^{\prime}  can be constructed by “gluing” the vertices  u , v u,v  and the edges  e u , e v e_{u},e_{v}  so that any path between  u u  and  v v  becomes a non-contractible curve on the torus. 
 
 
 
 We iterate over all possible parallel rooted paths using DFS, and identify all corresponding vertex pairs that lie on each path.
If for some paths, the endpoints of them have different  γ K \gamma_{K}  values, we abort the identification process.
If not, we proceed to construct a wrapping  K ′ K^{\prime}  by identifying all pairs of vertices that we accumulated in the DFS process. 
 
 
 For more detailed implementations of this algorithm, refer to the pseudocode in Appendix  C.2 . 
 
 
 
 8  Configuration of contraction size  ≥ 3 \geq 3 

 
 The main result of this section is the following. 
 
 
 Lemma 8.1 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample, and let  K K  be a C-reducible configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}   weakly appearing  in  G G .
Then  G ∸ c ⁡ ( K ) G\dotdiv c(K)  is 2-edge connected and not Petersen-like. 
 
 
 
 We first observe that if  K ′ K^{\prime}  is a strict wrapping of  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K} , by Lemma  7.1 , we confirm  | c ⁡ ( K ′ ) | ≤ 2 |c(K^{\prime})|\leq 2 . 
 
 
 When the contraction size of a configuration  K K  is two or less, it is easy to prove this lemma.
Indeed, in this case, due to the high cyclic-edge-connectivity of  G G , a  2 / 3 2/3 -edge-cut reduction occurs in  G ∸ c ⁡ ( K ) G\dotdiv c(K)  if and only if  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  are members of an edge cut of size exactly 5.
Even in this case, the reduction will only reduce the order of  G G  by 4.
As mentioned in the introduction, we observe that  G G  is of order at least 38 by the result of  [ 6 ]  (that generates all the snarks of order at most 36. It turns out that only the snarks in  𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0}  can be embedded in the torus).
Therefore, the resulting graph is of order at least  34 34  but if the resulting graph is in  𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0} , this would yield a  ( 2 , 2 ) (2,2) -annulus-cut, a contradiction to Lemma  2.7 . 
 
 
 Therefore, we can assume that in Lemma  8.1 , we may assume that  K K   does indeed appear . Hereafter, we only consider this case. 
 
 
 However, when  | c ⁡ ( K ) | ≥ 3 |c(K)|\geq 3 , we would need to individually track the contraction pattern in every  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K} .
We have implemented an algorithm to track the possible degree count of every vertex after contraction and check if its neighboring structure contains a bridge or matches the structure of (dual of) Petersen-like graphs.
Upon checking, we also make use of the following fact, which is a direct corollary of Lemmas  2.5  and  6.2 . 
 
 
 Fact 8.2 . 
 
 Let a contractible  k k -cycle ( 5 ≤ k ≤ 7 5\leq k\leq 7 )  F F  be contained in the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime} .
Then, the size of the smaller connected component of  G ′ − F G^{\prime}-F  is less than or equal to  1 1  when  k = 5 k=5 ,  3 3  when  k = 6 k=6 , and  4 4  when  k = 7 k=7 . 
 
 
 
 8.1  Avoiding bridges 

 
 For  G ∸ c ⁡ ( K ) G\dotdiv c(K)  to contain a bridge, there must be some edge cut  F ⊂ E ⁡ ( G ) F\subset E(G)  such that  | F ∖ c ⁡ ( K ) | = 1 |F\setminus c(K)|=1 .
We classify possible cases by the type of cut  F F ; when  F F  is a disk cut or when  F F  is an annular cut. 
 
 
 8.1.1  Non contractible loop 

 
 Figure 28 :  The dark solid line represents the ring, the red lines represent the contraction edges and the blue edge becomes a loop after contraction. The left and right figure represents case (i), (ii) respectively. 
 
 
 Here, we care about a noncontractible loop in the torus, after contraction.
Let  K K  be a configuration and  S S  be a free completion of  K K  with its ring. A noncontractilbe loop exists if two pairs of vertices  ( r p 1 , r q 1 ) , ( r p 2 , r q 2 ) (r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1}),(r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2})  in the ring of  K K  satisfy either
(i)  d S / c ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( r p 1 , r q 1 ) = 0 , d S / c ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( r p 2 , r q 2 ) = 0 d_{S/c(K)}(r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1})=0,d_{S/c(K)}(r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2})=0 , or
(ii)  d S / c ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( r p 1 , r q 1 ) = 0 , d S / c ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( r p 2 , r q 2 ) = 1 d_{S/c(K)}(r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1})=0,d_{S/c(K)}(r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2})=1 .
If a loop exists after contraction,
 r p 1 r_{p}^{1}  is identical to  r q 1 r_{q}^{1}  and the distance between  r p 2 r_{p}^{2}  and  r q 2 r_{q}^{2}  is 1 (or swapping the roles of ( r p 1 , r q 1 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1} ) and ( r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2} )) in the case (i), or
 r p 1 r_{p}^{1}  is identical to  r q 1 r_{q}^{1}  and  r p 2 r_{p}^{2}  is identical to  r q 2 r_{q}^{2}  in the case (ii) (See Figure  28 ).
If the contraction size is at most 3, a noncontractible loop does not exist. It is because case (i) is impossible and case (ii) is possible only when  d S ​ ( r p 1 , r q 1 ) = 2 d_{S}(r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1})=2  and  d S ​ ( r p 2 , r q 2 ) = 2 d_{S}(r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2})=2 , but this would yield a cut that contradicts Lemma  2.5  or Lemma  2.7 .
For all  K K  of our set  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , we check whether the above condition can be satisfied after contracting  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) .
If it can be satisfied, we re-check to see whether the annular configuration is reducible in this case. To simplify the implementation, we create an annular configuration from  K K  by only identifying one pair of vertices of the ring of  K K .
Almost all configurations obtained are reducible with contraction size at most 3.
However, 6 cases were not concluded immediately. We re-checked the annular configuration obtained when a loop appears (i.e., two pairs of vertices are identical or one pair of vertices is identical, and one pair of vertices is of distance exactly one).
It turns out that all the configurations obtained are D-reducible. Hence, a noncontractible loop does not exist after contraction. 
 
 
 
 8.1.2  Contractible loop when a free completion contains a noncontractible cycle 

 
 Figure 29 :  The dark solid line represents the ring, the red lines represent the contraction edges, and the blue edge becomes a loop after contraction. The left and right figures represent case (i) and (ii), one of the degree patterns in the above lists, respectively. 
 
 
 Here, we handle one particular type of contractible loop 26 26 
                 26 
                
                
                
              This type of contractible loop is different from other contractible loops. because it does not violate the minimality of  C C  in proving Lemma  6.2 . So we have to deal with it separately. .
This loop exists in a similar situation to a noncontractible loop (See Figure  29 ). The distance described above for cases (i) or (ii) is the same.
The difference is how these vertices are connected outside  S S .
 r q 1 r_{q}^{1}  is identical to  r p 1 r_{p}^{1}  and the distance between  r q 2 r_{q}^{2}  and  r p 1 r_{p}^{1}  is 1 (or swapping the roles of ( r q 1 , r p 1 r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{1} ) and ( r q 2 , r p 1 r_{q}^{2},r_{p}^{1} )) in the case (i), or
 r q 1 r_{q}^{1}  is identical to  r p 1 r_{p}^{1}  and  r q 2 r_{q}^{2}  is identical to  r p 2 r_{p}^{2}  in the case (ii).
The contraction size of at most three does not cause this kind of loop in the same discussion as the noncontractible loop case.
We check in the same way as the noncontractible loop case. The annular configurations obtained by identifying one pair of vertices are C-reducible with contraction size at most 3. There are 2 cases not concluded immediately, but we check the situation when such a loop appears. It turns out that they are D-reducible. 
 
 
 
 8.1.3  Other contractible loops 

 
 Finally, there is a case when a normal contractible loop of length  l l  turns into a self-loop after  l − 1 l-1  edges are contracted.
This may happen when a configuration contains a particularly long path consisting of  5 5  or more contraction edges.
However, even in such extreme cases, we have implemented a program and checked that the outside structure does not form a loop by using Fact  8.2 . 
 
 
 
 
 8.2  Avoiding Petersen-like graphs 

 
 We now check whether the contraction of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  would make  G G  turn into a Petersen-like graph.
To start, we use the algorithm introduced in the projective planar case  [ 16 ]  to eliminate vertices that could disappear after  2 / 3 2/3 -cut reductions.
Although we do need to modify some parts to consider annular cuts, the overall algorithm is the same. (For implementation specifics, refer to the Appendix.) 
 
 
 After the above elimination, we want to check whether or not the remaining structure matches any graph in  𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0} . 
 
 
 Hereafter, we use the dual of the remaining graph (hereafter, we use  T T ) since the degree of vertices is easier to handle than the size of faces in the original cubic graph.
For every graph in  𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0} , we take its dual and inspect every vertex  v v .
Since the dual is a triangulation, there should be a walk of size  d d  surrounding  v v , where  d d  is the degree of  v v .
For example, when  v v  is the vertex of degree 6 in the Petersen graph’s dual, the walk surrounding  v v  consists of vertices whose degree is 5, 9, 5, 9, 5, 9, in clockwise order.
The degree list of all possible walks (excluding rotational and reflective symmetry) is given in Table  2 .
Note that the graphs of the Blanuša snark family do not have a unique embedding. Each embedding is determined by the differences between  ( 2 , 2 ) (2,2) -annulus-cuts, and we want all the degree lists for each possible embedding.
The list below was generated by listing all embeddings of the Petersen graph, Blanuša snark, Blanuša-V1 snark, Blanuša-V2 snark, and the Blanuša-H1 snark. It is easy to see that no other degree lists are possible in any of the bigger snarks in either Blanuša family.

Table of neighboring degree patterns of Petersen-like (dual) graphs in the torus.
Each row contains all the possible degree patterns of a certain degree  k k .
