 With this table, we track the degree of each vertex of  K / c ⁡ ( K ) K/c(K)  and check if it matches with one of the above lists’ degree patterns.
When a vertex is of degree exactly  4 4  or  11 11  or more, the resulting graph  G ∸ c ⁡ ( K ) G\dotdiv c(K)  would not be a Petersen-like graph.
If not, we list up the degree of each neighbor vertex and check if it matches with one of the patterns in the corresponding row of the table above.
If it does not, the resulting graph would not be a Petersen-like graph. 
 
 
 We implement the above matching algorithm and run it over every configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  with contraction size  ≥ 3 \geq 3  (the pseudocode is provided in Appendix  C.3 ).
For some configurations, since some contraction edge sets  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  failed to pass the test, we have tested multiple contraction edge sets that admit C-reducibility of  K K , and selected the edge set with a successful outcome of the above algorithm. 
 
 
 As a result, we found that all the configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  with some contraction edge set of size  ≥ 3 \geq 3  pass these tests, except for one configuration (namely C(1), which was handled in Section  6  because the ring size is exactly six.). 
 
 
 
 
 9  The flat case 

 
 In this section, we assume that every vertex  v v  of  G ′ G^{\prime}  satisfies  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0  (final charge is zero).
In Lemma  4.5 , we show when a vertex of final charge positive exists, a reducible configuration weakly appears within its third neighbors. Our approach here is similar: when vertices whose final charge is zero are concentrated, we will find a reducible configuration near these vertices. We prove this by tiling several vertices and their neighbors, whose center vertices have final charge zero, and then by checking whether a reducible configuration weakly appears by computer in Lemma  9.1 ,  9.2  (An example is in Figure  1 ). To ensure that the execution time of this program is practical, we take the following approach. We check the cases in Lemma  9.1  from case 1 to case 9 in this order listed. First, we handle the cases when higher degree vertices of final charge zero are concentrated such as cases 1 and 2. An amount of initial charge of a higher degree vertex is a very small negative value, so when the final charge of such a vertex is zero, many neighbors send a charge to the vertex. That enables us to find many vertices of degree 5 or 6 that constitute a reducible configuration. 
 
 
 Then, we can restrict the structure of a minimal counterexample  G G  and its dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime} . For example, the degree of neighbors of  v , u v,u  in  G ′ G^{\prime}  in case 3 is  5 , 6 , 7 , 5,6,7,  or  8 8  after handling cases 1, and 2. In this way, we prune the case analysis. We check all the cases in Lemma  9.1  and all the cases in Lemma  9.2  by computer.
Note that case  i i  can be a subcase of case  j ⁡ ( j < i ) j(j<i)  (e.g.  i = 8 , j = 6 i=8,j=6 ) since we prune the analysis of case  i ⁡ ( 1 ≤ i ≤ 9 ) i(1\leq i\leq 9)  by using the fact case  j ⁡ ( j < i ) j(j<i)  is already proved. 
 
 
 Lemma 9.1 . 
 
 Suppose that one of the following is contained as a subgraph of  G ′ G^{\prime} : 
 
 1.  
 
 a vertex  v v  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = 10 , 11 d(v)=10,11 , 
 
 2.  
 
 an edge  v ​ u vu  such that  d ( v ) = 9 , d ( u ) = 7 , 8 , d(v)=9,d(u)=7,8,  or  9 9 , 
 
 3.  
 
 an edge  v ​ u vu  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = d ⁡ ( u ) = 8 d(v)=d(u)=8 , 
 
 4.  
 
 a facial triangle  u ​ v ​ w uvw  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = d ⁡ ( u ) = 7 , d ⁡ ( w ) = 8 d(v)=d(u)=7,d(w)=8 , 
 
 5.  
 
 a facial triangle  u ​ v ​ w uvw  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = d ⁡ ( u ) = d ⁡ ( w ) = 7 d(v)=d(u)=d(w)=7 , 
 
 6.  
 
 a vertex  v v  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = 8 d(v)=8 , and at least three neighbors of  v v  are of degree  7 7 , 
 
 7.  
 
 a vertex  v v  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = 7 d(v)=7 , and the clockwise consecutive neighbors  u 1 , u 2 , u 3 u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}  of  v v  satisfy  d ⁡ ( u 1 ) = d ⁡ ( u 3 ) = 8 , d ⁡ ( u 2 ) ≤ 6 d(u_{1})=d(u_{3})=8,d(u_{2})\leq 6 , 
 
 8.  
 
 an edge  v ​ u vu  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = 7 , d ⁡ ( u ) = 8 d(v)=7,d(u)=8 , or 
 
 9.  
 
 a vertex  v v  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = 7 d(v)=7 , and at least three neighbors of  v v  are of degree  7 7 . 
 
 
 Then a configuration
in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears. 
 
 
 
 In addition to Lemma  9.1 , we show Lemma  9.2 . 
 
 
 Lemma 9.2 . 
 
 If an edge  v ​ u vu  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = d ⁡ ( u ) = 7 d(v)=d(u)=7  exists in  G ′ G^{\prime} , a configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears, unless the subgraph that consists of neighbors of  u ​ v uv  is one of the graphs in the Figure  30 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 30 :  The exceptional cases of Lemma  9.2 . Two vertices placed in the center represent  v v  and  u u . 
 
 
 By Lemma  9.1 , every vertex  v v  of degree  8 8  or  9 9  is surrounded by neighbors of degree  5 , 6 5,6 .
The same statement does not hold when degree is  7 7  since a vertex  v v  of degree  7 7  can have a neighbor of degree  7 7 , but this happens to only a few cases by Lemma  9.2 . We can also prove Lemma  9.3 . 
 
 
 Lemma 9.3 . 
 
 If a path of length two such that its vertices are all of degree 7 exists,
a configuration
in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We consider the case that  u ​ v ​ w uvw  is a path that satisfies the assumption of this lemma. By Lemma  9.2 , each of  u ​ v uv  and  v ​ w vw  corresponds to the edge placed in the center of one of the graphs enumerated in Figure  30 . We say that an edge  u ​ v uv  corresponds to edge  i ⁡ ( 1 ≤ i ≤ 5 ) i(1\leq i\leq 5)  when  u ​ v uv  corresponds to the edge in the center of  i i -th graph from the left in Figure  30 .  A diagonal vertex of  e e  is a vertex that constitutes a facial triangle with an edge  e e .
We cannot tile two graphs in Figure  30  such that  u ​ v ​ w uvw  constitutes a path of length two, except when ( u ​ v uv ,  v ​ w vw ) correspond to (edge2, edge2), (edge3, edge3), (edge3, edge4), or (edge4, edge4) respectively, by the difference of degrees of diagonal vertices of  u ​ v uv  or  v ​ w vw . In each case, C(1), or C(26) is contained as a subgraph, which implies the statement of this Lemma.
∎ 
 
 
 
 By combining Lemmas  9.2  and  9.3 , the existence of an edge  u ​ v uv  such that  d ⁡ ( v ) = d ⁡ ( u ) = 7 d(v)=d(u)=7  implies the same conclusion of Lemma  4.5 . Hence, every vertex  v v  of degree  7 7  is surrounded by neighbors of degree  5 , 6 5,6 . It is the same situation as the degree of  8 8  or  9 9 . We can find a reducible configuration in this situation. We check the following lemma by computer. 
 
 
 Lemma 9.4 . 
 
 If all neighbors of a vertex of degree  7 , 8 , 7,8,  or  9 9 , are of degree  5 5  or  6 6 , a configuration
in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears. 
 
 
 
 Therefore, we only have to handle the case if every vertex is of degree  5 5  or  6 6 . A triangulation in the torus has an average degree of exactly  6 6 , implying that all vertices are of degree  6 6 . Hence, the reducible configuration C(27) weakly appears. This is the only case we use C(27). Actually, we do not need C(27) in checking the weak appearance of reducible configurations if a vertex of positive charge exists (Lemma  4.5 ) or vertices of zero charge are concentrated. Hence we have to ensure the graph after contracting the contraction edges of C(27) does not become Petersen-like only when all degrees of  G ′ G^{\prime}  are 6. This check is easy since a vertex whose degree is at least 18, which is a clear evidence of being non-Petersen-like, exists after contraction. 
 
 
 In summary, we have the following. 
 
 
 Lemma 9.5 . 
 
 Assume that every vertex  v v  of  G ′ G^{\prime}  satisfies  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0  (final charge is zero). Then a reducible configuration  K K  in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 
 10  Main proof 

 
 We now discuss Theorem  1.3 . Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample. We know the following properties by Lemmas  2.5 ,  2.7  and  2.8 . 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 G G  is embedded in the torus  Σ \Sigma , 
 
 2.  
 
 G G  is cyclically 5-edge-connected, and if there is an edge-cut  F F  of size exactly five, one of the connected components of  G − F G-F  is a 5-cycle and 
 
 3.  
 
 no cyclic (2,  ≤ \leq  3)-annulus-cut nor cyclic (1,  ≤ \leq  3)-annulus-cut 
 
 
 
 
 If the representativity of  G G  is exactly one, by Lemma  5.7 , either there is a subgraph  K K  of the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime} , that is a reducible configuration in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  and that does not hit the vertex in  G ′ G^{\prime}  that intersects a non-contractible curve of length exactly one in  Σ \Sigma , or there is an annular island described in Lemma  5.7 . In the latter case, reducing this annular island does not result in a Petersen-like graph, because the original graph has representativity one embedding in the torus (and Petersen-like graphs need to have representativity two for any embedding). 
 
 
 Assume that the representativity of  G G  is at least two. Now we apply our discharging rules  ℛ \mathcal{R} . By Lemma  4.5 , if there is a vertex  v v  of the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  with  T ⁡ ( v ) > 0 T(v)>0 , there must exist a subgraph  K ′ K^{\prime}  of  G ′ G^{\prime}  which contains either  v v  or at least one of its neighbors and a reducible configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 Consider the case  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0  for all the vertices  v v  of  V ⁡ ( G ) V(G) .
Then, according to Lemma  9.5 , a reducible configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears in  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 But by Lemma  8.1 , such a subgraph  K K  would not appear in  G ′ G^{\prime} . Indeed, reducing such a graph does not result in any Petersen-like graph.
This completes the proof of Theorem  1.3 .∎ 
 
 
 
 11  Algorithm 

 
 In this section, we consider the algorithmic corollary of our main theorem, Theorem  1.3 , see Theorem  1.5 . Let us mention the problem again. 
 
 
 
 Algorithmic Problem
 Input : A cubic graph  G G .
 Output : Either a three edge-coloring of  G G  or an obstruction that is not three edge-colorable, or else conclude that  G G  cannot be embedded in the torus (and output a forbidden minor for embedding a graph in the torus).
 Time complexity :  O ⁡ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2}) , where  n = | V ⁡ ( G ) | n=|V(G)| . 
 
 
 
 Description 
 
 
 Step 1:  Embedding  G G  in the torus. 
 
 
 This can be done using the algorithm by Mohar  [ 22 ] . If  G G  cannot be embedded in the torus, we just output the obstruction (i.e., a forbidden minor) obtained from the algorithm by Mohar  [ 22 ] . 
 
 
 Hereafter, we may assume that the input graph  G G  is embedded in the torus  Σ \Sigma . 
 
 
 Step 2:  Reduction to a cyclically 5-edge-connected graph. 
 
 
 This step is almost identical to that for the planar case, see  [ 27 ] . So we just give a brief sketch here, and give more details in Appendix (Section  F ). 
 
 
 We try to find a cyclic cut of size four or less.
We find such a cut (if it exists) with the smallest size. If there is such a cut  F F , let the two connected components of  G − F G-F  be  G 1 G_{1}  and  G 2 G_{2} , and we obtain a 3-edge-coloring by applying this algorithm to both  G i G_{i}  recursively.
If we fail to three-edge color one of  G i G_{i} , we obtain an obstruction and output it. 
 
 
 Hereafter we assume that  G G  is a cyclically 5-edge-connected graph. If there is a cut  F F  of size five, where both connected components are not 5-cycles, again,
we obtain a three-edge coloring by applying this algorithm to both  G i G_{i}  recursively (we never fail because we do not reduce the obstructions).
As in Lemmas  2.5 ,  2.7  and  2.8 , we can obtain the coloring of  G G  by merging the edge-colorings of  G 1 G_{1}  and  G 2 G_{2} . 
 
 
 Step 3:  Find a reducible configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} 
 
 
 By Lemmas  4.5  and  8.1 , a reducible configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  weakly appears. We give more details in Appendix (Section  F ). 
 
 
 Now let us estimate the time complexity of this algorithm. Step 1 takes  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time as in  [ 22 ] . Both steps 2 and 3 take an  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time and we recuse. For correctness, we follow the proof in  [ 16 ] , which is given in the appendix for the sake of completeness.

 Appendix C  Appendix: Pseudo codes 

 
 C.1  Pseudocode for checking cuts of size 6 or 7 

 
 We explain a pseudocode that checks Claim  6.9  in Section  6 . Let  K K  be a configuration in  𝒦 \mathcal{K} , and  S S  be a free completion of  K K  with ring  R R . Let us remind  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K)  is a set of edges to be contracted to prove C-reducbility. When checking the combinations of distances in (6cut- k k ) ( 1 ≤ k ≤ 10 1\leq k\leq 10 ), (7cut- l l ) ( 1 ≤ l ≤ 15 1\leq l\leq 15 ), we check the following patterns of distances for each configuration  K K  in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K} .
These precise check patterns for each configuration are rather complicated as shown below. This is because we have to check two patterns when the distance of two vertices of  C C  is 1: two vertices  r , r ′ r,r^{\prime}  of the ring of  K K  satisfy (i)  d S / c ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( r , r ′ ) = 1 d_{S/c(K)}(r,r^{\prime})=1  and both  r , r ′ r,r^{\prime}  belong to  C C  or, (ii)  d S / c ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( r , r ′ ) = 0 d_{S/c(K)}(r,r^{\prime})=0  and  r r  belong to  C C  and  r ′ r^{\prime}  is adjacent to a vertex of  C C . In the patterns below, to simplify notations when the symbols  r , r ′ , r ′′ r,r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}  are used, they unconditionally represent vertices that do not belong to  C C  but belong to the ring  R R . 
 
 •  
 
 (6cut-1) 
 
 –  
 
 d S / c ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( u 0 , u 2 ) = 0 d_{S/c(K)}(u_{0},u_{2})=0

 d S / c ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( u 0 , u 2 ) = 0 d_{S/c(K)}(u_{0},u_{2})=0 ,  d S / c ⁡ ( K ) ​ ( u 3 , u 5 ) = 0 d_{S/c(K)}(u_{3},u_{5})=0

 Algorithm 1    ReducableVerticesByTwoNonContractiblePaths( K K ,  S S ,  R R ,  l l ) 
 
 
               1: 
              
              
            A C-reducible configuration  K K , its free completion  S S  and its ring  R R , the length of  C C  is  l l 

 
 
               2: 
              
              
            Returns the set of vertices that may be deleted due to two non-contractibly connected paths of  R R .

 
 
               3: 
              
              
             d : d:  a function, receives two vertices in  S S  and returns the distance between them

 
 
               4: 
              
              
             d ′ : d^{\prime}:  a function, receives two vertices in  S / c ⁡ ( K ) S/c(K)  and returns the distance between them

 
 
               5: 
              
              
             r ​ R ​ r ′ : rRr^{\prime}:  the path obtained by moving along  R R  in clockwise order from  r ∈ R r\in R  to  r ′ ∈ R r^{\prime}\in R .

 
 
               6: 
              
              
             U ← ∅ U\leftarrow\varnothing 

 
 
               7: 
              
              
             for all   r p 1 , r q 1 , r p 2 , r q 2 ∈ R r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2}\in R  such that  r p 1 , r q 1 , r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2}  are listed in clockwise order of  R R   do 

 
 
               8: 
              
              
               ⊳ \triangleright  the representativity after contraction is at least 2 so the lowerbounds of  c 1 , c 2 c_{1},c_{2}  exist.
 
 
 
               9: 
              
              
               for all   c 1 c_{1}  in  max ⁡ { 0 , 2 − d ′ ​ ( r p 1 , r q 1 ) } , … , 3 − d ′ ​ ( r q 1 , r p 2 ) − d ′ ​ ( r q 2 , r p 1 ) \max\{0,2-d^{\prime}(r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1})\},\ldots,3-d^{\prime}(r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2})-d^{\prime}(r_{q}^{2},r_{p}^{1})   do 

 
 
               10: 
              
              
                for all   c 2 c_{2}  in  max ⁡ { 0 , 2 − d ′ ​ ( r p 2 , r q 2 ) } , … , 3 − c 1 − d ′ ​ ( r q 1 , r p 2 ) − d ′ ​ ( r q 2 , r p 1 ) \max\{0,2-d^{\prime}(r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2})\},\ldots,3-c_{1}-d^{\prime}(r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2})-d^{\prime}(r_{q}^{2},r_{p}^{1})   do 

 
 
               11: 
              
              
                  if   calcLowerBoundLength( K K ,  S S ,  R R ,  l l ,  r p 1 r_{p}^{1} ,  r q 1 r_{q}^{1} ,  r p 2 r_{p}^{2} ,  r q 2 r_{q}^{2} ,  c 1 c_{1} ,  c 2 c_{2} )   > l >l   then 

 
 
               12: 
              
              
                   continue 
 
 
               13: 
              
              
                  end   if 
 
 
               14: 
              
              
                  hasSmallCut   ← \leftarrow   false 
 
 
               15: 
              
              
                  for all   P 1 ← P_{1}\leftarrow  all paths of  r q 1 r_{q}^{1}  and  r p 2 r_{p}^{2}  in  S S   do 

 
 
               16: 
              
              
                   for all   P 2 ← P_{2}\leftarrow  all paths of  r q 2 r_{q}^{2}  and  r p 1 r_{p}^{1}  in  S S   do 

 
 
               17: 
              
              
                     C 1 ← C_{1}\leftarrow  the component of  S − P 1 S-P_{1}  that contains midpoints of  r q 1 ​ R ​ r p 2 r_{q}^{1}Rr_{p}^{2} .

 
 
               18: 
              
              
                     C 2 ← C_{2}\leftarrow  the component of  S − P 2 S-P_{2}  that contains
midpoints of  r q 2 ​ R ​ r p 1 r_{q}^{2}Rr_{p}^{1} .

 
 
               19: 
              
              
                     if   | P 1 | + | P 2 | + c 1 + c 2 = 4 , | C 1 ∪ C 2 | > 0 |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|+c_{1}+c_{2}=4,|C_{1}\cup C_{2}|>0  or  | P 1 | + | P 2 | + c 1 + c 2 = 5 , | C 1 ∪ C 2 | > 1 |P_{1}|+|P_{2}|+c_{1}+c_{2}=5,|C_{1}\cup C_{2}|>1   then 

 
 
               20: 
              
              
                      hasSmallCut   ← \leftarrow   true 
 
 
               21: 
              
              
                      break 
 
 
               22: 
              
              
                     end   if 
 
 
               23: 
              
              
                   end   for 
 
 
               24: 
              
              
                  end   for 
 
 
               25: 
              
              
                  if   hasSmallCut  is  false   then 

 
 
               26: 
              
              
                   for all   P 1 ← P_{1}\leftarrow  all paths where  | P 1 / c ⁡ ( K ) | = d ′ ​ ( r q 1 , r p 2 ) |P_{1}/c(K)|=d^{\prime}(r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2}) , connecting  r q 1 r_{q}^{1}  and  r p 2 r_{p}^{2}  in  S S   do 

 
 
               27: 
              
              
                     for all   P 2 ← P_{2}\leftarrow  all paths where  | P 2 / c ⁡ ( K ) | = d ′ ​ ( r q 2 , r p 1 ) |P_{2}/c(K)|=d^{\prime}(r_{q}^{2},r_{p}^{1}) , connecting  r q 2 r_{q}^{2}  and  r p 1 r_{p}^{1}  in  S S   do 

 
 
               28: 
              
              
                      C 1 ← C_{1}\leftarrow  the component of  S − P 1 S-P_{1}  that contains midpoints of  r q 1 ​ R ​ r p 2 r_{q}^{1}Rr_{p}^{2} .

 
 
               29: 
              
              
                      C 2 ← C_{2}\leftarrow  the component of  S − P 2 S-P_{2}  that contains midpoints of  r q 2 ​ R ​ r p 1 r_{q}^{2}Rr_{p}^{1} .

 
 
               30: 
              
              
                      U ← U ∪ V ⁡ ( C 1 ) ∪ V ⁡ ( C 2 ) U\leftarrow U\cup V(C_{1})\cup V(C_{2}) 

 
 
               31: 
              
              
                     end   for 
 
 
               32: 
              
              
                   end   for 
 
 
               33: 
              
              
                  end   if 
 
 
               34: 
              
              
                end   for 
 
 
               35: 
              
              
               end   for 
 
 
               36: 
              
              
             end   for 
 
 
               37: 
              
              
             return   U U 

 
 
 
 
 Algorithm 2    calcLowerBoundLength( K , S , R , l , r p 1 K,S,R,l,r_{p}^{1} ,  r q 1 r_{q}^{1} ,  r p 2 r_{p}^{2} ,  r q 2 r_{q}^{2} ,  c 1 c_{1} ,  c 2 c_{2} ) 
 
 
               1: 
              
              
            A C-reducible configuration  K K , its free completion  S S  and its ring  R R , the length of  C C  is  l l , vertices of  R R ,  r p 1 , r q 1 , r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2}  which are listed in clockwise order of  R R , the length of non-contractibly connected path of  R R  between  r p 1 , r q 1 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1}  (,  r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2} ) is  c 1 ≤ 3 c_{1}\leq 3  (,  c 2 ≤ 3 c_{2}\leq 3 ) respectively.

 
 
               2: 
              
              
            The lower bound of length of a circuit that bounds the disk inside  K K .

 
 
               3: 
              
              
             if   c 1 = 3 c_{1}=3  or  c 2 = 3 c_{2}=3   then 

 
 
               4: 
              
              
               return   0 0   ⊳ \triangleright  return trivial lower bound.
 
 
 
               5: 
              
              
             end   if 
 
 
               6: 
              
              
             for all   r , r ′ ∈ R r,r^{\prime}\in R   do 

 
 
               7: 
              
              
               L 1 ​ [ r ] ​ [ r ′ ] ← L_{1}[r][r^{\prime}]\leftarrow  the minimum possible length of a contractibly connected  r ​ r ′ rr^{\prime} -path of  R R  that is also a subpath of  C C .  ⊳ \triangleright  calculated by the function forbiddenCycle in  [ 16 ] 
 
 
 
               8: 
              
              
               x ← x\leftarrow  the minimum possible length of a contractibly connected  r ​ r ′ rr^{\prime} -path of  R R  that consists of an edge whose one end is  r r  and a subpath of  C C  (its calculated length is the same when the path consists of an edge whose one end is  r ′ r^{\prime}  and a subpath of  C C ).  ⊳ \triangleright  calculated by the function forbiddenCycleOneEdge in  [ 16 ] 
 
 
 
               9: 
              
              
               L 2 ​ [ r ] ​ [ r ′ ] ← x − 1 L_{2}[r][r^{\prime}]\leftarrow x-1 

 
 
               10: 
              
              
             end   for 
 
 
               11: 
              
              
             ⊳ \triangleright  check when all  r p 1 , r q 1 , r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2}  belong to  C C .
 
 
 
               12: 
              
              
             L ← max ⁡ ( L 1 ​ [ r p 1 ] ​ [ r q 1 ] , 2 − c 1 ) + max ⁡ ( L 1 ​ [ r p 2 ] ​ [ r q 2 ] , 2 − c 2 ) L\leftarrow\max(L_{1}[r_{p}^{1}][r_{q}^{1}],2-c_{1})+\max(L_{1}[r_{p}^{2}][r_{q}^{2}],2-c_{2})   ⊳ \triangleright  the original representativity is at least two.
 
 
 
               13: 
              
              
             L ′ ← L 1 ​ [ r q 1 ] ​ [ r p 2 ] + L 1 ​ [ r q 2 ] ​ [ r p 1 ] L^{\prime}\leftarrow L_{1}[r_{q}^{1}][r_{p}^{2}]+L_{1}[r_{q}^{2}][r_{p}^{1}] 

 
 
               14: 
              
              
             if    L + c 1 + c 2 ≤ 5 L+c_{1}+c_{2}\leq 5  and  L ′ + c 1 + c 2 ≤ 5 L^{\prime}+c_{1}+c_{2}\leq 5    then   ⊳ \triangleright  the number of vertices outside  C C  is at least four
 
 
 
               15: 
              
              
               L ′′ ← L + L ′ + 6 − c 1 − c 2 − max ⁡ ( L , L ′ ) L^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow L+L^{\prime}+6-c_{1}-c_{2}-\max(L,L^{\prime}) 

 
 
               16: 
              
              
             else 
 
 
               17: 
              
              
               L ′′ ← L + L ′ L^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow L+L^{\prime} 

 
 
               18: 
              
              
             end   if 
 
 
               19: 
              
              
            
 
 
               20: 
              
              
             if    c 1 = 2 c_{1}=2    then 

 
 
               21: 
              
              
               ⊳ \triangleright  check when  r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2}  and only one of  r p 1 r_{p}^{1}  or  r q 1 r_{q}^{1}  belong to  C C .
 
 
 
               22: 
              
              
               L a ← max ⁡ ( L 2 ​ [ r p 1 ] ​ [ r q 1 ] , 1 ) + max ⁡ ( L 1 ​ [ r p 2 ] ​ [ r q 2 ] , 2 − c 1 ) L_{a}\leftarrow\max(L_{2}[r_{p}^{1}][r_{q}^{1}],1)+\max(L_{1}[r_{p}^{2}][r_{q}^{2}],2-c_{1})   ⊳ \triangleright  the original representativity is at least two.
 
 
 
               23:

 We have mainly two parts of implementing these checks. One part is checking the minimality of  C C  and the other part is counting the number of vertices as explained in Section  6.2.2 . Note that the representativity of  G , G ′ G,G^{\prime}  are at least two in these algorithms since  G G  does not become Petersen-like otherwise. 
 
 
 The former part is almost identical to the pseudocode explained in Appendix of  [ 16 ] , which are the functions named forbiddenCycle and forbiddenCycleOneEdge. The only difference between that pseudocode and ours is when checking the minimality, we can only use the ”almost” minimality of  C C  by the definition of  C C . To be more precise, we have to check a cycle of length  7 7  that would contradict the minimality contains at least four edges that are not shared with  C C  when the length of  C C  is 6. This is done by checking that cycle contains at least four edges whose at least one endpoint is a vertex of  K K . The other implementations of this part is the same so we omit it here. 
 
 
 The latter part is almost identical to the pseudocode used to check the number of vertices after reductions used in  [ 16 ] .
The difference exists when checking a contractible cycle that consists of two paths inside  S S  and two homotopic noncontractibly connected paths of  R R , as explained in Section  6.2.2 . Here, we check these paths are compatible with  C C , whose length is 6 or 7. We show the pseudocode in Algorithm  1 . 
 
 
 In Algorithm  1 , we check whether the existence of short two noncontractibly connected paths of  R R  contradicts the length of cycle  C C  being 6, 7 or not. To do that, we calculate the lower bound of length of a circuit that bounds the disk inside  K K  in Algorithm  2 .
Let  r p 1 , r q 1 , r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2}  be the vertices of the ring  R R  of a configuration  K ∈ 𝒦 K\in\mathcal{K}  such that  r p 1 , r q 1 , r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2}  are listed in the clockwise order of  R R . The symbol  P 1 , P 2 P_{1},P_{2}  denote homotopic noncontractibly connected paths of the ring  R R  between  ( r p 1 , r q 1 ) (r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1}) ,  ( r p 2 , r q 2 ) (r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2})  respectively.
First, we consider the case where both of the lengths of  P 1 , P 2 P_{1},P_{2}  are at most 1. We use the following claim that says  | P 1 | = 0 , | P 2 | = 1 |P_{1}|=0,|P_{2}|=1  (or  | P 1 | = 1 , | P 2 | = 0 |P_{1}|=1,|P_{2}|=0 ) is possible in the restricted situation. 
 
 
 Claim C.1 . 
 
 We assume the followings: 
 
 •  
 
 u i = u j ​ ( 0 ≤ i < j ≤ l ) u_{i}=u_{j}(0\leq i<j\leq l)  and  u i ​ C ​ u j u_{i}Cu_{j}  (a subpath of  C C  from  u i u_{i}  to  u j u_{j} ) is a noncontractible cycle in  G ′ G^{\prime} , 
 
 •  
 
 a noncontractible connected chord  u m ​ u n ​ ( j < m < n < l ) u_{m}u_{n}(j<m<n<l)  of  C C  that is homotopic to  u i ​ C ​ u j u_{i}Cu_{j}  exists, and 
 
 •  
 
 the representativity of  G ′ G^{\prime}  is at least two. 
 
 
 Then,  l = 7 l=7  and  u i ​ u m ​ u n u_{i}u_{m}u_{n}  is a facial triangle. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We obtained two regions by deleting  D D  and chord  u m ​ u n u_{m}u_{n}  from the torus. One is a disk and the other is an annulus or a disk. By the condition of  C C  ( C C  is balanced), these two regions have at least four vertices, but Lemma  2.5 ,  2.7  and the hypothesis that the representativiy is at least two contradicts it unless  l = 7 l=7  and  u i ​ u m ​ u n u_{i}u_{m}u_{n}  is a facial cycle.
∎ 
 
 
 
 By Claim  C.1 ,
we know no paths in  S S  with  P 1 , P 2 P_{1},P_{2}  erase vertices outside  D D  if  | P 1 | = 0 , | P 2 | = 1 |P_{1}|=0,|P_{2}|=1  (or  | P 1 | = 1 , | P 2 | = 0 |P_{1}|=1,|P_{2}|=0 ). Hence, if both of the length of  P 1 , P 2 P_{1},P_{2}  are at most 1, all  r p 1 , r q 1 , r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2}  belong to  C C  since  C C  bounds the disk where  K K  appears. Each pair of vertices ( r p 1 , r q 1 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1} ), ( r q 1 , r p 2 r_{q}^{1},r_{p}^{2} ), ( r p 2 , r q 2 r_{p}^{2},r_{q}^{2} ), ( r q 2 , r p 1 r_{q}^{2},r_{p}^{1} ) are connected by the subpath of  C C . We calculate the minimum length of such a possible path by using the function forbiddenCycle, which uses the almost minimality of  C C . The sum of these lengths represents the least number of length of a circuit that bounds  K K . We compare the length and the length of  C C , which is 6 or 7. We have one remark here.
The number of vertices outside the disk bounded by  C C  is at least four by the definition of  C C . This fact combined with Lemma  2.5  and  2.7  implies not both two regions obtained by deleting  S S ,  P 1 P_{1}  and  P 2 P_{2}  from the torus are surrounded at most five edges.
We can calculate a tighter lower bound by using it. We added comments when using these remarks in Algorithm  1 ,  2 .
We do a similar check when the length of  P 1 P_{1}  is 2 and the length of  P 2 P_{2}  is at most 1. Unlike the case where the length of  P 1 P_{1}  is at most 1, it is possible that  r p 1 , r q 1 r_{p}^{1},r_{q}^{1}  do not belong to  C C  but the midvertex of  P 1 P_{1}  belongs to  C C . In this case, we calculate the minimum length of the subpath between the midvertex of  P 1 P_{1}  and  r p 2 r_{p}^{2} (or  r q 2 r_{q}^{2} ) by using the function forbiddenCycleOneEdge, which also uses the almost minimality of  C C .
Moreover, when the length of  P 2 P_{2}  is one,  C C  may pass through  r p 2 r_{p}^{2}  (or  r q 2 r_{q}^{2} ) twice. However, if it happens,  C C  does not pass through the midvertex of  P 1 P_{1}  by Claim  C.1 , so we do a similar check in this case. 
 
 
 
 C.2  Pseudocode for enumerating wrappings of configurations 

 
 We provide a pseudocode that enumerates all possible wrappings (defined in Subsection  3.2 ) which is used to check Lemma  7.1 . 
 
 
 The toplevel function is  enumerateAllWrappings  in Algorithm  3 .
This function calls the following  identifyTwoDirectedEdges  function, which returns the general configuration after merging two vertices  a 1 , b 1 a_{1},b_{1}  and the edges  a 1 ​ a 2 , b 1 ​ b 2 a_{1}a_{2},b_{1}b_{2} .
All possible  a 1 , b 1 , a 2 , b 2 a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2}  are tested, and the accumulated graphs are further passed into a recursive call of  enumerateAllWrappings .
Finally, all graphs which were generated in this process are returned. 
 
 
 The  identifyTwoDirectedEdges  function in Algorithm  4  uses a Disjoint Set Union (DSU) which contains the methods Unite and Same.
This data structure is used to track whether there is a parallel path  P 1 , P 2 P_{1},P_{2}  starting from  a 1 ​ b 1 , a 2 ​ b 2 a_{1}b_{1},a_{2}b_{2}  respectively, and ending in some pair of vertices  u 1 , u 2 u_{1},u_{2} , respectively.
In this case, DSU.Unite( u 1 , u 2 u_{1},u_{2} ) is called, and after this DSU.Same( u 1 , u 2 u_{1},u_{2} ) will return  True .

 C.3  Pseudocode for proving the avoidance of Petersen-like graphs after contraction 

 
 We provide a pseudocode explained in  8.2 ; a code which tracks the degree of each vertex in  K K  after contraction, and checks if it does not match any structure of graphs in  𝒯 0 \mathcal{T}_{0} .
The function  matchNeighboringVertices  in Algorithm  5  is the toplevel function. 
 
 
 The first part of the algorithm is identical to the projective planar case  [ 16 ]  (the function ReducableVertices and its dependencies in Appendix C.5).
What this function did was to check for paths outside  K K  which may form a contractible cut, and see if it becomes a 2,3-cut reduction after contraction of  c ⁡ ( K ) c(K) .
Upon checking paths, we prune cases that violate Fact  8.2 .
This fact held in the projective planar case too, so the code implementation will be exactly the same.
Therefore, readers should refer to  [ 16 ] . 
 
 
 After this, we use Table  2  to perform a degree matching of neighboring vertices.

               12:

               15:

               19:

 Appendix D  Small edge-cuts in minimal counterexamples 

 
 We provide a proof of the following lemma: 
 
 
 Lemma D.1 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a minimal counterexample and  F F  be a cyclical edge cut.
Then, one of the following holds. 
 
 •  
 
 | F | ≥ 6 |F|\geq 6 . 
 
 •  
 
 | F | = 5 |F|=5 , and one of the following two statements holds. 
 
 –  
 
 One of the connected components of  G − F G-F  is a 5-cycle. 
 
 –  
 
 F F  is an  annular cut  (a cut whose dual is two disjoint non-contractible cycles that are homotopic), and the size of the two dual cycles are 1 and 4, respectively. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 This lemma asserts the  internal 6-connectivity  (defined in  [ 4 ] ,  [ 26 ] ) of the dual triangulation  G ′ G^{\prime} , except when  F F  admits a representativity  1 1  structure. 
 
 
 When  F F  ( | F | ≤ 5 |F|\leq 5 ) is a  disk cut  (a cut whose dual is a contractible cycle), there is proof in  [ 16 ] .
So, the problem remains when  F F  is an annular cut.
When  | F | = 2 , 3 |F|=2,3 , it is easy to see that a 2/3-edge reduction suffices. 
 
 
 When  | F | = 4 |F|=4 , we attempt to use the proof for  [ 16 ] .
We consider the case when  F F  consists of two pairs of edges, each consisting of a dual cycle of size two.
For the proof to work, we need to show that for one of the graphs  G 1 , G 2 G_{1},G_{2}  (two graphs separated by  F F ), the six possible insertions of parallel edges / vertex pair insertion are all toroidal and non-Petersen-like.
By taking the contraposition, it is equivalent to saying that if  G 1 , G 2 G_{1},G_{2}  are both subgraphs of a Petersen-like graph with either one parallel edge or vertex pair deleted, the merging of  G 1 , G 2 G_{1},G_{2}  results in a three-edge colorable graph or a Petersen-like graph.
This merging process is actually equivalent to the  dot product  of two snarks defined in  [ 8 ] , and it is known that the dot product of Blanuša snark families can be written like the following: 
 
 
 

 
 
 B ​ H i ⋅ B ​ H j \displaystyle BH_{i}\cdot BH_{j} 
 = B ​ H i + j − 2 \displaystyle=BH_{i+j-2} 
 
 
 
 B ​ H i ⋅ B ​ V j \displaystyle BH_{i}\cdot BV_{j} 
 = B ​ V i + j − 2 \displaystyle=BV_{i+j-2} 
 
 
 
 
 Here,  B ​ H i BH_{i}  is the Blanuša-H i i  snark and  B ​ V i BV_{i}  is the Blanuša-V i i  snark.
The two equations above also hold when denoting the Blanuša snark as  B ​ H 0 BH_{0}  or  B ​ V 0 BV_{0}  and the Petersen graph as  B ​ H − 1 BH_{-1} .
Therefore, every Petersen-like graph (including the Petersen graph and Blanuša snark) merged in this manner also results in a Petersen-like graph.
Therefore, the condition holds, and the rest of the proof can be completed by  [ 16 ] . 
 
 
 When  | F | = 5 |F|=5  and the size of the two dual cycles are 2 and 3, respectively, we can see from Figure  35  that any 5-cycle can be embedded onto  S A S_{A} . 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 35 :  The two 5-cycles between a representativity two and representativity three cut. All possible 5-cycles are isomorphic to one of these 5-cycles. 
 
 
 Just like in the case of  | F | = 4 |F|=4 , we need to show that the ten possible insertions of 5-cycles are all toroidal and non-Petersen-like, at least for one side of the graph.
These are all the cases, and the lemma holds. 
 
 
 
 Appendix E  The remaining unsafe configurations 

 
 E.1  Dealing with C(1) 

 
 Our goal in this section is to prove Lemma  6.1 . The symbol  S S  denotes the free completion of C(1) with the ring and,  v i ​ ( 0 ≤ i ≤ 9 ) v_{i}(0\leq i\leq 9)  represents a set of vertices in  S S  as shown in Figure  22 .
The two sets of contraction edges for C-reducibility of C(1) are  C 1 = { v 0 ​ v 6 , v 6 ​ v 7 , v 7 ​ v 2 , v 5 ​ v 9 , v 9 ​ v 8 , v 8 ​ v 3 } C_{1}=\{v_{0}v_{6},v_{6}v_{7},v_{7}v_{2},v_{5}v_{9},v_{9}v_{8},v_{8}v_{3}\}  or  C 2 = { v 1 ​ v 6 , v 6 ​ v 9 , v 9 ​ v 5 , v 2 ​ v 7 , v 7 ​ v 8 , v 8 ​ v 4 } C_{2}=\{v_{1}v_{6},v_{6}v_{9},v_{9}v_{5},v_{2}v_{7},v_{7}v_{8},v_{8}v_{4}\} . 
 
 
 We show that  G ′ G^{\prime}  does not become the dual of any Petersen-like graphs when C(1) appears. We borrow some terms from Section  6 . A  noncontractibly-connected path  of the ring of C(1) is a path  P P  outside  S S  such that  P + S P+S  contains a noncontractible cycle. 
 
 
 Figure 36 :  Three reductions occurred by contracting  C 1 C_{1}  in C(1) 
 
 
 Claim E.1 . 
 
 If a 2,3-cycle-reduction occurs by contracting  C 1 C_{1}  and  G ′ G^{\prime}  might become Petersen-like, then it is one of the three cases shown in Figure  36 . Moreover, the number of faces deleted by each reduction is 2, 4, or 2 in order of Figure  36  respectively. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We assume the representativity of  G G  is at least 2 since otherwise,  G G  does not become Petersen-like after contractions. 
 
 
 Suppose a 2,3-cycle-reduction occurs by two noncontractibly-connected paths of the ring of C(1). This reduction occurs by two paths between each pair of vertices in the ring  ( v i , v l ) , ( v j , v k ) (v_{i},v_{l}),(v_{j},v_{k})  such that  v i , v j , v k , v l v_{i},v_{j},v_{k},v_{l}  are listed in clockwise order of  R R  and  d S / C 1 ​ ( v i , v j ) + d S / C 1 ​ ( v k , v l ) d_{S/C_{1}}(v_{i},v_{j})+d_{S/C_{1}}(v_{k},v_{l})  is at most 3 (e.g.  i = 0 , j = 2 , k = 3 , l = 4 i=0,j=2,k=3,l=4 ). The sum of lengths of two paths is at most  3 − d S / C 1 ​ ( v i , v j ) − d S / C 1 ​ ( v k , v l ) 3-d_{S/C_{1}}(v_{i},v_{j})-d_{S/C_{1}}(v_{k},v_{l}) . Unless the representativity of  G G  is at most 1, These two paths with  S S  constitute a (2,  ≤ \leq  3)-annulus-cut in its dual, so a small number of vertices remain after contracting  C 1 C_{1}  by considering Lemma  2.7 .
This number is lower than  5 5 , which is the number of vertices of the dual of the Petersen graph. Hence,  G ′ G^{\prime}  does not become Petersen-like. 
 
 
 If a 2,3-cycle-reduction occurs by one contractibly connected path of the ring, it contradicts Lemma  2.5  except in three cases in Figure  36 . 
 
 
 When no 2,3-cycle-reductions occur, the number of faces deleted by contractions is 12, which is the number of faces in C(1). We see more 2, 4, and 2 faces are deleted by each of the three reductions respectively.
∎

 Appendix F  Appendix to Algorithm for Theorem  1.5   

 
 Step 3 can be divided into the following steps. 
 
 
 Step 3.1:  When representativity of  G G  in  Σ \Sigma  is exactly one. 
 
 
 In this case by Lemma  5.3 , there is a subgraph  K K  of the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime} , that is a reducible configuration in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  and that does not hit the vertex that intersects a non-contractible curve of length exactly one in  Σ \Sigma . 
 
 
 Algorithmically speaking, this part is just to find a vertex  v v  of the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  with  T ⁡ ( v ) > 0 T(v)>0 , after cutting  G ′ G^{\prime}  into the plane. 
 
 
 Step 3.2:  When representativity of  G G  in  Σ \Sigma  is at least two. 
 
 
 Now we apply our discharging rules  ℛ \mathcal{R} . By Lemma  4.5 , if there is a vertex  v v  of the dual graph  G ′ G^{\prime}  with  T ⁡ ( v ) > 0 T(v)>0 , there must exist a subgraph  K ′ K^{\prime}  of  G ′ G^{\prime}  which contains either  v v  or at least one of its neighbors and is isomorphic to one of the reducible configurations of  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  (but  K ′ K^{\prime}  may not be an induced subgraph of  G ′ G^{\prime} ). 
 
 
 Consider the case  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0  for all the vertices  v v  of  V ⁡ ( G ) V(G) .
Then, according to Lemma  8.1 , there is a reducible configuration  K K  in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  in  G ′ G^{\prime} . 
 
 
 By Lemma  8.1 , such a subgraph  K K  would not appear in  G ′ G^{\prime} .
This completes the proof of Theorem  1.3 . 
 
 
 Now, let us estimate the time complexity of this algorithm. Step 1 takes  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time as in  [ 22 ] . All of Steps 2 and 3 take an  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time, and we recuse. More precisely, for the correctness and the time complexity: 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 We can find the shortest non-contractible curve in  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time by  [ 7 ] . 
 
 •  
 
 By Lemma  8.1 , all reducible configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  are safely reduced. 
 
 •  
 
 The discharging process takes  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time because we have at most 201 rules, each of which can apply just once to each edge. This allows us to find the set of all vertices  V ′ V^{\prime}  that end up with a positive charge (i.e.,  T ⁡ ( v ) > 0 T(v)>0 ) in  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time. 
 
 •  
 
 For the reducibility process, we only need to look at a vertex  v ∈ V ′ v\in V^{\prime} , the first and second neighbors, and the third neighbors of  v v . But we do not have to look at all the vertices of degree at least 13, as none of our reducible configurations in  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  contains a vertex of degree 13 or more. Let  Q v Q_{v}  be the graph induced by these neighbors, together with  v v , after deleting the vertices of degree at least 13. 
 
 
 Each reducible configuration is of order at most 20, and by our choice,  Q v Q_{v}  has at most 10000 vertices. By Lemma  4.5 , we know that  Q v Q_{v}  contains a reducible configuration  K v K_{v} . Thus we can find a reducible configuration  K v K_{v}  in  O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1)  time. 
 
 
 Consider the case  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0  for all the vertices  v v  of  V ⁡ ( G ) V(G) .
Then, by Lemma  8.1 , there is a reducible configuration  K K  in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  in  G ′ G^{\prime} . This can also be found in  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time, as below. 
 
 •  
 
 We may apply Lemma  6.2  if we find a separating cycle of length six or seven in  G ′ G^{\prime} . This case needs a bit of care. 
 
 
 We can actually enumerate all 5,6,7-cycles in  G ′ G^{\prime}  in  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time by using  [ 9 ,  12 ] . To this end, we first refer to Theorem 3.1 in  [ 9 ] . The important idea is that we run a
bread-first search from some root vertex  r r  in  G ′ G^{\prime} , and assign the label to each vertex  v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ′ ) v\in V(G^{\prime})  to be the distance between  r r  and  v v 
modulo eight. The union of any seven label sets is the disjoint
union of subgraphs, each consisting of at most seven breadth-first layers. By  [ 12 ]  each
of these subgraphs, and therefore the union, has treewidth
at most 100. 
 
 
 Thus by the standard dynamic programming on bounded treewidth graphs, we can enumerate all cycles of length 5, 6, and 7 in  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time for each of the seven label sets. It is clear that every cycle of length at most seven must be contained in some of the seven label sets, and in addition, there are eight such bounded treewidth graphs, thus we can enumerate all cycles of length 5, 6, and 7 in  G ′ G^{\prime}  in  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time.
We can also prove that the number of such cycles is  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  by showing that there are at most three such cycles that can cross by Lemma  2.5 , see also Figures  23  and  24  (and the proofs associated with these figures). 
 
 
 Among these cycles, we can take a minimal one, i.e. a cycle that satisfies Lemma  6.2 , and subject to that, the number of vertices strictly inside the disk bounded by the cycle is as small as possible. By Lemma  6.2 , we know that at least one of the configurations appears strictly inside the disk, which allows us to make a reduction. 
 
 
 This dynamic programming also allows us to find a reducible configuration when  T ⁡ ( v ) = 0 T(v)=0  for all the vertices  v v  of  V ⁡ ( G ) V(G) .
Then, by Lemma  8.1  there is a reducible configuration  K K  in the set  𝒦 \mathcal{K}  in  G ′ G^{\prime} . This can be also found in  O ⁡ ( n ) O(n)  time 
 
 
 
 
 Thus, the total time complexity is  O ⁡ ( n 2 ) O(n^{2})  as claimed. 
 
 
 
 Appendix G  Strengthening Tutte’s 4-Flow Conjecture for toroidal graphs 

 
 An important consequence of this paper is that the Tutte 4-Flow Conjecture can be strengthened for toroidal graphs. This is a direct consequence of our main result, Theorem  1.3 , where we consider cubic graphs. In this section, we prove that a minimum counterexample would need to be cubic,
thus obtaining the following consequence of Theorem  1.3 . 
 
 
 Theorem G.1 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a 2-connected cubic graph that can be embedded in the torus. Then  G G  admits a nowhere-zero 4-flow if and only if it is not Petersen-like. 
 
 
 
 Let us extend the notion of a  minimum counterexample   G G  to be a 2-edge-connected toroidal graph  G G  with minimum  | V ⁡ ( G ) | + | E ⁡ ( G ) | |V(G)|+|E(G)|  that is not Petersen-like and does not admit a nowhere-zero 4-flow.
The proof is almost identical to that given in  [ 16 ] , and the first half of the proof follows the standard strategy used, for example, in  [ 30 ] . We will make use of the following observation. 
 
 
 Lemma G.2 . 
 
 Petersen-like graphs on the torus are not apex graphs, i.e. every vertex-deleted subgraph is nonplanar. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 It is well-known that the Petersen graph is not an apex graph. It can be proved by induction that the repeated dot product of Petersen graphs contains a subdivision of the Petersen graph, so it cannot be apex either. The same is true if we replace vertices in such dot product with 2-connected graphs. We leave the details to the reader.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma G.3 . 
 
 A minimum counterexample is a 3-connected cubic graph. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Suppose that  G G  is a minimum counterexample that is not cubic. If  G G  is not 2-connected, then  G = G 1 ∪ G 2 G=G_{1}\cup G_{2}  where  G 1 ∩ G 2 G_{1}\cap G_{2}  is a cutvertex. Since  G G  has no cutedges, both  G 1 G_{1}  and  G 2 G_{2}  are 2-edge-connected. If they both admit 4-flows, so does  G G . So, one of them must be Petersen-like (as otherwise we would contradict that  G G  is a minimum counterexample). However, in that case, the other one must be planar, and it is easy to see that  G G  itself would be Petersen-like. 
 
 
 Similarly, if  G G  has a 2-vertex-separator  { x , y } \{x,y\} . In that case,  G G  would be a 2-sum of two graphs  G 1 G_{1}  and  G 2 G_{2} , both containing the edge  e = x ​ y e=xy . Let  ϕ i \phi_{i}  be a nowhere-zero 4-flow in  G i G_{i}  for  i = 1 , 2 i=1,2 . If  e ∉ E ⁡ ( G ) e\notin E(G) , then we change  ϕ 2 \phi_{2}  so that  ϕ 2 ​ ( x ​ y ) = − ϕ 1 ​ ( x ​ y ) \phi_{2}(xy)=-\phi_{1}(xy) . Then it is clear that  ϕ 1 + ϕ 2 \phi_{1}+\phi_{2}  gives rise to a nowhere-zero flow in  G G . On the other hand, if  e ∈ E ⁡ ( G ) e\in E(G) , then we change  ϕ 2 \phi_{2}  so that  ϕ 2 ​ ( x ​ y ) ≠ − ϕ 1 ​ ( x ​ y ) \phi_{2}(xy)\neq-\phi_{1}(xy) . Then, again,  ϕ 1 + ϕ 2 \phi_{1}+\phi_{2}  gives rise to a nowhere-zero-flow in  G G . 
 
 
 This leaves us with the possibility that  G 1 G_{1}  (say) is Petersen-like. In that case,  G 2 G_{2}  must be planar. Since  G G  itself is not Petersen-like, the edge  x ​ y ∈ E ⁡ ( G 1 ) xy\in E(G_{1})  is one of the edges confirming that  G 1 G_{1}  is Petersen-like. In that case, we let  ϕ 1 \phi_{1}  be a nowhere-zero 4-flow of  G 1 − x ​ y G_{1}-xy  (which is 2-edge-connected since otherwise it can be shown that  G G  would be Petersen-like). Now, taking a nowhere-zero 4-flow  ϕ 2 \phi_{2}  of  G 2 G_{2}  (or of  G 2 − x ​ y G_{2}-xy  if  x ​ y ∉ E ⁡ ( G ) xy\notin E(G) ), the sum  ϕ 1 + ϕ 2 \phi_{1}+\phi_{2}  is a nowhere-zero 4-flow of  G G , a contradiction. 
 
 
 This confirms that  G G  is 3-connected. Next, we claim that  G G  is cyclically 4-edge-connected. For a contradiction, suppose that  F = { x 1 ​ x 2 , y 1 ​ y 2 , z 1 ​ z 2 } F=\{x_{1}x_{2},y_{1}y_{2},z_{1}z_{2}\}  is a cyclic 3-edge-cut. Let  C 1 , C 2 C_{1},C_{2}  be the two components of  G − F G-F , where  x i , y i , z i x_{i},y_{i},z_{i}  are in  C i C_{i}  ( i = 1 , 2 i=1,2 ). By adding a vertex  u i u_{i}  joined to  x i , y i , z i x_{i},y_{i},z_{i}  in  C i C_{i} , we obtain a 3-connected graph  H i H_{i}  for  i = 1 , 2 i=1,2 . If  H 1 H_{1}  has a nowhere-zero 4-flow  ϕ 1 \phi_{1}  and  H 2 H_{2}  has a nowhere-zero 4-flow  ϕ 2 \phi_{2}  (with values in the group  ℤ 2 × ℤ \mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z} ), note first that the values on the cut  { x i ​ u i , y i ​ u i , z i ​ u i } \{x_{i}u_{i},y_{i}u_{i},z_{i}u_{i}\}  in  H i H_{i}  are all different. Therefore, we can permute the flow values in  ϕ 2 \phi_{2}  such that  ϕ 1 ​ ( x 1 ​ u 1 ) = ϕ 2 ​ ( x 2 ​ u 2 ) \phi_{1}(x_{1}u_{1})=\phi_{2}(x_{2}u_{2}) ,  ϕ 1 ​ ( y 1 ​ u 1 ) = ϕ 2 ​ ( y 2 ​ u 2 ) \phi_{1}(y_{1}u_{1})=\phi_{2}(y_{2}u_{2}) , and  ϕ 1 ​ ( z 1 ​ u 1 ) = ϕ 2 ​ ( z 2 ​ u 2 ) \phi_{1}(z_{1}u_{1})=\phi_{2}(z_{2}u_{2}) . Then, the two flows can be combined to obtain a flow in  G G . This proves that at least one of the two graphs say  H 1 H_{1} , has no 4-flows. Using induction,  H 1 H_{1}  is Petersen-like. Now, we claim that  H 2 H_{2}  must be planar. Since  H 1 H_{1}  is not apex by the lemma, the embedding of  C 1 = H 1 − u 1 C_{1}=H_{1}-u_{1}  is a 2-cell embedding with all three edges going to  u 1 u_{1}  emanating into the same face. That face contains  C 2 C_{2} ; thus,  C 2 C_{2}  plus the vertex  u 2 u_{2}  is planar. This means that  G G  itself is also Petersen-like. But it has a nontrivial 3-edge-cut, so it is not minimal.
This contradiction proves that there are no cyclic 3-edge-cuts in  G G . 
 
 
 The next claim is that  G G  has no edge  e e  for which  G − e G-e  would be planar. Assuming that is not the case, we can embed  G G  in the torus such that there is a 1-noose 27 27 
                 27 
                
                
                
              A  1 1 -noose  is a non contractible closed curve on the torus that intersects the graph in one point only (either on an edge or a vertex).  that intersects  G G  only at the middle of  e e . In the proof, whose details are below, we will perform the splitting of vertices. By performing such a splitting, we get a contradiction unless the graph obtained after the splitting would be Petersen-like. However, this outcome is not possible if we have the 1-noose for the embedding of  G G  since Petersen-like graphs are non-apex. Finally, we claim that  G G  has no 1-nooses at all. If there is one, it cuts through a vertex of  G G , splitting it into two vertices, both of degree more than 1. This gives a 2-edge-connected planar graph, whose 4-flow is also a 4-flow in  G G . This shows that  G G  has representativity at least 2. 
 
 
 Finally, if  G G  has a vertex  v v  of degree greater than 3, consider splitting off a pair of consecutive edges  v ​ x , v ​ y vx,vy  in a clockwise rotation around  v v . The resulting graph  H H  is 2-connected, since  G G  is 3-connected. A flow in  H H  gives a flow in  G G . As  H H  is smaller (it has fewer edges than  G G ), it must be Petersen-like. Let  v ′ v^{\prime}  be the vertex of degree 2 with neighbors  x , y x,y  obtained from  v v  after splitting. Since the edges  v ​ x , v ​ y vx,vy  are consecutive around  v v , the vertices  v v  and  v ′ v^{\prime}  are on the same face in the toroidal embedding of  H H , and since  G G  is not Petersen-like, they belong to different graphs when building  H H  from one or more copies of the Petersen graph. Suppose that the consecutive neighbors around  v v  are  x ′ , x , y , y ′ , … x^{\prime},x,y,y^{\prime},\dots . Now, it is easy to see that we could have split off the edges  v ​ x ′ , v ​ x vx^{\prime},vx  or the edges  v ​ y , v ​ y ′ vy,vy^{\prime} , and then  G 1 G_{1}  would not be Petersen-like, thus yielding a contradiction. 
 
 
 The above proof shows that the minimum counterexample is cubic. This completes the proof.
∎
